# Chapter Summaries: The Remainders Of The Entries For Chapters 181 To 250

*Moved verbatim out of `state/chapter-summaries.md` on the review repair of Volume 06 Batch 0004, so that the rolling file holds a bounded working set. Nothing was deleted and nothing was summarised away. Each remainder is filed under the chapter number it belongs to; the full entry is the heading and the paragraph in the rolling file followed by the remainder here.*

## Chapter 181 — remainder of the entry

**It is four lines and it is the first letter that clerk has ever written to anybody**, and it reports the reading of ten entries out of a bound schedule on the second day of the twelfth month of next year. **The finding is that a hearing is not a document.** Nine entries have a holder and a day; the tenth has a holder and no day, and the holder is the office in its own name as its own holder. Four people heard it: a man of fifty, a mason, who was asked beforehand how long he would be standing there and who said yes in five words and who is not thanked; a clerk of thirty-eight who is a party as of the day of the grant; and two parties who can both say in about four years what they heard and who cannot be cross-examined about it, because a party is served and not examined. **Nothing was written down. Ivet Sarn refused to write it down and gave the reason: there is no column in that book for the person who emptied a line, and a document about an office with no person in it is a document about the four of them.** She wrote the day in her own book with the tenth term in it and no name in it. Marn Ottery's own beat: **a letter is a thing a person can be shown, and a letter is also a thing a person can be shown to somebody else, and the difference between those two is a person, and the person is the one who wrote it.** She puts the letter in the counter's pigeonhole, takes it out again, and puts it in a book with no office on it, because a letter in a pigeonhole is a letter a clerk will answer and a letter in a book with no office on it is a thing nobody can be made to produce. She writes four lines in that book, with the day on it, and reads them back twice, the second reading slower, with nobody in the bay to hear either reading. **On the seventh day, four days later, a seat's office asks the fourth counter to certify one fact: that a copy of a record of property and debt for the Kest house was issued from that counter to a person, on a day, in the ordinary way, at the price at which copies are issued.** It is about the counter's own book, there is a form for it, and it is the one kind of question a table can be made to answer without anybody being hurt. **She says the price out loud first: a certification is a document, it is findable, it goes in about four years into a room with her hand at the foot of it, and it is the second thing in this matter with her hand on it and the first one she chose.** There is no charge for it, because the fee board carries four things and none of them is time and there is no line on it for a certification. **She writes it in about nine seconds** and signs it and dates it, and the seal is not read out and the woman's name is not asked for and is not kept at that counter, because a counter does not take the name of a person for a copy. The man with the wrapper says it will go a long way; she says it will go about four years and it will go to a room, and in that room a person will be able to say that a woman at a counter wrote down a fact about her own book because a table can be made to answer about its own book and not about anything else. Nobody thanks her. She said in advance that nobody was to.

## Chapter 182 — remainder of the entry

**The girl of about twenty-five at the intake desk opens it, because a counter opens wrappers, and she is not a party, and the reason she is the only person in the room who is not a party is that she was not told what was in the wrapper before she opened it, and a person who is not told cannot be made a party to what comes out of it.** She carries it four bays and puts it down in front of a party. The paper is good and not printed, the device at the head is **a seat's own and not the fire-and-water mark**, the foot is a seal, and **there is nothing under the seal.** Tamsin Rook goes and gets the other notice out of a press in her own bays — the one that came up that stair on the tenth of the fourth month of last year, with a seal and a name at the foot, treated by the file as *a seal with a person written under it and nothing else* — and puts the two on a table. **She points at the name at the foot of the earlier one and does not say it out loud**, and gives her reason: a document arrived about four hours ago proving that the absence of a name is a thing a seat can use, and there are now two notices of a seat in the world and one of them has a person's name under it, and she is not going to say that name in a room about the other one. **Three differences.** One: the second line. The old one says no inference may be drawn from *the fact that a person has not been named*; the new one says no inference may be drawn from *the absence of any name*, and the four words are the whole of what the document is. Two: the sixth line, which now declines to name the holder of the office of the seat, asks that the office be treated as the seat in all things touching the matter, says that no person is to be cited for the office and no office for a person, and states that nothing in it is a claim and nothing in it is a complaint. Three: **the third line is new, and it is the whole of the document** — where a recorded claim is entered and marked disputed and the family of the claimant holds no enforceable asset, the person of that family who is of age and resident is the family's representative, is to be entered as such in the office of the seat, and **no further consent of that person is required.** A schedule comes with it in a second hand, unsigned, with four definitions and a provision: *of age and resident*, which turns on a roll of households and says in its own words that that roll does not list the rights, holdings or claims of any person in it; *family*; *a sum payable to a person of that family*, which includes a sum nobody has claimed and one that cannot be enforced; *no further consent*; **and a provision that where a sum payable to a person of that family is entered as held, the wages of a person of that family are held at the same rate and for the same period, and a person whose wages are so held is not to be paid that held sum by any person, on account of the person of that family.** She gives four facts and no advice, because her third line forbids her advice and because she does not know. **Fact one: nothing in the notice is served on anybody**, and a document with no address is not a late document, it is a document that is nowhere. **Fact two: the second line is doing the work of a name and not the work of a protection**; the old second line was a shield and this one is a hood, fitted by the same hand. **Fact three: the third line does not require anybody's agreement, and the schedule says so in terms, and the notice requires consent to be unnecessary, which is not the same as not mentioning consent.** **Fact four: the definition turns on a roll of households, and a district keeps one because a district has to know who is in it, and the schedule says in its own words that it does not list rights, and that sentence is the most honest one in the whole of it.** She says the price of a party out loud for the fifth time, and this time it is about somebody who is not in the room: **the sentence works in both directions, and the second half is the half nobody prices** — a person who cannot be served cannot be told and cannot be warned, and warning a person is serving her. The girl of about twenty-five asks whether she should not have opened the wrapper; she is told she should have opened it, because she was going to anyway, and the only difference is that now it can be said out loud in a room that she did. **And then the chapter's other finding: a person who reads things out loud to people who cannot read them is a person who can be told to stop, and she has said that sentence about a girl of seventeen four hundred and thirty miles down this river and nobody has followed it, and it is not only her** — a clerk can be told to stop copying, a copyist can be told to stop reading, a man with a coat full of paper can be told to stop bringing it up a road. The only person in that room who cannot be told to stop by anybody is a man of thirty-eight who has no office and no fund and no register and no certification and a bar discharged on the twelfth of the ninth month of last year, and **nobody can instruct him and nobody can pay him and that is why he is the most dangerous person in this matter and it is also why nobody in that room can protect him.** The notice went into a press in her own bays with the day on the outside of it, and the schedule went in beside it unsigned, and neither can be produced in a room by anybody. And about four hundred and thirty miles down this river there is a furnace town with a dyers' lane of eleven houses, and the district that lane sits in keeps a roll of households, and the roll does not list rights, and there is a name in that roll at a house of one room over a shop with a woman of twenty against it, and she has never been told that she is in anything.

## Chapter 183 — remainder of the entry

**She reads the whole of it, and it takes her about four minutes to read and about nine to have finished thinking about it, and the second nine minutes are longer than the four.** She reads the words as they are on the sheet and does not improve them, because he will have to say them to somebody and the somebody will look them up. The form, read out in the yard, holds the district's works. **The finding, in about nine words: a form with a heading over it and no person under it can be executed against a person who has not been told it exists.** A notice of a seat that names a person is served on that person, and being served means being told, and being told means a person can refuse; **the only instrument in this empire for refusing a thing is a refusal to be served, there is no second one, and there is no box for a why anywhere in the form of it; and being entered as a representative is being served, because entering a person is a service on that person and it puts them in a matter and makes them findable.** And the schedule's fourth definition says in its own words that nothing done under the notice requires the agreement of the person it is done to, and that is not a gap in it, it is the definition. **A roll of households is a book of addresses that behaves like a book of people.** A district has to know who is in it, to send a notice to, to send a levy to, to count for the water, and to take off the roll when they go. **A person with nothing is in it, and a person with nothing cannot be taken off it.** And then the sentence that is the chapter: **a family that holds nothing enforceable is an eligible family and not a poor one**, and the whole of what a blanking buys a family is that nothing can be given to them, **and the whole of what it costs is a roll — there is a roll of households in the district of a furnace town about four hundred and thirty miles down this river, and that district has a lane in it with eleven houses in it, and there is a name in that roll at a house of one room over a shop, and behind that name there is nothing at all, and nobody put her there on purpose because a district keeps a roll of households because a district has to know who is in it.** **A family that holds nothing enforceable is in the roll. A man with nothing enforceable is not in the roll of anything, and that is the protection working, and it is not a coincidence, and it is the same nine lines.** Then the second thing, which is about the man: **a household is held, not a person, and the person who fills the box fills in the name of a thing, and if it says a household then it holds a household, and if the household is one then it holds one woman with nobody in the room to say no to it**, and she found out at about the fourth hour from a man who cannot read. The corridor: about four hundred yards, stone, the coldest part of the building; the water came in in the fourth month and stood about four inches off the paving for nine days and is still about four inches off it, and nobody has washed it off, and about a hundred and forty files went onto a shelf in a press behind her in her own hand and she has never read them as a set. **A person who is at a window is a person asking something, and a person walking through a corridor is a person going somewhere, and there is no form that makes a person going somewhere a party to whatever the room at the end of it is about; that is why she gives facts in this corridor.** A man of about fifty with a barrow of mortar is **told one sentence of it without being asked whether he wants it** — *a wage can be held against a household and there is no column anywhere for the person whose wage it is* — and says *my wage is my wage* and goes on up the corridor and is not thanked, and it is not known whether he heard the half of it that was under the cloth. She says the price of a party out loud for the fifth time and **wants the order of the two facts noticed: a person who has already accepted a price is the only person in a building who can tell anybody what the price was, and a person who has not paid it will tell you it is nothing.** She writes the whole of the schedule out in her own hard-backed book, in ink, with the day on it, **because a description on a schedule is not findable, and she is the one who is going to be asked to fill in a box, and she would like there to be a hand of hers that can be compared against in about four years.**

## Chapter 184 — remainder of the entry

**The sheet is printed whole in this chapter and it is the batch's document.** A heading that says what the nine lines are and where they came from: the fourth column of one line of a register of witnesses kept in a house in a lane about four miles off the old river road, **and a register of witnesses has five columns.** The second person is not named and the house is not named, and the sheet says why in its own words: **the only piece of evidence that cannot be published without publishing a person is the column the second person is in.** The day is not given, and the sheet says why: **the line is the only line in about four hundred that carries no day, nobody has established whose hand put a day there or whose hand took it out, and a date printed beside them would be a number of years on the only line anybody in this matter has, with nobody at the end of it.** The first column is the witness and the name in it is **Ilyra Kest.** The finding, in his own words: **a family that holds nothing enforceable is an eligible family and not a poor one, and a recorded claim with a name on it is a road, and a road does not care whether the claim can be enforced.** The nine lines are set out entire and without alteration. **Given up by me. M. Kest. I hold no office and no fund and I am not a lawyer.** Tamsin Rook reads it standing up, once, and gives him the count and the date back without being asked. **Then she says the best sentence on it is the sentence that ends him**, and it is *a register of witnesses has five columns*, because a heading that does not say what a thing is is a lie and he is not going to print one, and it is the sentence that tells about seventy people by the end of next month that there was a part. He says it out loud because in about four years somebody with a pen is going to ask why he told the world a thing existed and hid which thing: **I published one column of one line of a book of about four hundred lines and I told the room how many columns the register has, and the day is not on it, and I have explained it in four lines and I believe the explanation, and every one of those four things I did on purpose.** Then the part that is not on it. **The third column has no heading over it and it is where the second person is, and the second person is a woman of sixty-three at the end of a lane about four hundred yards further on, and he is not going to publish her.** She tells him that is **protection and it is a machine and he has just used it**, and that in about four years somebody is going to decide he did it kindly or for himself and neither is what happened — **what happened is that he could not do it the other way and he did it anyway and he wrote four words about it, and there is no virtue in either direction.** He says he has not called it a kindness and is not going to be thanked for it; she says then do not be thanked for it, **and do not be quietly pleased with yourself about it in a rented room either, and I say that as a person who has watched a man in a good coat do the second thing and I have watched it twice.** He says the price of being a party out loud for the sixth time and **says the number out loud with it, because a number is findable and a person who says a number can be held to it**: there is nothing to be struck off him, there is no career, **a person has to be damaged in what they are, and the only thing I am is a shape, and I have just handed the shape to a printer**, and he tried the other order first and in the other order it is a speech about courage. She says the seventh is not about either of them and that it stopped being about them the moment it stopped being about her, and she has not decided what to do about noticing that. **He pays for the printing: three hundred sheets, and a board at fourpence a copy, and a stall in a passage off the same building as the fourth counter, and the fourpence is the ordinary rate and it is the rate at which about four hundred people a year buy a fact about how a book was copied.** The printer does not ask who he is, **because at fourpence a copy the seller and the buyer of a thing are the same kind of person, and that is the only privacy there is in this trade and it is not a thing anybody built.** **And there is no form in this empire for stopping a copy.** Then he stands in the passage for about forty minutes on the day it goes up and had not planned to. A woman of about thirty reads the heading and the first four lines and asks whether it is a play or a thing, and does not buy one, **and he stands four feet from her and listens to a stranger read his mother's arrangement and cannot hear a sound of it, because a sheet of paper in the hand at a board makes no sound at all, and that is the first thing a person discovers about a public document and it is not a thing anybody warns you about.** A man of about fifty buys a copy, gives no name, pays fourpence, and goes. **He has written down that he stood there to see whether it was worth it, and that what he found out was that it is worth fourpence to a man of fifty, and that is a different question from the one he asked himself in a rented room, and he has not worked out which is the real one.** He writes four words in the margin of his own book that night, **the only four words in that book about anybody but the dead: *a person, not a column.*** Nobody thanked him and he said in advance they were not to.

## Chapter 185 — remainder of the entry

There is blue in the second knuckle of the third finger of her right hand. **The board is in a passage off the same building as the fourth counter.** The first line says what it is; she reads the second line about four times: *they are the fourth column of one line of a register of witnesses kept in a house in a lane about four miles off the old river road. A register of witnesses has five columns.* She buys a copy at fourpence, in full, and the printer's man does not ask her name and does not thank her. On a step in the passage she reads the nine lines and stops at the fourth: **a person who holds nothing cannot be given anything and that is the whole of it and it is the best of it.** **That is our house** — and then, nine seconds later, and in the wrong order, which is how the real ones come: ***the house is to hold nothing* means something other than a building, and it is about the people in it, and it is her family, and it was written by somebody who was in a room and was not asked where the idea came from.** She turns the copy over and counts on the back of it, because there is a step and a pencil and about nine minutes, **because counting is the only thing she has ever been able to do with a feeling.** She counts the eleven houses out of her head, and the last number is the one that is not a number, **the number of days worked by a person in each house, which is the number the fourth column has wanted since before she was seventeen, and she has it, and she has never written it down.** A woman of about forty at the next board asks how she does it without writing it, and is told: *you do it every week, and after about two years it is not remembering, it is a place in your head with your hand on it* — and the woman of about forty, who came for a copy of a will and has fourpence, is told **fourpence is the whole of what a copy costs and it is the whole of what anybody can find out, and there is a difference between those two and the difference is about four years**, and buys the copy of the will. On Sallowgate she stands behind about four people in a queue outside a counter charging **a shilling a question and fourpence a copy** and does the arithmetic in about four seconds, because that is what she does: **a shilling buys somebody about four answers and a person is not allowed to ask the same thing twice, and fourpence buys one answer, and one is a document and the other is a conversation, and the only one of the two that can be shown to somebody else afterwards is the fourpence one. A shilling is what a person pays to be told. Fourpence is what a person pays so that it can be shown to a person they have not met.** She pays fourpence again at a forms counter up Sallowgate and asks for the sheet a witness is entered upon, and reads its five lines, **and counts them on the wall of the passage with the flat of her finger without touching the paper.** **Five lines, and a register of witnesses has five columns, and a book that is kept from a sheet is the shape of the sheet, because that is what a book is.** And then the chapter's finding, which she has had ready for three years without knowing she had it: **a column with no heading over it is the same book with the machine taken out.** A register of witnesses is a copy of that sheet and the columns are the lines in order. **The first column is the first line, and the first line is the person who said the thing, and the name in it is Ilyra Kest** — so her mother is in a register about four miles off a road four hundred and thirty miles down a river, in the first column, and the only thing anybody ever put there is a name. The second column is the day, the third is the second person, the fourth is what was said, and the fifth is the question nobody has ever asked. **The third column is a person who is in a room and is not the one being written about, and a column with a heading over it is a machine for turning one thing into another kind of thing, and a column whose whole content is a person who was in the room and is not the one on trial is a column with a heading over it that says a job and not a name, and every person in it for about a hundred and forty years has been reduced to a function.** And then the difference. **In the fourth book of the dyers somebody ruled a line under the fourth heading about sixty years ago in a different ink and left it, and a woman of about sixty-eight said no in writing and gave no name: somebody took a machine out of that book and they knew they were doing it and they left a line where it had been. The third column of a register of witnesses has no heading at all and nobody took one out, because there was never one there to take out, and that is why the woman in it cannot be found. She is not hidden. There is nothing over her and nothing under her and she is a set of hands in a column in a book in a house, and a person with no heading over her is not a person anybody can ask whether they were told.** She goes through four things she could have said to her brother on a cart road about four hundred and thirty miles long and says none of them, **and the reason is not the four words he wrote in a margin in a book she has not read; the reason is that she has got a piece of paper and a piece of paper is not a person, and she is not going to be rude to her brother about a piece of paper, and there is not a person in this empire she is angrier with and she has been looking for one all day and there is not one, and that is worse.** She gets back to the counting room, puts her coat on the back of the door, and does the only thing she is any good at: a count of what every person in the eleven houses and the four other houses is owed and by whom, a page long, right, on the night she found out. Nobody thanked her and she is not thanked in her own trade either. **The copy stayed inside her coat and she did not take it out again and has not read the nine lines since.**

## Chapter 186 — remainder of the entry

Two: whether she is of age — *I am twenty, I have been twenty since the ninth of the sixth, and I can tell you the day if it is any use and it is not, because I have never once been asked a question in a room by a man with a paper and had it be a thing I could be wrong about.* Three: whether she is resident in the district, which is four lines in a second hand and which says that a roll of households does not list the rights, holdings or claims of any person in it, **and that nothing in a roll of households is a consent, and a person may be entered in one without being asked and without knowing, and may be put down in it as having a place of custom or of work by any person keeping the roll.** She reads it five times and refuses the third: **I am refusing to be served. That is the instrument. That is the only one there is in this empire and I have used it once and I am not a lawyer and I am not proud of it and I am using it now**, and she asks him to write that she did not answer it, with the day, and **not to write why, because there is no box for a why and I have looked.** She tells him he was told there was no need to ask, and **that is what the third line from the end of the four lines in front of her says.** Then the sentence: ***I am not a substitute for a thing you have not told me about.*** She says the rest of it, and it is that she is twenty and a dye-house worker and keeps the books of eleven houses and four other houses for nothing, and that she has one honest sentence about what the work has cost this family — *we got the levy off the mill and the shop lost its credit in the same month and both of those are true and one of them is why there is one job in this town and it is not mine* — and **that she is not going to be the second thing in this family that somebody decides is easier than telling her, and the first thing was a boy who was not asked anything in a room and the room was for something else**, and that she has a letter of his she has not answered since the sixteenth of the eighth of last year and it is hers to leave unanswered. She asks him two questions and lets both go unanswered, and wants it on the page that she let them go: **whether the hold has a name on it — he has not got the hold — and whether the hold has a day on it — he has got a day at the top of the sheet for when it is to be served and not one anywhere else on it — and then nobody in this room can tell me whether there is a name on the thing that is holding my wage, and the only person who could tell me is the person who is not allowed to, and that is not an accident either. It is the fourth line again.** Then the wage. **The hold is in the schedule in the second hand; it is a form with a heading over it and no person under it; the person who fills it in fills in the name of a thing; the thing is a household; the household is one room over a shop with one person in it and she is it.** Her wage at the dye-house stopped in the second week of the third month. Not reduced — stopped. There is no form for a reduction and no form for a stop, and the word used is *held*, and it is the same word the district uses about a wall and a book-keeper uses about a spring, and it means a person has been made to wait. The factor asks for the figure and is refused: **a wage is the whole of what there is; a figure in a document is a figure anybody can be shown, and in about four years somebody with a copy of it and a reason and about nine hours will not be asking whether I can live on it, they will be asking whether I could**; and she has written down what nine people in this lane are owed about nine hundred times and has never once written down what she is owed, **and that is not modesty, it is that a number about a person is the easiest thing in this empire to take away.** The factor writes *held*. The woman of about sixty at the eleventh door, who has cut plates out of a bench under a window on that lane for twenty-two years and cannot read a paragraph, comes on the fifth day and asks whether the fourth column is what this is, and is told: *I do not know, and I want to be exact with you, because the form says a household and a household is a house and this is one room over a shop and there is one person in it, and I do not know what it does when a household has four people in it and two of them are in a dye-house.* She is shown the fourth line on the counter rather than told it twice, **and is told that it says *a person may be put down in it as having a place of custom or of work by any person keeping the roll*, that any person does not say a person may be asked first, that it says nothing at all about asking, and that it does not say how many of us a household is allowed.** The woman says: ***then I will tell you what I want and you will tell me if you can. I want to be asked. Not before, not after, in a room, at the door, in words, out loud, where a person has to stand there while I say it. I have been on this lane forty years and nobody has ever asked me one single thing and I have got very good at it and I am telling you that I would like to be asked.*** And she is told she will not be able to do that, and told about the assessor and the sentence, and told **that she is going to use it today on a form and that she is not going to pretend to her that it is the same as using it in a doorway, because it is not and it is much smaller**, and the woman says *smaller is not nothing* and goes home and is not thanked. And then the finding, on the bench, in about nine minutes, and it is not about the levy. **The first time they came for her they came with a debt — a proper instrument with a seal on it and a word in it that somebody else had written and a name on it that was not hers in the box where her name belonged and a clause at the end that anybody could see and a guild's challenge in the ordinary way — and the whole of that was arguable, and she argued with it and was right about the exit clause and lost about the rest and was not sorry about losing. This time they have come with a description. A description does not have to be argued with. There is no instrument with her name on it that anybody can strike a word out of, and no seal, and no substituted signer, and no clause anybody can see. There is a heading over a form and the form says a household and the household is me, and there is nothing on it to be angry at, and that is why it is harder.** She takes the fourth book off the shelf and opens it at the fourth column: the six-year-old refusal with no name in it, the word *Noted* in an unknown hand, her own two days and her own name at the foot of her own line. **And under all of it, on the third day of the third month, a woman of twenty wrote a fourth thing, and it is four lines long, and it is the second time she has left her name out of that column on purpose and the first time anybody has asked her to:** she answered the first two out loud because they are facts and facts are free; she did not answer the third, out loud, in a room, with the day on it, and she did not put her name to the line; **the reason is in the line below and the reason is that the whole of my work for three years has been to keep names out of a column that turns a person into a record, and I am not going to be the first person in this book who put one in and called it a duty**; there is no why on the form they had and there is no why in this book and she is not going to supply one to either; **there are four words and they are: a person, not a column. My brother wrote them in a margin in a book I have not read. I have not seen the margin and I know the four words, and I want that written down, and I want it written down here, in my own hand, by me, in a book that is not mine, in a column with no heading over it. That is the whole of it. — N. Kest, and I am not going to be thanked.** She reads it back once, because there is nobody in the room and she is not doing it for anybody.

## Chapter 187 — remainder of the entry

Asked which bit of them is us, he says the last bit, and that a clerk filled it in because the box says to, **and the box says the person, institution or thing engaged in the matter and a person fills a box the way the box is printed.** The yard does the rest of it, because that is what a yard is, in about eleven minutes. Prawle gets the list of works off the nail in the tool shed, with jobs against days and a name at the foot and **a day at the end of it in chalk and the same day in ink in a clerk's own book with no office on it**, and puts it on the bench by the mortar, and about nine men come over it. **The rate on a wall on that list is a shilling a day.** A man of about thirty from the second gang: **then the form has got the works in it. Not a wall. The works. And every man on that list is in the works.** Nobody says anything for a while and then about six of them say it at once, which is the only arithmetic a yard has ever needed. And Prawle: **it was never the wall, the wall is why I am here every day and nobody has ever once said the wall was in dispute; the form is not about the wall, the form is about the works, and a works is a thing, and the thing is what the box asked for.** The man from the office looks at the list twice and says **nobody told me that**, and asks it to be said in the yard, and is told: **say it again to your office, and if they ask you what the words mean, do not tell them you asked us. Tell them you were told by a mason. That is worth more than anything you could have found out in an office and it will last about four years.** A boy of sixteen on the list asks whether that means he is finished, and is told: **you are on a list of works with a day at the end of it and this form has got no name under it and no day at the end of it, and there is no way on earth for a form with no name under it to finish a boy who is on a list with a day against it — and I have to tell you that I cannot tell you that for certain, because I have been in an office for nineteen years and every single thing I have ever read there that had no name on it finished somebody.** Then the finding in a mason's mouth, said in the shed and not in the yard, about eleven days after: **a heading is a machine, a column with a heading over it is a machine, and a form with a heading over it and no person under it is a machine that runs on whatever is nearest, and if there is nothing else near it then it runs on the works, and the works is a thing, and a thing is a thing you can be held against without anybody being in it at all. I did not put my name in it. I put a wall in it.** About four days of a man's work in a fortnight are held; the rate is a shilling on a listed wall and three shillings on an unlisted one; **he has argued that with a man in a shed four times and lost four times and is not going to argue it a fifth time for nothing.** The cost in nine sentences in the yard, because he is not a man who says a hard thing quietly: four shillings is a fortnight of a boy's food and a fire and a barrow's wear, **and the barrow's wear is the part that does not come back at the end of a fortnight because the barrow is the same barrow and it has been up a wall four more days.** He has a boy of nine who does not know and is not going to be told, **and he is not going to be one of those men who tells his boy about the state of the district works; he will find out at some point from somebody who is not me, and that will be a different afternoon and it is not going to be this one.** And then: **I stood in a room for about twenty minutes on the second day of the twelfth month of next year with a lamp and a bench and ten entries read out loud, and I was not asked whether I would mind, and I said yes in five words. She told me beforehand how long I would be standing there, and that is the only time in about eleven years anybody has told me how long anything was going to take. I have not been thanked for that and I am not going to be thanked for it, and the day somebody thanks me for standing in a room is the day a person comes about the boy.** And the thing he is not going to say: **I am not going to tell anybody that it was worth it. It was not worth four days of a shilling and it was not worth four days of anything. A man who says a thing was worth it after it has cost him four days of a shilling is a man who has stopped counting, and I have counted every day of my working life in this trade and that is the only thing I have that my father did not have.** He went and found the clerk. **He did not ask her to fix it. He said one sentence in the ordinary way, the way a man says a thing he has said four times in a shed: the rate is a shilling and the district owes him for a wall. The form is not the wall.** Ivet Sarn read a definition out of the schedule to him standing in the corridor: *Engaged in the matter: having been asked to do a work of or about the matter, or being paid by a person who has been so asked, or being under the direction of a person who has been so asked. There is no column under this heading and this heading is not entered in any roll, and a person may be engaged in a matter without knowing that the matter is a matter.* He has it again: **then I have not been engaged. I have been standing where a wall is.** And she will not write it down, and gives the reason: **because I am in it, and because a thing written down at this window about a form is a thing a form can be answered with; there is no box for a why in any of this and I am not going to supply one in ink and find out in four years what it does.** Then the two of them work it out together in about nine minutes, standing in a corridor, and neither of them wanted to. **A form can hold anybody it likes as long as the person administering it is not asked what the words mean.** And the person administering it is a clerk in an office about nine miles away who is not a villain, **and she is a clerk; she opened a form, she read a box, she put in the name of a thing because the box asks for a thing, she did it correctly, and she is not going to be asked what the words mean, because nobody in this empire has ever asked anybody what the words mean, and that is not a failure of her, it is the condition of her.** And Ivet Sarn: **I have been at a window for nine years and nobody has ever come to this window and asked me what a word on a form meant, and I have been waiting for it, and it has not come, and I have gone on serving them out at the rail.** And he says: **then you are waiting for the wrong thing and it is not coming, and I have lost four arguments in a shed to men who are not villains either.** Nobody thanks him. **He wants it written down that he was not thanked more than anybody else in that building was, because a woman in that building had said the price of being a party out loud five times and a man of thirty-eight in a city four hundred and thirty miles up this river had put nine lines in a dead woman's hand on a board at fourpence a copy, and a mason of fifty who has lost four days of a shilling is not in the same market as either of them and would be insulted to be told he was.** He goes back to the wall and works a shilling's worth of a day on a listed wall, **which is a third of what the same wall is worth to anybody who is not on the list, and he said it this fifth time to nobody at all, in a yard, on his own, and nobody answered him because there was nobody there.** And the four days are still held, and he is not going to ask for them back, **and the reason he is not going to ask for them back is that asking for them back means asking a clerk what the words on her own form mean, and he has been in this trade for about eleven years and he has never once seen anybody do that and survive the doing of it.**

## Chapter 188 — remainder of the entry

**In the second week of the eighth month of next year he refused, in about nine seconds, to rule a fourth column in that ledger, and the reason he gave was that a column ruled in a man's own hand is a heading over a page in about four years put there by a man who did not stand in the room. A clerk of thirty-four four hundred and thirty miles up this river has been carrying that reason for about a month and has never met him. This is what his reason was for.** His act: the box says the person, institution or thing engaged in the matter, and there is nothing under it. **Nold Prawle's is on a flood terrace a long way from here and says the district works; the mason who put a wall in that one wrote that a heading is a machine that runs on whatever is nearest, and a mason put a wall in it because there was a wall to hand and he had a boy of nine. Rennick Adley put the name of a building in it, in his own hand, in about four seconds, because there was nothing else to hand** — *the Slade Cut Company, of the hired stone store off the Slade, engaged from the first day of the fifth month of next year and continuing. — R. Adley, book-keeper, and no other person has signed this line and there is no other person in this empire who could.* When Doreth Ferrow asks what he has done, he says it is the worst thing anybody in this store has done since the spring bank, **and that it is the only thing he can do, and that he found the method out four hundred and thirty miles from here from a woman of thirty-four who has never met him and has been carrying his reason for a month; he has a choice of two things, and leaving the box empty means a clerk in an office fills it in and she will fill in a thing and it will not be a good thing and it will not be this thing, or he fills it in himself and then it is this and the roof is in a matter and he knows exactly whose roof it is because he has paid the rent out of the whole of it. It is four shillings a year and it is the roof over about forty books with no office on them and the bench the people stand at, and he put it in a box in about four seconds and would do it again tomorrow and would rather it was him than her.** Then he stands in the bay and reads it out. **He reads the names first** — not the form, the names, out of the third column, in the order they came on, every one, without hurrying the slow ones and without skipping the ones he had wanted to skip for about four months. **The third column is the one with no heading over it. It has had no heading over it since the sixth year and every one of the four hundred and something people in that book has gone into it, and about nine hundred and forty names are in that book and about four hundred of them are dead. It is the only column on this bank he is allowed to read a name out of, and the reason he is allowed to is that nobody has ever asked him for it, and a column with no heading over it is not a heading, and a person in a column that is not a heading has not been entered under anything by anybody.** Then the form, once: *Held against the Slade Cut Company, being a thing, in the matter of a recorded claim entered and marked disputed, and in the matter of the works engaged in it. The wages of the persons engaged are held at the same rate and for the same period as the sum held, and a person whose wages are so held is not to be paid that held sum by any person, on account of the person of that family.* **And then he reads it a second time, slower.** Nobody in that bay has ever asked him where he got that from, and he has been asked twice about the double reading by a woman of thirty-four four hundred and thirty miles up this river and has not told her and is not going to be asked, and **the first reading is for the words and the second reading is for the people who did not have them the first time, and he has never said that out loud to anybody either and it is written now and it is not going to be in his mouth.** Then the finding: **a hold on a rotation of about forty-one people holds every one of them and it has to name nobody, it has to name a thing, and the thing is a company, and a company is a roof, and the roof is four shillings a year** — and the part that ought to be frightening and is not: **the second season was never agreed to by anybody, never asked of anybody, began in the first month of the year after and it is about the length a season is, forty-one people are on it, nobody signed anything and nobody refused anything, and that is exactly why this is the cheapest hold in this empire. There is nothing to break. There is nobody to complain, because complaining needs a thing you were refused. There is no form anywhere that says a person may ask to be paid, and I have looked, and I know where the forms are kept and they are not there. If they had refused it, we would have had nine reasons in a book. Because they did not refuse it, we have nothing, and the nothing is what is being held.** About four people in that bay cannot read a paragraph: never asked once, not asked twice this week, **and there is no column for them and there is not going to be one, and the reason there is not going to be one is that a column for them would be a column somebody would have to fill.** A girl of seventeen is in the room at the back on a written engagement, at forty-five pence a day and four days a week, reading out loud. **She was not spoken to about the hold. She did not ask about it. A person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop**, and he has said that sentence out loud in this bay twice and has not sent anybody to anybody and is not going to, and she was not named, was not thanked, and is not going to start, and this is the last season in which anybody on this bank can ask her something and be answered for nothing. The chair of sixty-four in the bay says one thing, once, to him and not to the bay: **a carriage is not a wage; a thing that pays for a person to come and does not pay them for the day is not an offer, it is a way of counting how many rooms a person is in. I said that in this bay in the sixth month of last year to about thirty people and it was the truest thing anybody said that month and it has not been useful to anybody since, because being right about a thing is not the same as a thing being done about.** He then tells the chapter that the chair has another sentence he has not been given whole and has wanted for about four months, and is asked whether he is going to ask her, and says: **I am not going to ask her. The half I have is about a foreman throwing a paper with a heading on it away twice, and the half I do not have is on a slate in a counting room in a town about four hundred and thirty miles up this river, and a girl of twenty carried it up there on a cart and read it out in a lane, and I have never been in that lane and nobody on this bank has ever heard that half, and I am not going to be the man who says to a woman of sixty-four in a bay, please, what is the other half, because the day I ask her that is the day she stops saying the half she has got.** Then the factor's letter, on the bench since the third week of the third month, nineteen days old: *I am directed to require from you the name, place of custom and description of a person in the above works who may be served, together with the consent of that person to be served, and I am to say that without a name and a consent this office cannot proceed and will not proceed, and the matter will stand against the works until one is given.* **He has not answered it and has not thrown it away, and every day he does not send it is a day nobody has been entered, and when he does send it there is a name in a box with a heading over it, and the name belongs to a person who works about four hundred yards from that store, and the person will be served, and served is party, and party is a person a book can be true about for the rest of that person's life, and there is no form in this empire for taking a party back out of a box.** And: **we are all of us sitting on it, waiting for a letter neither of us is going to send — and I would like it minuted that I said the name of it out loud, and that about four people in this room cannot read the minute when it is written, and that I have read it out loud instead, and that the reason I have read it out loud is not kindness. It is that a fact said in a room with about thirty people in it is the only kind of fact in this empire that a person who cannot read a paragraph is also a party to.** He writes it in the bay's own ledger in the third column, with the day on it, in his own hand, and reads it back twice, once for the people who can see the words and once for the people who cannot, **and the second reading is the one he did not skip.** Nothing was thanked and nobody thanked him and he had said in advance that nobody was to. The hold is still in force; about nine men in three gangs are on a list of works with a day at the end of it; the factor's request is unanswered and is not going to be; and about nineteen people who said no with a reason in the first season are still behind that bank and are not going to be asked again by anybody in this stretch of it. And about four hundred and thirty miles up this river there is a board in a passage with nine lines in a dead woman's hand on it at fourpence a copy, and about seventy people had bought one by the end of that week, and there is no form for asking who bought them, and nobody asked.

## Chapter 189 — remainder of the entry

Marek Kest comes in at about the seventh hour and has been up two nights and has a third fair copy of a thing that is already on a board. She says the price of being a party out loud, with the number, **and it is the seventh time, and four of the seven were about her, one was about a person who is not in the room and cannot be told and cannot be warned, and the seventh is not about either of them, and she has thought about why that is and has not worked it out and is not going to guess.** Then the other thing, said for the first time to him: **she is in four bays because a paper says so, and the paper is not the reason, and there is not one of those six hundred printed slips that she put there, and she has been good at it, and she has been the door in this building for about four months and it is not the reason** — and why on this day: **if I ever say a true thing out loud about why I am in a room, then that is who I am, and there is no form in this empire for a person to be who they are, and I have been hiding behind a paper for four months because the paper is a machine and a machine cannot be asked a question about itself.** She reads what she is about to say in the middle, and says so, and counts the sentences under the table where he cannot see her, and has never admitted to anybody that she does that. **"I am not going to comfort you." It is not coldness: a person who is being comforted has stopped being in the room. You have spent nine days deciding to hand a dead woman's method to a printer, and four weeks finding out you had been eligible for an office since you were a child, and about four months since finding out that your mother made you unservable so that she could not tell you, and you have not eaten properly since the second week of the second month, and you have a third fair copy of a thing that is already in type in your hand as though the fair copy were the part that mattered. And I am not going to be the person who takes you out of this room tonight. If I do that then in about four years there will be one version of the last four months and it will be the one where a clerk of twenty-four was kind to a man of thirty-eight, and that version has no sheet in it and no board and no woman of sixty-three in it and no third column.** Then the difference between protection and secrecy, **said in the middle, with the sentences counted under the table where he could not see her:** one of them is a thing you do to a person and the other is a thing you do about them; there is no form in this empire for the first one and everybody does it anyway; the reason everybody does it is that it works, and it worked. **His mother did it to him and it worked and he is not alive anywhere anybody can serve. He did it to a woman of sixty-three in the second month of the year after and it worked and she is not on a board in a passage and her name is not in any book anybody can buy.** And the part nobody has said to him: **she was in the book the whole time, and she is in about nine hundred of them, and there is no heading over the third column of any of them, and what is in that column is her hand and not her name.** So keeping her off a board did not take her out of a book and I want you to hear that before I say the other half. **A hand is findable, and it is the most findable thing there is. Anybody with a copy and about four years can put about four hundred hands side by side and find the one that is hers, and nobody is served anything and no name is said out loud and there is no form for it and no fee and no day at the end of it.** And a register of witnesses is not a copy anybody has, because the register does not travel and the leaf does not travel and the three things a leaf needs are all three required and none of the three has been had, so the finding is not free and is not available to anybody who wants it and is not refused either — **it is simply out of reach, which is the same thing as the whole of this matter and is not the same as a refusal, and there is no form anywhere that turns out of reach into a refusal.** So the act of protection wrote down the only sentence in the world that says where the books are, put it on a board at fourpence a copy, and told every buyer there are five columns. **Not printing the third one is the kindness, and telling the world there is a third one is the part that is not, and that is not a thing I have caught you doing. It is a thing the doing is.** I am going to say that it was the same act, and I am going to say it about your mother, and about your sister, and about you, and the same hand is not a figure of speech. The same hand is the point. It is one hand in one family writing the same sentence in one register and one margin and one form, and the sentence is always that a person is better off not being asked. And I do not believe any of you three decided to do it and I do not believe the woman of sixty-three decided to do it either, and I have watched about four hundred people in this city decide to do things and almost none of them decided.** He says he is not going to say his mother was right; she says **nobody is going to tell you she was kind, and if somebody does tell you that in the next year, you will want to know what they get for it** — and then **there is nothing in this room to forgive. I have said the word out loud twice this month in two rooms and I have not got a softer one for it. I have not done anything to you that needs forgiving. I have been a party, and I have been useful, and I have said the price of both out loud every time, and that is the whole of what I have done and I would do it again on the same day.** Then, mid-conversation, **the girl of about twenty-five at the intake desk puts her head in without knocking** and asks about the board: about seventy copies in nine days at fourpence, and the printer's man does not keep them and is right not to, and she has been three times to the counter with about fourpence of her own and has not been able to ask the man who wrote it. **Tamsin Rook gives four facts and refuses to help her.** One: there is no form anywhere that says a counter takes the name of a person for a copy, and that is not a rule anybody made, it is a trade, and it cannot be asked for without inventing it. Two: he has not asked her to buy one and he cannot ask her and he would not want to. Three: a printer's man at fourpence a copy is the only person in that passage who is nobody's clerk, and if he kept a list it would be a list of strangers. Four: **if you want to know who bought a fourpence copy of a printed sheet there is nothing in this empire that will tell you, and the reason there is nothing is that about four hundred people a year pay fourpence for a fact, and a great many of them would not care to be one.** And: **then say it to the next person who asks you, and do not say it to him, and do not ask me again, because I have just given you the whole of the answer and the second time I will have nothing to add and you will know that I have nothing to add and it will be worse.** Marek Kest stood four feet away through all of it and said nothing, and gives the reason: **she is a party to this room and not to anything of mine** — and she says **that is the correct reason and it is the reason I am going to say the next thing at all.** Then the one thing in the room he has not asked about: **you are not going to ask me and if you do then I will not answer, and it will not be a refusal to be served. It is a refusal to be asked, and I have never had to use that one and I would rather not learn tonight what it costs. There are four in it and one of them has been there since the first month of the year before last and it has not been sent and it has not been opened, and this is the seventh time in two years I have not thought about it, and I am not going to start now.** And the last of it, as a fact and not advice, **said standing up with her hand on the back of her own chair:** **I am not going to decide anything in a room on a day when one of us has been up two nights. I am not going to be told things. I have not asked anybody for anything since the day I was refused in a room and nobody has asked me for anything either, and those are two facts about the same week and only one of them is in a book, and I have made my peace with which one. And there is a form in this empire that makes a person a party, and there is no form in this empire that makes a person safe, and I have been the first of those since the last week of the seventh month of next year and I have said the price of it out loud seven times and nobody has thanked me once and I did not expect to be thanked once and I would like it written down that I did not, because in about four years somebody is going to read that I said the price seven times and they are going to assume somebody heard it.** Nobody had heard it. He put the third fair copy inside his coat, where four sheets of copyist's paper had been since the second week of the twelfth month of next year, and went out of the door and down four bays and along a corridor, and she shut the room, put the lamp out, and left the one thing in it alone.

## Chapter 190 — remainder of the entry

**A printed sheet on a board with nine lines in a dead woman's hand in it and a man's name at the foot of it and a heading that says what it is and where it came from, and the heading is the whole of why anybody can now tell there was a part. What is not in it: a third column with no heading over it, and a woman of sixty-three, and a man of thirty-eight who did not put her there and will not be thanked for it and has not called it a kindness.** Then the last movement, and **nobody sent her.** A printer in the town where the lane is set that sheet in type in the third week into the fourth week of the second month, **because a copy of a printed sheet is worth exactly what a form is worth, which is that anybody may have it for nothing, and a printer who does not set a sheet that is going round four hundred yards of a town is a printer who has decided to keep it, and there is no form anywhere that says he may.** A man who cuts kindling and sells at every door on that side of the water bought one at fourpence. **He can read. He is the only person in that lane who can, and he has never said so and nobody has ever asked him, and he read the whole of it standing in a doorway, and then he read the nine lines out loud in that doorway to a woman of sixty-three who cannot read a paragraph, because they are in her own hand and he did not know that and worked it out in about four seconds from the middle of it, and he read them out because there was a person in front of him who could not read them and he did not have a form.** He told nobody, has not been asked, is not going to be asked, does not want to be, is not named and is not in this, and fourpence is about four days of what a man makes selling bundles at doors, and nobody has told him and nobody is going to. And she got on a cart. He took a room for four days by the day above a shop in a street in that city, out of about forty-four pounds a year with no fund and no form for a fund, because he has a rented room with a bed in it and he is not having a woman of sixty-three find where he sleeps. She came up four flights at about the sixth hour on the second day, stood in the room she had not been asked into with her shawl on, did not sit down, and saw the fair copy on the table. **"There is my hand." "Yes." "Read it out." "No." "You read it out to me in a house in a lane in the second month of the year after, twice, the second time slower." "That was a different thing and I told you at the time that it was a different thing and you are not going to get it again, and there is nobody in this room who is going to read it to you a third time and not one of them is going to be good at it."** Then the question she came for: **is there more of me than nine lines.** **Yes.** There is more of her in about four hundred lines in a bound book in a house about four miles off a road, and in about nine hundred buildings in about nine hundred books that have never been compared with anything, and in every one of them there is a column with no heading over it and a set of hands in it that is hers. And then why he cannot tell her: **because the only instrument in this empire that can hand a person the rest of what they wrote is a question, and the question is on the fifth line of a printed form, and the fifth line has been done once, in pencil, in a kitchen, in about nine minutes, in the first month of the year after, by a clerk of thirty-four who is a party and therefore can be told and therefore is the only person in the room entitled to ask it, and she said the price of it out loud before she asked, and the price was that she can be served anything said in that room for the rest of her life, and she said it to a room. And she asked you, in your own kitchen, and you answered, and the girl of nineteen wrote it in pencil, and there is a sheet of paper in that kitchen with your name on it that is not the office's name. And that was the once. Nobody is going to ask you a second time. You are sixty-three and you are owed one question and you have had it, and there is no form in this empire for asking a person the same thing twice, and if there were one then the second asking would be about the first, and you would be asked in a room whether you remember, and a person remembering a thing and a person reading a thing back are two different acts and only one of them is evidence and I said that to you in the second month and I am not going to soften it now.** **Nobody asked her the fifth line again. Nobody has sent anybody and nobody is going to.** Then she asks about the girl of nineteen: will she be asked, by anybody, ever, and the answer is **by anybody, ever, and I made sure of that by not writing her down, and there is no form anywhere that says she was ever in that room, and she has a bundle of kindling and a story about a kitchen and that is the whole of what she has and it is not nothing** — and Bess Tarrant says *then it is not only me*, and he says **it is not only you. That is the half of the fifth line the clerk of thirty-four said out loud in that room and did not make a speech about, and I have not said it in a room to anybody in about four months. You are the wrong person to be told it and the only person in this empire who is owed anything and I could not think of a better one.** And she says that is a very odd sentence, and he says neither do I, and that was the end of it. Then the second question she is owed, asked in the same words she asked it in a doorway in the rain: *Will somebody tell her. That I wrote it down. That it is there.* And **Marek Kest gives her the same answer, in the same words, because there is only one answer and he had about four days to think of a second one and did not find one: a person in a house that is shut has no address. A person who is not told has not been erased. Those are two different things, and I have known the second one for about four months and I have never once been able to say it out loud without the first one coming with it. I am saying both of them now, and the first one is the harder, and I am not going to leave it out to make the second one easier.** She says it is a very cruel thing to say to a woman of sixty-three in a room; he says yes, it is. And she says: **well, I have come four hundred and thirty miles to be told it in a room, and it is worse in a room, and I would have had it in a doorway for nothing, and there is a bed in it and you have not offered it me and I am sixty-three and I am not going to ask you twice.** He says he is sorry; she says do not be, and gives the reason: **I did not come to be told anything by a man who has no career to lose and no office to be struck out of. I came because a man who cuts kindling stood in my doorway and read my own hand out loud to me for nothing, and that is the only thing that has happened to me in this that was not done to me, and I have had four months of things being done to me and one afternoon of a man reading, and I know the difference, and I have put it in a book in my own hand with the day against it, and there is no name in it.** And: **I have never once been asked whether I am a party to anything in my life and I am sixty-three and I have kept a house and I have never once been in one of them by name, and that is the only thing I have got and it is not nothing.** Then the guarantee, **printed whole and word for word identical to Chapters 0161, 0164, 0170 and 0180, and no child named**; the Lowcross bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence, unpaid, nobody liable, about a penny a day, no line for a bridge in that fund and there has not been one in nineteen years, four hundred and eleven on the far side and three hundred and eighty-eight crossings, **and those are two figures and they are never the same**; a second season in its fourth month, agreed to by nobody, asked of nobody, **and the wages of about forty-one people on it held against a company whose whole money is four shillings a year and all of it is the rent of a roof**; about nineteen people who said no with a reason in the first season still behind that bank, not asked a second time, **and that is not this volume's last chapter**; about four people who cannot read a paragraph never asked once and now not asked twice, and no column for them and not going to be one; **The thirty-one of Mosswake are thirty-one.** And the last of it, **not a cliff and a name:** a form printed in about nine hundred buildings with five lines on it, the fifth asked once in pencil in a kitchen by somebody who said the price out loud first, and nobody going to ask it again. **A hearing is a room. A room is where records are asked for, and a record is what a person asks for when a person is going to do something with it**, and a clerk of thirty-four said that out loud at a counter in the second week of the first month of the year after, and **about four years later there is going to be a room in which a woman of sixty-three is asked whether there is more of her than nine lines. That room has a name. The name is a hearing. Nobody has written the hearing down yet.** And in a passage in this city there is a thing on a board at fourpence a copy, and it says, in a dead woman's hand, that it is to be done on a box and not on a paper, because a paper goes to a house and a box goes to a roll. **This is the first time in about four months that the paper has gone to a house, and nobody asked her to send it.**

## What Volume 04 Batch 0004 resolved, what it did not, and what it hands on

**Resolved:** a copy of a record bought at fourpence at a counter in the second week of the first month is now certified, in nine seconds, by that counter, with a clerk's hand at the foot of it, and the seal was not read out and the buyer's name is not kept. A notice of a seat with nobody's name on it has arrived in Auremar in six numbered lines with a seat's own device and a seal and nothing under the seal, and a schedule in a second hand with four definitions and a provision. The reading of ten entries is reported in four lines of a letter and its finding is that a hearing is not a document. Nine lines in a dead woman's hand are on a public board at fourpence a copy with a heading that says what they are and where they came from, and about seventy copies have gone out in about nine days. A wage is held in a household of one person and against a company whose whole money is the rent of a roof. A mason has four days of a shilling a day held and is not going to ask for them back. A book-keeper has put the name of a building in a box in his own hand and read the names out of a column with no heading over it. A woman of twenty has refused the third of three questions in a room, out loud, with the day on it, and has written a fourth thing in a book that is not hers with no name in it. The conversation about protection and secrecy has happened, as a scene of its own, with a day on it.

**Not resolved, and not this batch's to resolve:** nobody has been entered as a representative, and nobody has been asked; about seventy people now know there was a part and cannot tell which part; the answer Bess Tarrant is owed is still not payable and has now been given twice in the same words and does not pay either time; a clerk of thirty-four's own reading of her own price is on a page and nobody heard it; a mason is owed four days and a book-keeper is owed the whole of a second season's wage and neither is going to ask; the factor's request for a name is unanswered and the letter is on a bench; a girl of seventeen has not been asked anything and has not been contacted and has not been named; about nineteen people are still behind the bank and have not been asked again; the end of the run, the row, the striking-out, the column headed *by whose authority* and the name in it are untouched for the fifth movement running; the Ashfall bundle is shut and unnamed and what Ilyra Kest found is unanswered; no hearing has been written down; and the V4 half of the relationship milestone is still unpaid and is not paid by anything in these ten chapters.

## Chapter 191 — remainder of the entry

**The woman at the table is Orla Denning, about fifty-five, nineteen years of minute books, not a lawyer, not a party, not a judge, and she has never been asked what any of it meant.** She says a copy is fourpence and **a shilling is a person, and there is no price in this building for a day and if there were one I would not know what to do with it.** He says **a date with nobody's name on it is not a summons to a person**, and she tells him he is the second person in four weeks to have said that to her, and she wants it noted that she noticed. **The first entry: the restitution hearing has a day — the third day of the third week of the fifth month, at the second hour, in a room with a rail in it** — and its notice is in six numbered lines with a seat's own device cut into the head and a seal at the foot and **nothing under the seal**, a second line asking that no inference be drawn from the absence of any name, and a sixth line that will not name the office's holder. She declines to say the name at the foot of the earlier notice, he did not ask her, and **she writes a line in the margin saying nobody asked her, and it is the first line she has written in nineteen years that is not a date.** **The second entry: a room, four years off, for a question — whether there is more of a person than nine lines — with the person described and not named, about sixty-three, and a line under it saying the person has been asked once.** The figure is the figure a clerk of thirty-four said out loud at a counter in the second week of the first month, entered on the day it was said and not in a minute book, and this is the first time it is in one. **She did not know what she was entering and entered what she was given, and says so in the room, and writes that down, and says that a clerk of nineteen years who says afterwards that she did not know is a clerk of nineteen years who was asked.** The finding: **a person who is not told cannot be told, and a person who cannot be told cannot be warned, and a person who cannot be warned cannot be asked whether she wants to be asked**; a date is findable and a person cannot be stopped from a date. **The only protection a person has against a day is to be told about it, and being told is a service, and being served is a party, and there is no way back out of a party.** He asks where she is: in this city, came up the road on a cart, stood in a room hired by the day for four days, asked him a question he could not answer, and has not gone home or has come back. Then: **the protection a name at the foot of an entry gives is fourpence of ink and about nine seconds of a woman's time, and it works in one direction — it makes the clerk answerable and it does nothing whatever for the person the date is about.** She writes down what he says because he asks her to and gives the reason, and says the part she does not have: **there is no column for telling anybody, and there is no column for it in any book in that building, and she has looked for nineteen years.** And then the ordinary machinery answers the date: **a third line with no day under it is a third line, and a third line with a day under it is a thing to be done, and what is to be done is a box printed in about nine hundred buildings, and a person fills a box the way the box is printed.** It went into a box on the fourth day of the third week of the fifth month in an office about nine miles from a counter, by a clerk who is not a villain, correctly, and nobody was asked and nobody was told and nobody knew, and **it was not done by anybody who wanted it done and was not done in a hurry and was done in about nine seconds by a person doing her job properly, which is the whole of what has gone wrong in this matter for about a hundred and forty years and is the reason nothing can be put right by anybody being found.** He does not go to the counter and ask to be entered, and writes down why: **a man with nothing enforceable behind him has no place of custom or of work anywhere in this empire to be entered, and he is the cheapest person in this empire for it, and the reason he is the cheapest is that his mother made him that on purpose, and she was right and he is not going to say so and nobody has told him she was kind.** He does not tell his sister. He writes both dates in a book with no office on it — *asked for by me and entered by a clerk who was not asked what it meant*, and *not asked for by anybody and entered anyway* — and **a book with no office on it is a thing a form cannot be answered with, and that is the only kind of record a person in this matter has ever had, and it has cost him nothing and it is worth nothing and he is going to keep it.** Nobody thanked him and he said in advance that nobody was to. And about four hundred and thirty miles down this river a foreman of fifty-one has never once in nine years been asked to read anything by anybody at the district works, and **nobody sent her, the same as nobody sent the woman of sixty-three, and there is no form in this empire for a person who comes on her own account, and the two of them are not compared and are not going to be.**

## Chapter 192 — remainder of the entry

Four facts: **the seal on the paper the buyer produced is not the seal of this table and it was not read out**; the buyer's name was not asked for and is not kept at that counter; the copy was issued in full at the ordinary price to a person on a day; **and there is a certification at the foot of the request for it, written in about nine seconds on the seventh day of the first week of the second month, and she said the price out loud before she wrote anything — a document with her hand at the foot of it is findable in about four years by anybody with a copy of the right page.** There was no charge, **because the fee board at that counter has four things on it and none of them is time and there is no line on it for a certification of that counter's own book, because a table cannot charge a person for telling the truth about its own book.** Then a sheet is handed in and put on the table face up: the sheet a witness is entered upon, five lines, printed, in about nine hundred buildings, with a stack of them about nine inches deep on the table, **and not one of them has ever been looked at by anybody for any reason.** **There is no post in this empire whose job is to stand in front of a heading and ask, and on the fourth day of the second week of the fifth month somebody asked**, in four words: *what is the fifth line for.* She said the price out loud first, **because the answer is a thing she did and the thing she did is findable**: **from the day I was served I can be served anything about it for the rest of my life, and there is no form anywhere for taking a party back out of a box, and that is the eighth time I have said it out loud in a room** — four of the eight about her, one about a person who is not in the room and cannot be told, one about nobody in particular, one about neither of us, and this one about a sheet of printed matter. Then the answer, in the ordinary way, in about nine minutes. **The fifth line is a question, and it is the only instrument in this empire that can hand a person the rest of what they wrote** — not a copy, not a register, not a court — **asked in a room, out loud, by somebody entitled to ask it, before the thing is said, and written down with the answer on it.** The person entitled to ask is the second person, which is the person in the room who is not the person being written about, **and that is in a printed sheet in about nine hundred buildings and has been for about a hundred and forty
