I have carried more of them than I can count and I have never once seen anybody stop to read that one.** She answers him out loud, in a corridor, in about four minutes, and tells him what the box is and where the words come from and that the office is the only place in this empire that fills it in and does it right, and that what goes in that column is a household or a company and that nobody has ever asked which of the two a thing is, and she tells him the definition and the roll and the four miles of road and the day in the fifth month, **without once saying the name.** **He asked because he was going to say something about the trays, and she answered because he was standing there, and neither of them chose it, and she has thought about that since and it is the reason the answer was any good. A room wants a record. A corridor wants a tray gone. Neither of those is a reason to ask a question and both of them are why one got asked. A form does not get asked anything by a man with a tray. A woman does, and the woman answered, and there is not going to be a post for it.** He asks whether to put it down as usual. **Put it down as usual and read the head of the file to the man at the counter on the second floor, out loud, both lines.** I want him to hear the second one and not only the first, and I am not going to say why and you are not to ask me. *That is not usual.* **And I would like it noticed that you asked me who fills that box in, and that nobody in nineteen years has asked me what the words on it are for, and that you did not ask a form anything. You asked a woman standing at a counter, and I answered you because I was standing there.** He reads it out on the stairs, both lines, which is not usual, and she hears him from the bottom and does not go up. Last: **There is no post in this empire whose job is to stand in front of a heading and ask what it is for** — not in that building, not in that city, not in any of the four hundred and thirty miles between that desk and a district where a woman of about sixty walks down a lane with a sheet on a board and a basket once a year, **and she does not know what the fourth column of her own roll is for either, and she never will, and nobody is going to ask her, and it is four hundred and thirty miles and there is no fund.** The file is on the table with two lines on the head of it and it is not in the press and it is going to stay not in the press for as long as she is the clerk who keeps the books.

## Chapter 202 — remainder of the entry

Nobody had heard it.** A woman of twenty has been up to that counter three times; the third time is the time, because the first two times she stood at the rail and looked at the board and went away, and a clerk notices that the way a clerk notices everything, which is to say she notices it and does nothing about it for about eleven days. **You have not asked it twice. You have looked at the board twice. The board does not have your question on it and it is not going to, and there is no price on that board for a question.** The woman puts a sheet of her own on the slab with a heading she wrote herself and one day on it and nothing else. **That is yours.** *Yes.* **I am not going to write anything on it, and here is the price of what I am about to do.** A document with my hand at the foot of it is findable in about four years by anybody with a copy of the right page, and I have said that number out loud twice in a room and I am saying it again because a number a person has said can be held to. A question about a box is worth a question at a notary's table, a question is a shilling there, and here there is nothing, **and the nothing is why I can do this and the nothing is also why you had to come three times.** Then the answer, in about four minutes, without a single word twice: **A representative is a person the machinery can act on.** That is the whole of the word and it is a box's word, not a lawyer's, and about four hundred people a year come up to that counter wanting a lawyer's word and there has never once been one on the board. **Being in that box is the only way anybody has ever found of making you a party**, and it did it without asking you, and the third line of the notice says in its own words that no further consent of the person is required, **and that is not a wicked sentence. It is a sentence written by somebody who needed a form to go out in a week.** What it lets them do: **Nothing you would recognise.** It lets a form put your name in a place where a name is a thing that can be asked about. There is no hand behind it. There is no man behind it. **The wage of yours that is held is held under a heading with nothing under the heading, and this box is the same, and the two of them are the same act done by the same machinery in the same year, and about four hundred and thirty miles apart.** *And can it come out.* **No.** *Write that down.* **I cannot.** **Then say why not, and say it out loud, and I want it in the room.** Marn Ottery puts both hands flat on the oak, which she does about twice a year, and says: **There is no form in this empire for a document about a person who did not ask to be in one. That is the reason and it is the whole of the reason and it is not a matter of taste and it is not caution. A form is a thing that happens to a person.** Every one of them is printed, every one of them is filled in by somebody else, **and not one of them in about a hundred and forty years has had a line on it for a person to put a question on and get an answer in writing, and the reason is that a form about a person is the thing the person cannot get out of.** And she has looked, at that counter, in the book of forms under it, in the ordinary course of her job, and it is not there and never has been, and the last time this empire came close was about a hundred and forty years ago, and what it had then was a fifth line that asked the second person whether she was a party before anything else was said, **and that line is dead, and it is dead because the person it asks cannot be told that there is a question.** The woman of twenty: **So the answer exists, and there is nowhere to put it.** **The answer exists and it is in your head now and it has a day on it because you asked it on a day,** and the only place in this empire it can go is a room with a person in it, and a room is not a form, and a room writes down whatever it likes and can be asked about it. Then a blank out of the box, where there are about four hundred, bought and not filled in: **That is fourpence and I am not charging you fourpence and I am not going to pretend that is a kindness, because it is the ordinary trade of this counter and I would have sold it to anybody who asked for it. Take it. It is proof that you asked.** The woman does not take it. **It is about an instrument, and I am not in an instrument. I am in a box, about four hundred and thirty miles from where I was standing when you said that, and if I take that sheet off this counter it will be a sheet in my hand that says nothing about me at all, and I have got one of those already and I made it myself.** *Take it anyway.* **No, and I am going to tell you why, and it is because a person who takes a piece of paper off a counter is a person who has been helped, and I have been helped four times this year and I can count them, and a person who is helped is a person who owes, and I have not got a figure for what I owe and I am not going to be the one who starts.** Marn Ottery writes the day on the slab in pencil where it will come off, because that is what a counter does, and says **That is not a record.** *I know what it is.* Then the rule, said standing up: **The only thing standing between you and the rest of your life is somebody asking a question out loud in a room. That is it.** There is no other instrument in this empire and there has not been one since the second person stopped being a person. And there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask — no post, in about nine hundred buildings, in any of the four hundred and thirty miles between that counter and that lane, in any office, at any counter, at any window, in any court, whose business is to stand in front of a heading and ask what the words on it mean. **Ask for a form for it. Go round all nine hundred of them. It is not there, and it has never been there, and in a hundred and forty years there has been one asking of a word on a form, and the one was a person asking another person in a room that wanted a record, and nobody chose it, and it will not happen again by itself.** *And you. You have answered me. Why.* **Because you were standing here and I have had eleven years, and because I am a clerk, and a clerk is a person who can be asked things, and about nine people a year find that out, and I say so at the counter before anybody asks me. A foreman can be sent for and a clerk cannot, and I have not been sent for and I have not been protected and I have never been asked whether I wanted to be in a room and I have never been able to leave one and nobody has ever had the manners to notice the difference.** A man of about fifty comes up, says four words, pays fourpence for a copy and goes; **That is about four hundred of them a year, and every one of those men has walked out of this room with a piece of paper in his hand and a thing in it that they could not get before they came in. And not one of them was asked what the fourth line means, and about nine in eleven years have stood at that rail and looked at the board and gone away, and two of them were you.** *That was not the same.* **It was exactly the same, and the reason it was not the same is that I am a person and a counter is not, and the whole of what is wrong with this room is that a person stands in it on more days than the forms do, and there is a form for the counter.** And then, at the rail on the way out, turning round, which nobody at that counter does: **You said a room writes down whatever it likes and can be asked about it.** *That is what I said.* **Then write this down and put your name at the foot of it. Not for me. I do not want it and I will not carry it. Write it and put it in your own book, and in about four years I will not know you did it and you will not be able to give it to me, and that is not a kindness either, it is the only shape the thing has.** And Marn Ottery, exactly: **That is the first thing anybody has asked me for at this counter in about eleven years, and I am not going to be able to do it, and I want to be exact about why, because you will not ask me again.** *Then say it.* **There is no form for it, and I have been a clerk long enough to know that a clerk who puts a sentence of her own into a book with no office on it has made a document, and the whole of what I have been afraid of since the second month is a document with my hand at the foot of it.** Out on the street: **There is a day, and I am not going to send it to anybody, and I am not going back down that road with nothing, and I have been in this city since the third week of the fifth month and I am not going home until somebody asks me a question in a room.**

## Chapter 203 — remainder of the entry

**He has no office and no fund and no register and nothing in this empire he could certify anything with, and a bar of his own that came off him on the twelfth of the ninth month of last year on an application of one line which he wrote himself. There is a line about two inches long on the inside of his left wrist. It reads nothing. It decides nothing. It does not know where the ford is.** She is in the room because two instruments require it and the two instruments are not one instrument and neither of them merges them: a paper made in a form in four days with a day on it, and a watch that requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be in any room he is in. The thing he could not do was count, and he had spent nineteen days failing to do it in about nine different ways, and on the third day he stopped, **and the stopping was the finding.** The office of a seat keeps eleven minute books, and a minute book takes down what a room did, on a day, with a clerk's name at the foot, and eleven of them on a shelf is a complete record of a thing nobody looks at twice a year. **An entry is not a minute.** That is the whole of the difficulty and it took him nineteen days because he kept trying to make the books into a register, and they are not a register and never were, **because a book that takes down what a room decided cannot be made into a book of what a room did to a person, because the second thing does not happen in a room. It happens in an office about nine miles from a counter, in about nine seconds, in a hand, on a sheet, and the sheet goes out.** **So the office of a seat has a shelf of what was said and no shelf at all of what was done.** Then the engine, in his mouth and then hers: **A roll of households does not say a person is of age and resident. It says a household is at a door.** The office asks a district for the person of a family who is of age and resident, and a district has got nothing in the world that answers that, and so the office asks the next question, **which is the only question there is an answer to, which is who is on the roll.** Said again in the other order: **A roll of households does not list rights, holdings or claims. That is on the face of the third line and it has been there longer than I have been alive.** So a district can tell the office where a person is and can tell it nothing else whatever — not what they hold, not what they are owed, not whether they are of age, **and not which of the people at that door the office is asking for** — and the office is asking for a person and is putting the answer in a box that says the person, institution or thing engaged in the matter. **And a roll of households has a household in it.** *That is the answer to something.* **That is the answer to a question nobody asked. That is how a company of nine families got into a box with a heading over it, and how a foreman of fifty-one got into one, and how a chapel and a poor-box and a company of watermen got into about four hundred returns in a bundle in about nine hundred buildings.** And then the sentence he has not said out loud in about two years, and says in this room: **The third line catches anybody of age and resident of a family that holds nothing enforceable, and it catches them by finding them on a roll of households. My mother made sure there was no Kest on any roll in this empire, and that was the right thing to do and was done in a room in about nine minutes by a person who was not asked, and it is the reason there is no Kest on the roll of that district four hundred and thirty miles up this river, and the roll is the only thing in this business that could have found my sister, and she is on it, and she is found, and the finding is what got her in. The same hand is not a figure of speech and I have said so before in another room and I am not going to improve it. One family, one act, and it made two of its children unfindable and findable in the same year.** Nobody says anything for about a minute and a half. Then the count, and the counting is the discipline: two are known; he writes the two in his own book on a clean sheet, looks at them for about a minute, **then takes the pen and puts a line through both of them and writes *two* against them instead.** *You have destroyed it.* **I have taken two names out of a book with no office on it, in a rented room, with nobody in it, and there is no instrument anywhere that stops me, and I did it before the ink was dry on the line I had struck through, and that is not a record of anything. That is a man tidying.** The sheet is for the questions: **One line per question in this matter that nobody has asked. I have got eleven and most of them would take a person about four minutes to ask and about four years to answer,** written down in the order they ought to be asked, and there is no order, and the day is against the sheet and not against the lines. *There is a twelfth.* **There is a twelfth line and it is empty and it is going to stay empty.** *Then write on it what it is.* **If I write on the twelfth line who is going to ask the questions, then I have appointed somebody, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and a person who appoints somebody to a post that does not exist is doing the thing the whole of this is about, which is filling in a box the way the box is printed. I am not going to do that and I would like it noticed that it took me nineteen days not to do it and that I was certain about it by the time I had the pen down.** He reads the eleven out because she asks: what a second person is on a witness form; whether a roll of households can say a person is of age; what a company of nine families is inside a definition that says a family; what a room wants when it asks a question; whether a minute book with a clerk's name at the foot of an entry protects the person the entry is about. *That one has an answer, and the answer is no, and it was given by the person who wrote it, out loud, in that room, and it protects the clerk and nothing else, and I would have struck it off the sheet.* **I have not struck it off.** **Do not strike off the things that have been answered. A list of questions with the answered ones still on it is the only honest list there is, and the moment it is tidy somebody will believe there is less to do.** Then: whether a person can be un-entered; what a hold on a household of four does; who wrote a line in a margin in a hand that was not his; whether a company that cannot be served can be said to have refused anything; whether a person who is in a house that is shut has an address. *That one is a finding, and it is in a minute book with a day on it and my name at the foot of it, and you did not ask it, I answered it in the first week of the fifth month, in a room, and you were in the room, and it is on your list as though it were still open.* *Then I will put a day against that line as well.* **Put the day against it, and not my name, because if you put my name on eleven lines then in about four years that sheet is a list of things a clerk of twenty-four has been asked, and I am a person who cannot be instructed and I am not going to be a document about a woman of twenty-four who was in a room.** He writes the day against the tenth line and leaves the name off it, reads the sheet back once and not twice, and the eleventh line is the one neither of them says out loud. Then the rule, used once, in a hand and not a form: *Marek Kest's choice, the third day of the third week of the ninth month of the year after. Either I go to the office of a seat and ask a question out loud in a room, or I do not. If I go, I am a person who has asked, and a person who has asked is answerable for what he is told. I am not asking for an answer. I am asking to be a person who has asked. — M.K.* She confirms the rule and the date and answers no, in words, with the date, and gives the reason as a fact and not advice, using her third line and her fourth line: **The office of a seat puts more people through that room in one year than there are lines in any of the eleven books on that shelf. It does not remember them.** It has no book of them, because a book of them would be a register and that office does not keep a register, **and that is the entire structural reason the third line works and the entire structural reason nothing can be done about it in a room.** And a clerk of about fifty-five in that office is a person who has written about four thousand entries in nineteen years and has never once been asked to remember one of them, and that is a good life and it is not anybody's fault. And: **if you walk in there and ask what the fourth line of a form means, then you are a person that office remembers, because you are the first one, and you will not be remembered as a man who asked a question. You will be remembered as a man who came in about the fourth week of the ninth month and wanted to know what the words on the boxes were for.** And in about four years somebody will come back to that room to look at a return, and the clerk will not remember him, and the room will, **and one question from you teaches that room a thing it does not know, which is that the question can be asked, and a room that knows a thing can be asked stops being a room you can ask.** *That is a good reason.* **It is a fact. It is about what rooms are for. I have not advised you and if you take it as advice you are a man who has been advised in a rented room by a woman who is a party and cannot leave it, and I would rather you did not.** *You know what I did with it.* **You wrote it in order to be refused.** *Yes.* **And you got refused, and the refusal was a real answer, and it was given in about forty seconds by a person who had already thought about it, and you did not argue. That is the first time I have believed a document you wrote. Every one of the others was true and every one of them was a man getting a thing ready so that somebody would catch him at it, and I have caught you more times than I can count and I have never once wanted to catch you again, and this is the first one where I did not want to.** He puts the sheet in the book at the back after the four definitions and shuts it: **That is the rule used once, in writing, with a day on it, and it is the only instrument either of us owns, and it is the first one where the answer is a thing I did not know in the morning.** Then his wrist: it told him nothing, it has not told him anything since the fourth hour of the second of the fifth month of next year when it was up for about nine seconds, and it does not know where the ford is, **and it does not know that a roll of households four hundred and thirty miles up this river has a woman of twenty in its fourth column.** **There is no office in this empire whose business it is to ask me what that is for, and I have got about four feet of wall and a table and a rule I used once and a list of eleven questions with a day on the sheet and an empty twelfth line, and I am not going to write anybody's name on the twelfth line, and I would like it read back to me now, in this room, in words, whether you think that is cowardice.** **It is not. It is the first thing you have done since the sixth day of the third month that is not a way of making yourself expensive to look at, and I am not going to soften it and I am not going to be thanked for saying it, and nobody is going to thank me, and there is no form anywhere that requires a person to be thanked.**

## Chapter 204 — remainder of the entry

**She can read. Nobody at the district works has ever once in nine years asked her to read anything.** And: **a person who tells a shed beforehand has asked the shed for a hearing and a hearing is a party.** She has the six lines in a book of her own with a day against them, and she has read them to herself about nine times and to nobody at all, **and there is a difference between the two and nine men in a shed are not going to be shown the difference.** What is on the sheet is a notice of six numbered lines and a foot, a seat's own device cut into the head of it and not the fire-and-water mark, **her name at the top of it, in the ordinary way, in the hand of whoever sent it, and nobody has ever asked her whether she wanted it there and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires anybody to ask.** The second line asks that no inference of any kind be drawn from the absence of any name. The third line is the one that has her in it. **The sixth line declines to name the holder of the office of the seat at all, which makes it the first document in this matter that names an office instead of a person.** She announces it: **I am going to say the two lines that nobody has ever said out loud in this shed, and then I am going to do a thing, and I would like the nine of you to understand that the thing is not for you and is not about you and I am not asking any of you for anything.** The second line first: **It says that no inference of any kind is to be drawn from the absence of any name. Forty-two years I have been on this bank and I have never been in a book of anybody's, not a roll, not a return, not a list of works with a head on it, nothing, and I have been proud of that in a way I have never said out loud to anybody about anything, and I have just read a piece of paper that instructs anybody who reads it not to draw any inference from the fact that there is no name on it. That is not a threat. That is a man telling the world in advance not to look.** And I have been doing it for forty-two years without a piece of paper and he has now put mine in writing, and he could not have known that, and it would not have mattered, because the sentence is about him and not about me and I am the only person in this shed it can be said about. Then the sixth line: **It declines to name the holder of the office. So the thing that put me in this cannot be named.** It refused, not answered, not produced, not held to. Named by nobody, in a document, in a hand, with a seal at the foot and nothing under the seal. **A man wrote a line that says *I am not going to tell you who I am* into a piece of paper he is sending to a foreman on a bank, and he sent it to me, and I cannot do one thing about it, and I have wanted to say that out loud in a room for about eleven weeks.** Then the refusal, and it did not work, and she said so in advance and did it anyway: **I am refusing to be served. That is the instrument. It is the only one there is in this empire and I have never had to use it, and a woman about four hundred and thirty miles up this river used it in a room with a day on it in the fourth week of the fifth month and it did not work there either, and I am going to use it now, in this room, out loud, with today on it, and I would like one of you to write it down, and I do not care which.** **Nobody wrote it down.** One of them said afterwards, in the yard, that his hands were cold and that he had wanted to, and that nobody had given him paper, and that he had not thought to fetch any. **It has not worked, and I am going to say why, and the why is the reason I have had since the seventh month and have not said, and it is not the same why as hers, and I would like that noticed, because if the two whys are the same then it is a bad system, and it is not a bad system. It is the worst system anybody has ever written down and it works. A refusal to be served requires a service. That is all it is. It is a door you put your back against, and there has to be a door and there has to be somebody knocking. And nobody knocked. I was not served. I was entered.** I was got at by a class, and a class is not served on anybody, and there is nobody in this to knock on, and **a person who has not been served cannot refuse to be served, and the only instrument this empire has for a person to say no has just been found to be no use at all to me.** And: **that is not a hole anybody has left in it. It is a sentence written in about nine hundred buildings by somebody who needed a form to go out in a week, and it says that no further consent of the person is required, and it says that about me, and it is right, and there is no second instrument, and I have looked for one since the seventh month in every form in this empire I could get hold of and there is not one and I am the first person to have had to look.** A man of about thirty from the second gang: **And you have said it in here.** **Nothing.** That is the answer and I have had it since the first week of the seventh month and I have been carrying it about like a stone. **You do nothing, and the sheet goes on being on the bench, and there is no procedure in this empire for a person to be let out of a thing that does not need her, and I have looked for one, and the reason there is not one is that in about a hundred and forty years nobody has ever needed to be let out of one.** *You are in a box.* **I am in a box by a class, and a company of nine families and about forty-one people is a family inside the third line's own definition, and the only one of us with a place of custom or of work in a roll of households is the one whose job was never written down. That is me. I have been the reason I could not be found for forty-two years, and I have never once chosen it, and I was nine years old when it started, and a child does not choose anything.** And the fifth time, said once and not listed: **That is the fifth time, and I have worked that out this week and I am not going to list them in here, and the reason is that a list of five is a list of five, and the people in the other four are not in this shed, and every one of you has a family up the road, and I am not going to read five names out in a room with men in it. The protection and the catch are the same act, and it has been the same act five times, and nobody chose it, and the reason nobody chose it is that the people who were protected and the people who were caught were never in a room together and could not have known about each other.** Then the thing with no form behind it, which took the better part of a quarter of an hour and is the first thing she has done in nine years anybody in this trade will be able to find: she takes out a sheet and writes the six lines, all of them, in her own hand, and the device, and the foot, and the seal, and the fact that there is nothing under the seal, and the day against it in the corner, **and she does not write her name anywhere on it.** Told *that is not any use without a name on it*: **It is not any use at all without a name on it, and that is the point, and you will not understand it for about four years and I am not going to explain it to you now. A record of a document is not a record of a person.** I am going to write down six lines and a seal and nothing under the seal, and that is a thing in a room and not a woman on a bank, and in about four years a man with a copy can go and hold it up beside the one on the bench and ask somebody to say whether the two are the same, and the two will be the same, and the somebody will have to go and look at a roll, and the roll is four hundred and thirty miles up this river, and he will have to go and ask the woman who keeps it, and she is not going to know what the fourth column is for either, **and that is where it stops and it stops about four hundred and thirty miles from here with a woman of about sixty holding a board and a basket and no idea what she has in her hand.** And my hand is at the foot of it, and my hand is the only part of this anybody cannot take off me, and I have known that since I was seventeen. Then why, and not for the record: **I have said in this shed that I would rather be asked than not, and I have been asked twice and said yes twice, and I have refused to be asked a third time without being asked.** I have said that a person who is paid nothing is the only kind of person in this cut who can be told no by anybody, and I have said that a carriage is not a wage, and I have said it twice now, and I am not going to say it a third time. And the other half of the reason, which is the half she has not got, and which she uses about four times a season, and which does not go in a room that writes it down: **the day this book-keeper asks me for it is the day I stop saying the half that I have got**, and she has said that in that shed before and every man in it heard it and one of them wrote nothing down because there was no paper. Then the last of it, to her face: **I would like to be found. I have spent forty-two years being unfindable and it is the reason I am standing in this, and if it had been any other way I would be standing somewhere else with a worse thing on me, and I am not going to be embarrassed about saying that out loud at fifty-one in front of nine men. What I am not going to do is be the reason four other people get found**, and there are four of them on that bank who cannot read a paragraph and have never been asked once, and the season is coming round again, and I have thought about it since the seventh month and this is where I have got to, and I have not told a soul until now and I am not going to be thanked for it, **because there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to be thanked, and I have said that in advance and I mean it.** She puts the sheet on the bench face up beside the other one and the two of them lay there for anybody who came in. Then the thing the chapter is for: there was a man at the back of the shed who had not said anything for about nine minutes, with a book under his arm, who had not sat down, and whom she had known since the second day of the first week of the seventh month and had never once said so in a room. **He did not ask her for the other half.** He stood at the back and looked at the two sheets on the bench for about a minute and a half and then went out into the yard and shut the door behind him, which nobody else did. Afterwards, to nobody at all, in the yard, at about the eighth hour, with the dark coming up off the water and the door open behind her: **He did not ask.** Nobody answered, because there was nobody there, **and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to be answered when she says a true thing to an empty yard, and she had said it out loud because a thing a person says to a yard is not evidence of anything, and it is the only kind of saying there is that nobody can take off you in about four years**, and she has known that since the sixth month of last year and has used it about four times a season. Then she lit the lamp, which is a quarter of an hour later than it ought to be, and did not shut the door.

## Chapter 205 — remainder of the entry

**She is not hiding anything. Her caution is partly justified: the last time a docket was reopened it cost that district a winter of aid, and there is a paper about that winter in a drawer in her own room and she has never told the court why.** A return comes up the corridor in a bundle and it is not going into a file, it is going into a register, **and a return going into a register is a return that has to be copied, and a return that has to be copied is a return somebody is going to read the column on before they copy it.** The column is headed for the person, institution or thing engaged in the matter, filled in in ink in a hand that is not hers, dated the fourth day of the third week of the fifth month. **There is nothing to copy. It is done.** That is not a reason to put a sheet in a press; it is a reason to look at it, and she looked at it for about four minutes. **The date on the return is the day the column was filled in and not the day the return was sent, and there is no space on the sheet for the second day.** The office of a seat had asked that court, in the ordinary way, for the person of a family who is of age and resident, **and the court keeps a roll of households of that district for the water, and the roll has got no answer to that question in it, and about nine hundred buildings print a form with a box on it for the answer, and the office had filled the box in itself.** **So the box is not asking us anything. The box is the office's own column coming back to us with a day on it, and we are to copy a thing we did not find.** Then the man at the window, at about the ninth hour on the third day of the week, about thirty-four, moved in the second week of the ninth month from a street with a shop on it to rooms over a yard four streets off inside the same district, and told nobody at the counter because there is nothing at the counter that asks him. **What does resident mean. On that. Not on yours. On the return. It is on the return and it is a word and I have looked at it for a fortnight and it does not say whose word it is, and I am not asking you as a favour, I am asking you because you are the only person in this building who reads the forms, and I have a wife and a boy and I would like to know whether a word is going to be used about us.** And she says the price before she answers, and did not know she was going to until about a second after she had said it, **and that is how it happened: I am going to answer that in a room, out loud, in about four minutes, and there is no charge on the board for it, and I am not going to write it down for you, and here is the price of answering it: a person who asks a question about a form in a room becomes a person somebody has answered, and there is no instrument anywhere in this empire that takes that back off him. I am telling you that now, at the start, before I say the words, because you have a fortnight of not knowing and I am about to take it away, and I would rather you had the price first.** *I have not asked you to take anything away.* **Nobody ever does. Ask.** Then the answer, in about four minutes, without a word of it twice: **A roll of households is not a register of persons. It is a list of doors.** This court keeps one because the water has to know how many houses there are on a bank, **and the fourth column of mine has had no heading over it for nine years and I have not filled it and nobody has ever asked me why, and that is a different fourth column from the one on your return and I am not going to stand here and pretend the two of them are one thing.** **A return like that one goes out to a district, and this empire has about nine hundred of them, and the office sends one to every one, and each one asks the only question this empire knows how to ask a district about a person, which is *where is that person*, and each one comes back in a column with a heading over it with whatever the district had.** A district's roll of households has a household in it. It does not have a person in it, and it certainly has not got a person of twenty in it, and it has not got consent in it, and it has not got a claim in it, **and the form on your sheet says in its own words that the roll does not list rights, holdings or claims.** **So the answer that came back is correct. The answer is a household. The box wanted a person and a household is what the roll had, and the office wrote the household down because the alternative was writing nothing, and a person who writes nothing in a box is a person who has been struck out with a pen and not a rule, and I have seen that on about four hundred of these in nine years and I could not tell you one of the people who did it.** And the word itself: **And the word resident means what it has always meant, which is *on the roll at a door*, and it does not mean living there, it does not mean paying anything, it does not mean anybody has agreed to anything, and a man can be resident on four rolls in four districts in this empire and be on none of them in the sense he means.** *That is not what I asked.* **It is exactly what you asked, and you asked it well, and I would like it noticed that you asked it because you were afraid, and not because a room wanted a record.** She turned the return round on the sill so it was facing him. **Nobody has ever asked one of these in this building. I have been at this window nine years and the only person who has ever asked me what a word on a form means is a man who came in afraid about his own household, and he is not a lawyer and he is not a clerk.** And it is the second time in a hundred and forty years that anybody has asked another person what a word on a form meant, and the first one was a room that wanted a record, and neither of the two of them is a plan. *I would rather not have asked.* **That is the correct answer, and I want to say to you that it is the correct answer and it is also the answer a woman of twenty did not give about four hundred and thirty miles up this river, and she said no in a room with the day on it and it did not work, and you have just said the same thing in about four words and yours is going to work better than hers did, and I would like you to sit with that for a minute before you go.** *I have not got a minute.* **Then take it on the walk back, and understand what you have done, because almost nobody does and it is the whole of the cost. You have not learned a thing about the water. You have become a person who asked a question about a form in a room, and in about four years a man with a copy of the right page can put a line under your name saying that a clerk of this court told him what the word resident meant, and you will not be able to say that you asked about your own household and not about anybody else, because the form does not have a place to put that, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that has a place to put that, and I have looked.** He went. He did not say thank you and she did not want him to, and there is no form anywhere that requires a person to be thanked, and she has never once in nine years asked for it and is not going to start. Then the ordinary thing with the return: a return that is copied goes into the register and a return that cannot be copied goes in a basket under the sill, because it has nothing in it that this court is the person to copy, and the form says so and has said so for about a hundred and forty years, and the basket is a shallow one of wicker about nine inches deep and sits under the sill where everybody who comes to the window can see it. **There are returns in that basket. There have been returns in that basket in every year of the nine. She has never thrown one away. She has never shown one to anybody. She has never told anybody what is in it, and it is nine sheets deep**, and every one of them has that column and every one of them is the only document in the world that says how a box got filled, **and the form that the basket comes from calls them rubbish.** The corridor is about a hundred and forty feet long, the floor of it is wet from about the eighth hour, there is a lamp at the north end that is out, and the door of the back room is on the left about halfway and was open a couple of inches, which it has been every working day for nine years. She went past it. She has been past it about two thousand times. **A person who opens a room nobody has asked her about is a person who has given herself a duty, and a person who has given herself a duty cannot stop.** She has got enough of those in nine years and one of them is the reason she has a paper in a drawer about a winter. She had been in that room once, in the fifth week of the fifth month, on a matter that is somebody else's, and there was nothing in it, and she has said in her own book that she knows what the presses behind her are for, and that a person with a pen at a table can put a name into a column and take a name out of a column, and that there is no instrument anywhere that stops it. And then, to the window, with the register under her arm: **I have been sitting on the answers for nine years, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask me what is in the basket, and if there were one it would not be a clerk of this court, because a clerk of this court is paid and a person who is paid can be told to stop.** The man had gone by then and there was nobody at the sill. She did not show him; she had thought about it while he was standing there, in about nine seconds, and the reason was not caution and she said so to an empty window afterwards, in the ordinary way, at about the tenth hour: **because a clerk who shows a man her basket is a clerk who has given the man a reason to be interested in it, and a man with a reason to be interested in a thing cannot be left alone with it, and about four years from now a person with a copy of the right page is going to come along that corridor and ask me what the basket is, and I am going to have to say a thing about it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person to say *I have had those sheets for nine years and I never asked what they were* without that sentence being the answer to a question he came with.** Then she wrote it in her own book with no office on it, in her own hand, with the day on it: that a return had come, that the column on it was filled in at the other end in advance, that the day on it was the day the column was filled and not the day it was sent, that no space existed on the sheet for the second day, that a man at the window had asked her what the word *resident* meant, that she had answered, and why she had given the answer for nothing. She read it back twice, the second reading slower, and not a third time, and at the bottom she wrote the reason, which is the reason she has written eleven times in nine years and which has not got any better: **A hand of mine is the only hand anybody is going to be able to put side by side in about four years, and I have now done this for the eleventh time and I have not stopped and I am not going to.** Then the return went in the press with the other returns and she copied nothing, because there was nothing in it she was the person to copy, and the fourth column of her own register was where it has been for nine years, which is empty, and she went and stood in the embankment corridor for a minute and a half and did not go into the back room, **and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to be told she has a duty about a room she has never been inside.**

## Chapter 206 — remainder of the entry

He can count and he has a fair hand, **and the book he keeps has no heading at the top of it and a name at the foot of every line of a list of works.** The second season was asked for on the fourth day of the last week of the seventh month and about nineteen people said no again, one after another, out loud, in a bay, in about four minutes, **and every one of the nineteen reasons went in the third column of that bay's own ledger with the day against each one, and the reasons were read out and the names were not, because a name in a room is a thing that can be carried out of a room.** It is now the second week of the tenth month; the season is running; nobody agreed it and nobody signed anything, and the wages of about forty-one people are held against a company whose whole money is four shillings a year. The ordinary things first, on a day: the count of the works, which takes about four minutes and which nobody has ever asked him for and which is the only thing he is paid for; the water, which is at about the third-of-the-way mark and is not coming up to the second week of the tenth month and which he wrote down because he writes everything down; **the bank, which is about four hundred and forty foot long and which he does not have a figure for and which he is not going to invent.** Then he read the third column back, all nineteen, out loud, in the bay, in the ordinary way, the second reading slower, nothing skipped, **and the second reading is his own rule and he has had it since the second week of the fourth month of this year and nobody decided it and that is what a rule is.** Then again on the fourth day of the week, **because a person is entitled to have the reason they gave written down in the place where the no is, and a person is also entitled to hear it said out loud in a room once a season, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that says so, and he has read every form in that store and there is not one page in any of them that says a person has to give you a better reason than *I have to be home*.** **They are still behind that bank, and the reason went in the book in the place where the no is, and the book does not go anywhere, and the bank does not go anywhere, and both of those are the same sentence and I have not been able to improve it since the seventh month.** Then the arithmetic: about forty-one people on this cut, about nineteen of whom have said no to this season with a reason in the right place **and they are owed nothing and they have been asked once and not a second time, and that is correct and I am not going to touch it**; and **about four people in this bay who cannot read a paragraph. They have never been asked once. They were not asked in the first season and they were not asked on the fourth day of the seventh month and they were not asked this week, and there is no column for them and there is not going to be one, and the reason there is not going to be one is that a column for them would be a column somebody would have to fill, and nobody has appointed anybody, and there is no post in this empire whose job it is to be appointed.** *Then ask them.* **I have.** He puts the ledger down and his hand flat on the third column, which is a column of nineteen reasons and a day against every line of it, and the names are in a different book with about nine hundred and forty of them in nine hands over about sixty years and a day against those too. **I have worked out how. That is what I have been doing for nine days instead of counting the works, and I counted the works this morning first, because I am not going to let a thing I have thought about all week get in front of the water. There is a person in this bay who reads.** She reads better than I do, she does it for forty-five pence a day and four days a week, and she is here because she is paid to be here, **and if I asked her to read the fourth column out to those four people, in this bay, on a day with a day on it, then four people who cannot read a paragraph would know what the hold is and who is holding it and what it does to a week's money, and that is the whole of what a person is owed in this matter and I have got it worked out in about nine seconds and it is the correct thing to do and I am not going to do it.** *Why not.* **Because a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop.** That is the whole of the reason and I have said that sentence out loud in this bay and I am saying it again and I am not going to improve it. If she reads a hold to four people then in about four years a man with a copy of the right page can stand in a room and ask her whether she read it, and read it to whom, and on what day, and in what words, and she will not be able to say no to any of it, because she will have done it, and because she was paid, and because a person who is paid is a person who cannot refuse. And I am not going to spend that. Not hers. It is not mine to spend, and it is not the four's, and I have not got the standing to spend it and there is no form anywhere in this empire that would give me the standing and I have looked for one in about nine hundred buildings and there is not one. *So we sit here.* **Nothing. We do nothing and I am going to write down that we did nothing, and the day, and the reason, and I am going to read it back twice, and the second reading is the one I am not going to skip, and I am not going to put a name at the foot of it, because a name at the foot of it makes the four people on that line findable and I am the man who put a roof in a box in the second day of the second week of the fourth month of this year and I have not stopped being that man.** And the finding, in his own hand, with the day on it: *Asked on the second day of the second week of the tenth month of the year after, in this bay, out loud, whether the four persons here who cannot read a paragraph are to be told what the hold is. There is one way to tell them and it costs a person of seventeen who is paid to read, and a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop. Not done. Not this season. The four are not asked. That is the finding and it is mine and I am not going to hand it to anybody. — R. Adley.* **Nobody thanked him. There is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to be thanked, and he has not asked for it, and he is not going to.** Then the rest of it, standing up, at about the seventh hour, because a bay is a room and a room gets a day and he had a list: **a guarantee stands offered on this bank and unanswered, on four hundred and forty foot of it, and about sixty children under sixteen are inside it, and inside a guild's blank list, and inside a rotation they have not been asked about and have been told about none of it. It is not going to be taken up and I have not got the terms of it in my head and I am not going to read them out in a bay at about the seventh hour.** A bill at Lowcross is nineteen pounds three and fourpence and is unpaid and nobody is liable, and there has not been a line for a bridge in that fund in nineteen years, **and I am not paying it and nobody in this bay is paying it and I am not going to stand here and ask any of you for a penny.** A factor's letter of four lines is sitting on a bench in that store since the second week of the third month and it wants the name of a person who may be served and that person's consent, and it says without both it cannot proceed and will not proceed, and the matter will stand against the works. **It is not going to be answered, and every day it is not answered is a day nobody has been entered, and I know what a day of that is worth and I am not going to be the one who spends it.** And: **And the thirty-one of Mosswake are thirty-one.** He said that last one and moved on, and it is another district's figure and it is not his to explain, **and about nine men in that bay do not know whose it is and are not going to be told, because there is no form anywhere in this empire for a book-keeper of a company to explain another district's number to a bay full of men who have never crossed it.** Then, alone in the bay with the ledger open: **There is a foreman of fifty-one in a shed up the road who has put the words of a document in her own book and has refused to put herself in it**, and Rennick Adley heard about that the way everybody on that bank hears about everything, which is from a man who was in a shed, and he has thought about nothing else since the fourth week of the ninth month. **He did not go up the road. He has not gone up the road. He is not going to ask her for the other half of the sentence, and the reason is the one he gave in the seventh month out loud in front of nine men, and the day he asks is the day she stops saying the half she has got, and he has wanted it since the third month of this year and has not asked, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to ask.** Then a carter came into the bay at about the eighth hour, which he does not do. *Are you going to ask me again.* **No. Not until the season comes round, and not by a second person, and not in a letter, and not by anybody but me, and I will come to the gate and I will ask you in the same words I asked you in the seventh month, and you may say the same words back, and if you say something different I will write down what you said and not what you said last time.** *You have not asked me once since the first month, and I have been waiting, and I said in the bay that I wanted it noted that I had been waiting, and you wrote it down and you read it back twice, and you did not argue with me, and I have thought about that every day since.* **I wrote it down. I read it back twice, and the second reading was the slower one, and that is my own rule and nobody decided it, and it came to me off a girl of seventeen who is paid to read and is not paid to think, and that is the whole of where it came from.** *Then write this one down, because I am going up the road for the winter and I am not coming back to this bank for the asking, and I am telling you that as a fact and not as a threat, and I would like it in the place where the no is, and I would like it to say the day.* He wrote it in the third column with the day and read it back twice, the second reading slower. **That is in the place where the no is, and it is a yes and it is a no in the same line, and both of them are in my own hand and neither of them is a name.** *You have never put a name at the foot of anything in that book.* **The same name is at the foot of three lists of works in that book and it is not mine, and a man at a gate who asks to be asked last and is written first has decided once what he wants to hear, and the day he stops being able to hear it is the day somebody in this bay is doing him a kindness he did not ask for, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that would let me do that to him.** The carter put his hat on and went out into the wet and did not say thank you and was not thanked, and the ledger stayed open with nineteen reasons in the third column and a new line under them. The last of it: **The season is still running. About nineteen people are still behind the bank and about four of them in this bay have still never been asked anything, and he has written down on a page in a bay's own ledger, in his own hand, with the day on it, that he decided not to, and he read it back twice, and nobody thanked him, and the girl of seventeen was at the back of that bay until about the eighth hour and then she was not, and nobody spoke to her about the season, and she did not ask, and nobody thanked her and nobody is going to, and she is not going to start doing the rest of it for nothing, and he has not asked her for anything in nine days and is not going to, and the door of the store is open and about four hundred and thirty miles of road is what it costs anybody who wants to come and look at a page in a ledger and there is no fund.**

## Chapter 207 — remainder of the entry

**Two instruments require her in any room he is in and she has never once merged them and he has never once merged them, and a form gets merged by being inconvenient**: the first is a paper made in a form in four days with a day on it, and the second is a watch that does not name him and does not name her and requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be present. About six hundred people in this city could satisfy the watch, **and she has satisfied it every time it has been called for and nobody has ever paid her for it. Both instruments require her and neither of them says anything about her.** The bays have a press in them and a corridor, the press has about six hundred printed slips in it that say the fourth line is not to be completed by this office, and there are about four hundred blanks in a box under the counter four bays along; she has a desk of her own and a hard-backed book with no office on it and her own hand in it in about nine places, and the book is not the court's and has never been in the court. **Read that before it goes. Read it in front of me. And if you have a reason against it, say the reason in a room and out loud and not on a page.** He read the four lines four times, the last standing up, in about a minute and a half, and did not sit down. It is a copy of four definitions, set out in ink on foolscap in a copyist's hand which is hers, with a heading that says what it is and where it came from, **and no date on it, and about four lines under the heading arguing why there is no date**, and it is going about six miles to a household at the end of a lane, in a hand and not in a wrapper, at fourpence a copy, **and nobody is being paid to carry it because the person carrying it is walking it and has agreed to walk it.** *I have a reason.* **I know you have. Give it.** **I gave it in the second month of this year and you said no and it went anyway, and that was the arrangement, and the arrangement is mine and not yours, and I put it in a book on the fourth of the first month at about the seventh hour and told you it was worse than the rule we made. The reason is that this is the first sheet in eleven weeks that says out loud what a household is inside a definition, and it is going to the one household in this matter that has a person in it who cannot read a paragraph, and the woman who keeps that house is hard to find and has been for sixty years and has never once been asked anything and is never going to be, and a sheet is the cheapest way in the world to a house and there is no form that asks a house's leave.** **And I am going to tell you that you are right. That is the arrangement. Say the rest of it.** The rest of it: **the woman who keeps that house is not my mother's sister and is not in this matter and has done nothing that anybody can be shown, and about four years from now a man with a copy of this page can put it on a table in front of her and say *this is about you*, and she will not be able to say one word about it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire in which she can be told first, and that is the whole of the cost and it is a small cost because she is a person who is hard to find and has been for sixty years, and I am not going to pretend it is not a cost just because it is small.** She writes *agreed* and the day under his reason in her own hand. **That is the fifth time the undertaking has been done, and it is the second time you have given no reason against one word, and I am going to say the number out loud because a number a person says can be held to and I would like this one held to. And the rule of the twelfth month of last year has not been used today and I am entering the not-using as a use, because a correct non-use is a use and it is the only kind either of us gets right.** Then the ordinary counter: a man came into the bays at about the ninth hour for a copy of a form at fourpence and she had to serve him and it took about two minutes, **and about four hundred of those blanks are bought and filled in in a year, and the buying is done at the fourth counter four bays along, and nobody at this counter and nobody at that one has ever been asked a question by anybody.** He did not ask her anything, and **she has never once in this matter had a person stand at a counter she was serving and ask her a question, and there are about four hundred of them a year, and the number is not the point.** The point: **this counter is a room. A room gets a day. A person can come up to it, and be served in it, and ask in it, and walk out of it, and there is nothing on the board under the rail and no fee and no form that says what may be asked at it and none that says what may not. It is the only counter in this city where a person can come up, stand in a room with a clerk in it, and the clerk is a person and is not an office. And in the whole of the time this counter has stood nobody has asked at it, and the reason nobody has asked is not that they do not want to know.** It is that there is no post, and a person who wants to know something at a counter does not come to a counter, they come to a counter to buy fourpence worth of paper, **and they would be ashamed to stand there and use up a minute of a clerk's time for nothing.** And, to the counter after he had gone: **I have thought about that for about eleven days, and the honest shape of it is that the room is there and the asking is not, and the two of them have never once been in the same place at the same time, and in the fifth month of this year, in a room with a rail in it, a clerk of thirty-four who works at the fourth counter four bays along was asked one question out loud, and there were about four hundred blanks in a box under that clerk's own counter at the time, and that is the only time in about a hundred and forty years the two of them were in the same place, and it was an accident, and it was because a room wanted a record.** Then the reason she had actually come to the desk with: **I have a fifth version of the sentence you did not finish. I have had it since the third week of the seventh month and I have not said it out loud and I have not written it down and I am not going to write it down, and I am telling you that it exists and that is all I am going to do about it.** **The reason I have not written it is not privacy and it is not the third line.** It is that **a sentence a man has not finished, written down, is a sentence a clerk can read back to him in about four years and ask him whether he meant it, and a man asked whether he meant a thing has to say what he meant, and what he meant is a thing about two people, and there is no third person in this empire who could be made to hold a version of it that is wrong.** And so it goes in nothing, and it stays in the room, and I have said that out loud in a room to you and it is the fifth time and I am not going to improve on it and I am not going to be thanked for it, because I have asked you in advance, in the third week of the seventh month, not to thank me, and you said you were not going to, and there is no form anywhere that requires a person to be thanked and there never has been one in this matter or in any other. *You have the two boxes in your own book. Both of them filled. Both of them the same name.* **They are two boxes and one name in each and they are not the paper and they are not the watch and they are not this. I have not ruled a third and I am not going to, and a person who rules a box for every thing that happens to her in a year has turned a book into a form, and I would not be the first clerk in this city to do that and I would like to be the last.** Then her hand on the sheet, because it was the only object in the room going six miles to somebody's house: **You have been asking me for about nine days who is going to ask the questions on your sheet, and I have been not answering you, and I am going to answer it now in a room and I am not going to write it down afterwards, and both of those are deliberate. It is not you. It is not me either, and that is the part that has taken me the whole of these nine days, because a woman of twenty-four in four bays with a book with no office on it is the cheapest person in this city to send, and I know that, and I have known it since the second month, and nobody has sent me and nobody is going to and I have made sure of that myself and I would like it written down that I made sure of it myself. You cannot be asked, because you have no office and no fund and a bar that came off on the twelfth of the ninth month of last year on a line you wrote yourself, and there is nothing anybody can instruct you about and nothing anybody can pay you for and nothing anybody can thank you for, and that is the reason you are the most dangerous person in this matter and it is also the reason nobody in it can protect you, and I have said both halves out loud in this room and I am not going to soften the second one.** **So it is going to be nobody, and here is the part that is worse than that, and it is a fact and not advice, and my third line forbids advice and my fourth line says a fact is not advice:** a person who asks a question in a room about a form becomes answerable for the answer. Not the person the question is about. The one who asked. A clerk of thirty-four in this city has said the number out loud twice, which is about four years, **and a clerk of thirty-eight in a building four hundred and thirty miles down this river has found a thing she wanted to write down and could not and has said that wanting a form and not finding one is the worst morning she has had at that window since she came.** **And I have said the price of being a party out loud ten times and nobody has thanked me once and I did not expect to be thanked once and I would like it written down that I did not.** And I know exactly what a room does to the person who says a thing in it. **And I am four hundred and thirty miles from that bay, and I am not going to be sent, and nobody is going to ask me, and if anybody in this matter ever asks me to go and ask a question in a room on that bank, I am going to say no, and I am saying now that I will say no.** He did not argue with it, and she watched him not argue with it, and about nine days ago she would have found that flattering and about four hours ago she had worked out that it is the only evidence she has ever had of anybody's judgment and she has not decided what to do with it. **Say one thing for me, and say it in words, and I am not going to write it down and you are not to either, and if either of us writes it down then I will know that one of us is going back to being careful and I would rather have the sentence than the caution.** **There is no post in this empire whose job is to ask. It has now happened twice, and the first time was a room that wanted a record and the second time was a man who was afraid about his own household, and neither of those is anybody's plan, and two is not a plan either, and in about four years there is going to be somebody at a counter who is going to be asked what a word on a form means, and they are going to say the same thing you would say, and I want you to have heard me say it before that happens.** He said it, in about four seconds, flatly, and then asked one question and the question was not about the sheet. *Are you going to be all right.* **No. Nobody in this matter is going to be all right and I would like it noticed that I have never once been the person who said that, and I have said it now, and there are four of them in my rooms and one of them has been there since the first month of the year before last and it has not been sent and it has not been opened, and I have not thought about it and I am not going to start.** Then she carried the four lines down the corridor herself, put them in the hand of a woman who was going to walk them six miles for nothing, did not watch her go, and shut the door, **and it is not the door she does not shut on the third day of the third week of the seventh month of this year and the two are not the same door and she is not going to be told they are by anybody.**

## Chapter 208 — remainder of the entry

**Nobody sent her. There is no form anywhere in this empire for a person who comes on her own account, and she came on her own account, and that is the only instrument she has ever had.** The day: she gets up before it is light because the room has one window and the light comes in sideways off a wall; she does not go out before about the ninth hour because there is nothing open and she has not got the money for the eating places; she goes out at the ninth hour and walks, **and there is about four miles of it she can walk in a day without being asked anything by anybody, and she has got it to a wall at the end of a street that is not long, and she stands at the wall for about an hour and a half, most days, and goes back.** **She cannot do anything else. There is no work in this city for a woman of sixty-three who cannot read a paragraph, and she has looked, and there is no form on which she can write *I cannot read a paragraph* and hand it to somebody, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person to say what they cannot do. There is a form for what a person is. There is not one for what a person cannot do, and that is the whole of why a woman of sixty-three can come into a city in the second month and go to the fourth week of the tenth without one question being put to her that she cannot answer.** The second object a person can hold is in her coat: a copy of nine lines, **in her own hand, which she has not read and cannot read, and which she has not had anybody read to her since a doorway about four miles off the old river road where a man who cuts kindling stood and read the whole of it out loud because it was in her hand and he worked that out from the middle of it and he did not have a form.** She took it out once, in a room above a shop, in the fourth day of the week of that month. **She knows what it says. She wrote it.** Knowing what you wrote and being able to read what you wrote are two different things, and the gap between them is a person's whole life, and about a hundred and forty years of this empire is the width of it. **You can write a thing and not read it back. I have known that since I was about nine. And nobody has ever asked me about it, and nobody is going to, and there is no post.** Then the woman who hires the rooms, about thirty, does about nine rooms on that street, has never once asked Bess Tarrant anything and is not going to, comes up with the day on the slate and puts it against the doorframe the way she puts it against nine doorframes, and says the figure. *I have not got it.* **That is all right, I have got it on the slate.** *Then read me what it says.* The woman stops. It is about four words on a slate, four days and a figure, **and it is not a form and it is not a receipt and there is no office on it and it is scratched on with a piece of chalk because the woman cannot afford ink for nine rooms.** *Four days, and so much.* *Read it again.* **I have just read it.** *You have told me two numbers and a word.* **That is not reading it to me.** That is telling me the answer. I have been in this room since the second month and I have never been able to say the difference out loud to anybody and I have just said it to you, **and you are the first person in this city who has been asked a question by me since the second month, and it is about a chalk mark on a slate.** The woman read it again, slowly, with her thumb under the line, and the two of them stood in a doorway for about a minute and a half. **There, that is what it says. And that is everything, and it took about ninety seconds, and it has taken you ninety seconds and me nothing, and I could have done it in the second month and you have been here ever since.** Then the thing she had worked out in the ninth month, said in the doorway to a woman of about thirty who hires rooms by the day: **Asking works.** That is what I have got wrong since the second month and it has taken me until the fourth week of the tenth month to say it and I am saying it to you because you have read me four words off a slate. It works on a person. It has always worked on a person. **A man stood in a doorway about four miles off this road and read nine lines of my own hand out loud to me and I have not been the same since, and there was no form and no fee and no day at the end of it.** And it has been asked once since the ninth month of this year. I do not know who asked, and I have been told that much and no more, and that is not a conspiracy and it is not a plan either. **It is two accidents about four hundred and thirty miles from each other, in rooms that had nothing to do with one another, and a person who wants to know why nobody asks forms anything has now got the start of the answer and it is that it keeps not happening to the same person.** And: **it does nothing whatever to a piece of paper. You can ask a person until you are hoarse and the paper does not change. And a form cannot be asked at all, and I have been sitting in this room since the second month waiting for a form to change its mind, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask one, and the first time anybody asked one in about a hundred and forty years it was because a room wanted a record and a person happened to be in the room.** *And you have tried it on paper.* **I have tried it on paper since the second month. You cannot put a question to a form. You can only fill it in, and filling it in is what it is for, and there is not a line on any of them, in about nine hundred buildings, that says a person may ask what the words mean, and I have looked at a great many of them since the second month of this year and I cannot read a paragraph of any of them and I have had that looked and read to me and that is a different thing and it does not count.** Then the woman in the room below, about twenty-eight, who comes up at about the tenth hour with a chair she has to carry up two flights and who stays about four times a week. *Read me this.* The woman looks at it and puts it down face down. **No.** *You have not looked at it.* **I have looked at the first line of it. I know what it is. It is a thing you have written for yourself and you want read back to you, and I am not going to be the one who reads it.** *Why not.* **Because you are not paying me and that makes it worse, not better, and I want to be fair with you about that, because everybody says the paid one is the one who can be stopped and it is the unpaid one who cannot say no at all. A woman who is paid to read is a woman somebody can tell to stop, and she can be shown the day she read it. A woman who is asked as a kindness in her own time by a woman of sixty-three she has lived above since the second month is a woman who has said yes in a room with nobody in it but the two of them, and if anybody ever comes and asks me what I read for you, I have to say yes I read it, and I have to say it to a person, and I have no slate and no day and no figure and nothing to point at.** *You have me. You can say that I asked you and that I wanted to know.* **I can say it and it will not help me, because you are sixty-three and cannot read a paragraph and in about four years that is going to be the only thing anybody knows about you, and I am twenty-eight and I will be able to read a paragraph for another forty years, and I would rather not be a thing a person above me was heard asking for.** She sat in the chair for about a quarter of an hour and they did not talk about it again, and when she went down she took the salt and left the chair, which she always does. Then what Bess Tarrant is going to do, told plainly, with the reason: **I am going to go and stand in a room where boxes are filled in. That is the whole of the plan.** There is no more of it than that and I have had since the second month to make a better one and there is not one, and I am sixty-three and I cannot read a paragraph and I have no money and I have a house about four miles off this road that is not shut and that I am the only person who knows is not shut. And I have worked out, this week, why nobody ever came to find me, and it is not the reason everybody thinks. **There is a line about me in a third column with no heading over it four hundred and thirty miles down this river, and it has been there since the sixth year, and my name is not in it and it is not a name, it is a description, and I have been unfindable on purpose, and I have been proud of it, and I have said in rooms that I was the only one of us nobody could find.** And I was wrong, and I was wrong in the most stupid way a person can be wrong. **The machinery does not need me. It needs a person who writes.** There is a woman in an office about nine miles from a counter who has written nineteen years of minutes and about four thousand entries in them and has never been asked what a box on a form is for, and she is the one the whole of this runs through, and she is the easiest person in this empire to find, because she is at a desk every day of her life in a building with a door on it. **So the arrangement did not keep me out of the way of the thing that was going to happen. It kept me out of the way of the only thing that was never going to happen, and being unfindable is the reason I was never asked and it is not the reason I was safe, and I have had those two the wrong way round for nine years and it has taken me until now to turn them over.** *You could have written and asked for the room. I do not let rooms to anybody I have not had a word with.* **Then that is what it is for. It is not for the room.** It is so that a person who cannot read a paragraph is a person somebody has had a word with, and that is the only thing I have wanted since the second month and I could not have said what it was and I have said it now in about ninety seconds on a step.
