It has been here the whole time and it is not a form and it is not a post and it is not a person asking anybody anything, and it costs nothing and nobody can be told off for it, and it has been running for nine years without anybody deciding to.** Then the two decisions she is not going to take: **She is not going to ask the book-keeper whether he is going to keep doing it**, because asking is the only instrument there is and she has watched it cost a man of about thirty-four an afternoon and a woman of twenty about nine miles of walking, **and I am not going to spend either of those on a thing I could hear about in a bay in about four minutes.** And the bigger one: **She is not going to tell the girl of seventeen.** She is on a written engagement, there is a copy in a satchel with a date on it, nobody has ever thanked her and there is no form anywhere that requires it, she reads better than anybody in that bay, she is at the same rate as everybody else on the list, and Halla Wray has known her since she was about eleven and has never once asked her to read anything. And a book-keeper worked out in the second week of the tenth month of the year after that she was the one way of reaching the four, and said so out loud in that bay, and did not do it, and wrote down that he was not going to and read it back twice, **and the reason he gave was that a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop; Halla Wray credited that argument in the second week of the eleventh month without pointing at her, and has not improved it since, and is not going to.** She could have said in about four seconds: *you are the one person here who could read that column to four men*, and it would have been true and correct and ordinary, and no foreman is stopped from saying a correct thing in the ordinary way. **And she is not going to, and the reason is that the moment I say it to her she is a person who has been asked about it, and she is paid, and that is the whole of the argument in one act. He took nine days and a written finding to get to it. I have just got it out of my mouth at about the ninth hour and it took me five weeks and a man with a satchel.** And afterwards, in about four years, a man with a copy of the right page can ask a foreman of fifty-one what she said to a girl of seventeen in a bay, and the foreman will have to say it, and the girl will have to hear that she was asked about it, and neither will be able to say nobody put it in her. **I would rather be the woman in this bay that nobody ever says one word about than the woman who said one word to the only person in it who reads for money. That is not brave and I know it is not brave. It is the same shape as the reason I gave about the four and it is the same bad reason, and I have now got two of them and they are the same one, and a woman with two bad reasons is not a woman with one good one.** Then the ordinary things, in order, because a foreman who stops doing them is a foreman who has begun to make a speech of her own job: the count of the works, the water up about a foot since the twelfth month, the hold against a company whose whole money is four shillings a year and all of it the rent of a building and which has not moved since the fourth week of the fourth month, the factor's letter **not going to be answered and every day it is not answered is a day nobody has been entered**, the nineteen reasons in a column with a day against every one and the names in a different book with about nine hundred and forty of them in nine hands over about sixty years, **a bill at Lowcross at nineteen pounds three and fourpence, unpaid, nobody liable, and no line for a bridge in that fund in nineteen years**, the guarantee standing offered and unanswered on about four hundred and forty foot of bank with about sixty children under sixteen inside it and inside a guild's blank list and inside a rotation they have not been asked about, **and the thirty-one of Mosswake, which is thirty-one, and which is another district's figure and which she said once and moved on from, and about nine men in that bay do not know whose it is and are not going to be told, because there is no form anywhere in this empire for a foreman to explain another district's number to a room full of men who have never crossed it.** Then the other thing: **There is a man in this bay who had a piece of paper read to him by a book-keeper in the first week of the twelfth month of the year after, and nobody wrote a word about it down, and the book-keeper has told me nothing about it and I have asked him nothing, and I know about it because a foreman on a bank knows everything a bay says by about the second hour, and that is the whole of how I know. He is one of the four. He is one of the four and he has had a piece of paper read to him and nothing was written down and he is not going to be asked about the hold this season and neither am I going to ask him about the paper. And I want it said in a bay that both of those are decisions and neither of them is mine.** And: **I am not going to say his name in a room and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a foreman to say a man's name to nine men, and the reason I have just given about the girl of seventeen is the reason I am not going to say his name about the paper, and it is the same reason three times over, and a woman who has found the same reason three times has not been thinking, she has been refusing.** Then the bay, after: a man of about fifty-five was in it for the reading, his work was in it, she read it the way she reads everything, and afterwards he asked her what the day under it meant, because there is a day against every line of a list of works and he has never once asked anybody what a day is for. *I meant whether it is the day it started or the day somebody decided.* **Then somebody decided. Then somebody decided, and you have just worked that out in about nine seconds at about the ninth hour in a bay, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a man to work that out and there never has been one, and I have never once been asked to help anybody work it out before and I have been doing it since I was forty-two.** He said thank you and she has not been thanked in nine years and told him not to, and he said he had not meant it as anything. **And she had done it four times in about a quarter of an hour, on purpose, in a room, to a man who cannot read a paragraph, about a day on a list, and she has not written a word of it down, and she is not going to, and the four of them have still never been asked what the hold is.** And last, at the bench, with the lamp not lit, which is later than it ought to be, with one sheet of her own hand in a book of her own, six numbered lines and a day against it and no name on it, and the notice on the bench with a seal and nothing under the seal, **and the two of them are two sheets and only one of them is in her hand**: **Four askings in about a hundred and forty years and no post anywhere in this empire whose job is to ask**, a court room in the fifth month that wanted a record, a window in the first week of the tenth, a desk in the second week of the eleventh that a woman of twenty walked four miles to, and a rented room in the first week of the first month that the same woman walked nine miles to. **Two of the four were accidents. The third was on purpose. The fourth was on purpose and she did it on her own account and nobody sent her**, and I have not been told her name and I have not asked and I am not going to, and there is no post and there is not going to be one, and it will not happen again by itself. And a book-keeper on her own bank had read a man's own paper to him in a yard and written nothing down, **and she had been answering a man of about forty from behind a bench in about four seconds flat for nine years without being asked, and neither of those is one of the four, and neither of them is a form, and neither of them has anybody's name in it.** **Somebody asks, or nobody asks. That is still the whole of it. And the answer that has come out of this bank since the second week of the eleventh month of the year after is not a person. It is a habit that two people have and neither of them has appointed and neither of them has written down, and I have not decided whether that is better and I am not going to decide it in a bay at about the seventh hour with the lamp off.**

## Chapter 218 — remainder of the entry

**Two instruments require her in any room he is in and they are not one instrument and he does not merge them and she does not let him: the first is a paper made in a form in four days with a day on it, and the second is a watch that does not name him and does not name her and requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be present. About six hundred people in this city could satisfy the watch. She has satisfied it every time it has been called for and nobody has ever paid her for it.** He has a sheet of paper in his hand and wants to put it on her desk and she makes him hold it. *Read it before it goes. Read it in front of me. And if you have a reason against it, say the reason in a room and out loud and not on a page.* He has asked her to come to a counter four bays along and she has said she will not and said why, and he has come anyway. **I want to buy paper.** She puts her pen down. *You have a room with a table in it and about four feet of a wall you do not use, and I have a press in the next bay with about six hundred printed slips in it that say the fourth line is not to be completed by this office, and there are about four hundred blanks in a box under a counter four bays along. A copy is fourpence. A question is a shilling at a notary's table. I have worked out that in about nine hundred buildings in this empire there is a printed form that nobody has ever shown to a person, and that the only instrument anybody has ever found for saying no to a box is somebody asking a question out loud,** and the two have never been in the same room in about a hundred and forty years. **And the reason it has never been done is not money. It is that nobody thinks of a form as a thing you can hold.** A clerk of thirty-four stands behind a slab in this city four bays along with a fee board on it with four things written on it and none of them is time, and those four words are printed, and every person who reads them in a year reads a heading they have never been asked about, **and there is no post in this empire whose business it is to ask what a word on a board means, and the reason there is not is that a board is not a form, and a person does not think of a board as a thing that can be asked a question.** She did not say no straight away and that is on the page. *You know I am not going to be able to do it.* **I do not think you are going to be able to do it either. I have not got any money.** *I am telling you what I want and it is paper and I have not got the price of it, and a price is a small thing to say out loud in a room and it took me about a fortnight to be able to say it.* Then the reason, and she reads the four definitions twice and a third time which he had not asked for: **You are not going to buy anything with this. No. I am going to hold it. That is the entire instrument and there is nothing behind it and there is not going to be anything behind it,** and what he is proposing is that a person should be able to hold a printed form in a hand and not have to ask anybody to read it to them, **and that is a thing about paper and not about anybody's duty, and there is no post in it and there is no fund and there is no form.** *And you have brought it to a clerk of twenty-four.* **I did say that I would not go and ask anybody anything, and I said I would say no, and I am not going to unsay it, and nobody is going to ask me and I have made sure of that myself and I would like it written down that I did. And you have not asked me. You have brought me a piece of paper and a reason and you have stood in a bay and waited for me to say something about it, and that is the correct use of a room and it is the whole of what there is.** *Agreed*, written, with the day on it, in her own hand, the fourth day of the fourth week of the first month of the year after next. Then the count, itemised on a counter in about four minutes, because she is a person who counts and he is a person who counts and there is no form anywhere in this empire for counting anything: **one, a court room in the fifth month of the year after, and the room wanted a record; two, a clerk's window in the first week of the tenth month, and a man was afraid about his own household; three, a desk in the second week of the eleventh month, and a woman of twenty walked about four miles and nobody sent her; four, a rented room in the first week of the first month, and the same woman walked about nine miles and nobody sent her. Four in about a hundred and forty years. Three of them are a year old or more. One of them is a fortnight old. Two of the four were accidents and nobody chose either of them, and the other two were on purpose, and one of the two was done by the same person as the other.** And then: **in the four weeks since the fourth one, nobody has asked anybody anything. Not at this counter. Not at the one four bays along. Not in a bay, not at a window, not in a shed, not in a rented room.** And: **That is not a finding about people. That is a finding about what happens when a thing happens once. It will not happen again by itself. And the reason it did not spread is not that nobody wanted to know. It is that there is no way to want to know a thing you have not heard about, and there is no way to hear about it, and the only way anybody found out about the first three was to be in a room where somebody else was asking.** Then a man comes in for a copy at fourpence and is served in about two minutes and does not ask her anything, and the shape of it: **A room gets a day. A person can come up to a counter, be served in it, ask in it, and walk out of it, and there is nothing on the board under the rail and no fee and no form that says what may be asked at it and none that says what may not. And in the whole of the time this counter has stood nobody has asked at it, and the reason nobody has asked is not that they do not want to know. It is that a person who wants to know something at a counter does not come to a counter. They come to a counter to buy fourpence worth of paper, and they would be ashamed to stand there and use up a minute of a clerk's time for nothing, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that a person can put in front of a clerk saying I have a question and I have not got a shilling, and there is no shilling's worth of nothing in the world.** And: **the reason I have not said anything to any of them is that a clerk of this table is paid, and a person who is paid can be told to stop, and a person who is paid can be shown the day she answered and the words she used and cannot say no to any of it. And I have said that reason out loud on a bank about four hundred and thirty miles down this river and a book-keeper said it before me, and I am not going to say it as though I thought of it, and I am not going to improve it. And that is the answer to who asks, and it is the fourth time this year that the answer has come out the same way, and I am the second person in this matter to have arrived at it and neither of us appointed anybody.** Then, in the room, before the price: **And I am not going to be a room that knows a thing can be asked**, and the rest of it, and the two boxes in her book with the same name in each, **and they are the paper and the watch and they are not one instrument and they are not this piece of paper, and I have not ruled a third and I am not going to. A person who rules a box for every thing that happens to her in a year has turned a book into a form, and I would not be the first clerk in this city to do that and I would like to be the last.** Then the price, standing, because he has not asked her a question at all and that cost more: **I have said the price of being a party out loud ten times and nobody has thanked me once, and I did not expect to be thanked once and I would like it written down that I did not. And there is one thing left and I am going to say it and then I am going to serve the man at the counter who has been standing there for about four minutes, and he has a slip with a reference on the top of it and he is going to pay fourpence and he is not going to ask me anything, and about four hundred of those are bought and filled in in a year and not one of them is a question.** And the debt that is not discharged: **You have never once asked me whether I have decided whether to trust your judgment, and I have never told you that I have not, and both of those are the same silence and it has been going on for a long time, and I am not going to decide it in a bay at about the ninth hour, and the decision is not going to be made in a room and it is not going to be written down. You have now been refused by me in a room in writing twice and you have not argued once, and that is two pieces of evidence, and two is not a decision, and a woman who treats two as a decision is a woman who has been alone too long.** She serves the man, he pays fourpence, she reads his copy out, he says it is right, he goes. **A piece of paper was agreed in a bay and could not be bought, and the undertaking of the fourth of the first month had been done for the sixth time, and the reason was the best one either of them has ever brought her, and it is a reason about paper.**

## Chapter 219 — remainder of the entry

**That was not the same as not asking. That was the board working exactly as it is printed, and the board is printed to be a list of what a thing costs, and a person can stand in front of a list of what a thing costs for about a minute and a half and work out in about nine seconds that nothing on it is free, and then there is no way at all to stand there, and so she goes away, and I have watched about nine people a year do it and not one of them has ever come back and told me why.** And: **And the reason I could not put a free question on that board, and the reason she had to come three times, is the same sentence, and it is the sentence about being identified,** and it took me the second week of the ninth month, the second week of the eleventh, a man of sixty and a knife in a drawer to get to it, **and nobody in this matter has said it out loud yet, and I am not going to be the one who says it in a room where it goes in a book.** Then a woman of about thirty-five asks a plain question about how long a thing stays on the counter, gets four sentences in the plainest words, and says thank you, and is thanked anyway when told there is nothing to thank her for. **Nobody has thanked her once in eleven years at that counter and a woman of thirty-five did it in about four seconds in the first week of this month, and Marn Ottery has not got a rule about that and did not have one about the other thing and is not going to get one now.** Then the man who has been coming for years: **He is not ashamed of buying fourpence worth of paper. He is ashamed of asking a question that costs him a minute of a stranger's time and nothing else.** There is a difference, and the difference is the whole of this counter and the whole of this matter, and she has never in eleven years been able to put it into a sentence a customer could read. At about the tenth hour he asks, unexpectedly, *Are you all right to be asked something*, and she puts the copy down face up. **I am going to answer that in about four minutes and out loud and for nothing, and I am going to say the price first, and I am not going to write any of it down. Here is the price: a person who asks a question about a form in a room becomes a person somebody has answered, and there is no instrument anywhere in this empire that takes that back off him** — and she has said it to two people in this city since the second day of the second week of the ninth month of the year after, and one of them said afterwards that he would rather not have asked, and that was the correct answer. *Have you had this before.* **No. Three times, in about a hundred and forty years, in this empire.** A court room in the fifth month of the year after that wanted a record, a clerk's window in the first week of the tenth, a desk in the second week of the eleventh that a woman of twenty walked about four miles to. **Two of the three were accidents and nobody chose either of them and one was on purpose and nobody sent her, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and it will not happen again by itself.** *Then I had better not.* **That is a decision and it is yours and I am not going to argue with a man of sixty about it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that entitles anybody to be troubled a second time about it, and I am not going to trouble you about it again.** Then the thing she had worked out on the fourth day: **You may ask me anything. Not because of anything that has happened in another county, and not because a woman of twenty asked me a question in the ninth month of the year after and I answered it and could not write it down. Ask me because I have been at this counter eleven years and I have said that sentence to two people since the ninth month, and there has been a hundred and forty years of a board over my head with four things on it and none of them is a question, and I have never once had the nerve to say out loud that a person may stand here and use up a minute of my time for nothing.** *Why is it about you and not about me.* **Because a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop, and a person who is paid to answer is the same thing with a different word on it.** I have been paid to answer for eleven years and have never been asked to, have said the price every time and answered anyway, **and I would like it said somewhere that a clerk of this table is the cheapest person in this city to send and nobody has ever sent me.** And: **I have said that I would not go and ask anybody anything, and I said it in a room to a woman of twenty-four in the third week of the tenth month of the year after, and I am not going to unsay it, and nobody is going to ask me, and I have made sure of that myself. So it is about me, and I am not going to be a post, and there is no post, and a person who appoints somebody to a post that does not exist is doing the thing the whole of this is about, which is filling in a box the way the box is printed.** Then he looks at the board. *Is there anything on that board about a question.* No. **Then a man of sixty has to be told he may ask, out loud, by a clerk, once, before he dares, and he has to be told it by a person and not by a slip, and there is nothing at that counter to point at. That is the whole of the finding and I have arrived at it at about the fourth minute past ten of a day in the first week of this month —** He stopped, and said he had not been here today, and put his hat on, and did not say thank you, and was not thanked, and went. **And he did not ask her a single question. She had told him in about nine sentences that he could, and he had used none of it,** and he had found the shape of the whole thing out loud in a room in about four minutes, and she has thought about that for a fortnight and is not going to improve on it. Then the decision. She takes a blank out of the box and looks at the printed strip along the top, **and it takes about four seconds to cut a line into it with a knife, and she has the knife in the drawer because it is the same knife she uses for the box. She did not cut it.** **A question is not going on the board.** Not because I have not been asked one since the ninth month, and not because it is a good idea, and not because it would cost nothing, and not because there is no post, though there is no post. A person who is charged for asking will not ask, and that took about a fortnight; a shilling is on that board for a question at a notary's table and there are about nine hundred of those tables in this empire, and if the asking is on the board here then it is on the boards of all of them, and then a man with a wife and a boy stands at a rail and does the sum, and the sum is that he cannot afford to be identified. **A free answer is the only thing in this empire that leaves nobody findable.** The moment there is a price on it, the person who pays has a receipt, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a receipt about a question. The blank goes back in the box, and the box is under the counter, **and the counter is the only place in this city where a person can come up, ask a question, and walk out with nothing.** And then the second half, said to an empty counter: **A board is not a form and that is the whole reason nobody asks at it.** A form is a thing that happens to a person, printed in about nine hundred buildings, filled in by somebody else, with a box on it a person can be put in; a board is a list of what a thing costs, and a list of what a thing costs is the friendliest piece of furniture in this empire, and it is the reason a person can stand at a rail for a minute and a half and not be ashamed, and if I put a question on it I turn the friendliest thing in this empire into the thing a woman of twenty stood in front of three times in the ninth month of the year after and could not use. **And there is a line on the way to that which I have not seen anybody take, and I am not taking it now, and I want it said that I know what it is and that it is not a form and that it is not a post and that nobody sent me to think about it. A clerk of a notary's table can stand up in a public room and say, once, in front of about nine people, that a person may come and ask a question, and about nine hundred clerks can do that, and none of them has, and the reason none of them has is that saying it in a room is a thing a room writes down. So it goes on the counter and not in a room. That is the whole of where it goes.** And: **a question of mine that is free is the only thing I have that the office of a seat does not have, and I am not going to put a price on it and I am not going to write it down,** and I am not going to let a person in a room about four hundred and thirty miles from this counter decide that I should. **That is the decision, and it is mine, and it is a decision not to do a thing that would help, and I would like somebody to put that on a slate and read it back to me, and there is nobody here who is going to, and there is no form.** A man of about fifty comes in at about the eleventh hour and pays fourpence and goes. **The board over her head had four things on it and none of them was time, and none of them was a question, and she was not going to make it one, and a man of sixty had been in a room and had been told in nine sentences that he might ask and had not asked, and about four hundred blanks sat under her hand in a box, and a woman of twenty in a city about four hundred and thirty miles up this river had a book of eleven houses in it that nobody paid her to keep, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and the count is four, and it will not happen again by itself.**

## Chapter 220 — remainder of the entry

Then somebody decided,** and neither of them made anything else of it and about nine men in that bay did not hear it at all. **That is the fifth time in nine years. She has done it since she was forty-two and nobody asked her to and nobody has ever written it down, and she is the only person in this empire who knows it,** and a book-keeper of thirty-eight saw her do it in the fourth week of the twelfth month of the year after and did not understand it for about two hours, and has never been told that she knows. Then the count, said standing, with about nine men in the bay and one of the about four in it: *I told a book-keeper's face in a shed in the last week of the eleventh month of the year after that nobody had ever asked me what the words on a form mean, and it was true, and it was the only true thing I had said in a year.* **And a woman of sixty-three stood in a room in this city in the second week of the twelfth month of the year after and gave a clerk her own name out loud, and nobody sent her, and a clerk of about fifty-five said no when she was asked to go and look for her, and did not look, and was right, and has not said so to anybody.** And a man of about thirty-four stood at a window in the third week of the twelfth month and had the price said to him a second time and heard it the second time. And a woman of twenty walked about nine miles in the first week of the first month of the year after next and asked a man with no office a question out loud in a room because she had decided to and nobody sent her. **Four. A court room in the fifth month of the year after, a window in the first week of the tenth, a desk in the second week of the eleventh, and a rented room in the first week of the first month. Four in about a hundred and forty years and there is no post anywhere in this empire whose job is to ask,** and two of the four were accidents and nobody chose either of them, and the fourth was the first one anybody did on purpose, and the count has not moved since, and nothing has happened since, and it will not happen again by itself. **So the answer to who asks is that it is nobody's business, and there are four of them, and they were all found by accident or by a woman who decided on her own account, and not one of them was sent, and it will not happen again by itself.** Then it, out loud, in a room, without listing the five: **I am the person in this whole matter who has the most reason in the world to be asked about a thing. I have been in a box since the first week of the seventh month of the year after. I was not asked. And the only reason nobody has ever come and asked me is that there is no form in this empire with a space in it for a woman of fifty-one on a bank, and there is no post, and no way to serve me,** and no way for a person to stand in front of me and say the words out loud. **And I have been glad of that. I have been glad of it for about eleven weeks and I have not admitted it to anybody, and I am admitting it now because a man of about sixty in another city has just worked out that a person has to be told out loud, by a clerk, once, that they may ask, and he worked it out in about four minutes standing at a counter, and nobody told him, and he is not a lawyer and he is not a party and he is sixty.** And the decision: **And I have decided that I am not going to make myself easier to ask. That is the decision, and it is mine, and I made it in about four seconds, and I have not told anybody until now, and I am not going to be thanked for it and I am not going to be argued with, and I would like it read back to me by somebody in a room and there is nobody in this empire who is going to, and there is no form.** *Why not.* **Because of the same reason as the four, and worse, because the four cannot read a paragraph and I can, and if a person stands in front of me and asks me what the third line means then I am a woman who has been asked, and then I am a woman who has been answered, and there is no instrument anywhere in this empire that takes that back off me either. I would be found. That is the whole of it. I have spent forty-two years being unfindable and I said in this shed in the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after that I would like to be found, and I meant it,** and I am going to hold both of those at once for the rest of my life and it is not going to be comfortable and it is not going to be a rule. **And a foreman who has been found is a foreman somebody can be asked about the four, and I have already given one reason for not asking them and it was a bad one, and I gave a second one about a girl of seventeen in the third week of the first month and it was the same bad reason twice, and I am not going to hand a third one to a person in a room who writes it down.** Then she says it before anybody can: **And it is the wrong decision, and I know exactly what the right one looked like, and it was a woman of twenty walking about four miles to a desk in the second week of the eleventh month of the year after and asking how the box got filled.** Nobody sent her and nobody told her to and there is no form for it and she did it on her own account and nobody appointed her, and she is the only reason I know what I am, and I am not going to be able to say that to anybody else. **And I have not asked her. She has not come to this bay. About four hundred and thirty miles of road is what it costs anybody who wants to come and see a page in a ledger, and there is no fund and no form for a fund, and I have said out loud that I am not going to be the reason four other people are found, and that still stands and nobody has talked me out of it.** And I have now also decided not to be found myself. **A woman of fifty-one in a shed in the fourth week of the second month of the year after next has arrived at two bad reasons and one wrong decision, and she is going to have to live in the room where all three of them are.** Then the ordinary thing again: a man of about fifty-five comes in with a chain and asks about the third link, and it takes about two minutes, and every man in that shed watched a foreman do a perfectly ordinary thing to a man who cannot read a paragraph. **And she did not ask him.** Not about the hold, not about the season, not about the two sheets on the bench with a seal and nothing under the seal. And a book-keeper had read a piece of that man's own paper to him in a yard in the first week of the twelfth month of the year after and had written nothing down and had told nobody, and she knew, and she was not going to ask him about it, and the reason is the same reason she was not going to ask the book-keeper in the third week of the first month of the year after next: **asking is the only instrument there is, and I am not going to spend it on a thing I could hear about in a bay in about four minutes, and I have not spent it once in about nine years and neither has anybody else on that bank.** Then a scene at the seventh hour: a man of about fifty from the fourth gang wants to cross to Lowcross and the bridge is shut and has been shut since before the water came in and *that is not a reason the bridge knows about*, and he has a reason and it is that he has not been over it in about three weeks and there is a thing at the far end he wants, and **that is not a reason and I am not going to ask you for another one, and there is no form in this empire that requires a man to give a better reason than I want to get over there, and I have read every form in that store and there is not one page in any of them that says a person has to give you a better reason than that.** *That is the reason I am still here. You have said that in this shed as many times this year as there are weeks in the season and I have counted them and I have never been asked to count them out loud and I am not going to be.* **Nobody is going to ask you to count them out loud. If they do, there is no form for it and you do not have to help them.** And then, from a bench in a hired stone store off the Slade, **the third column of that ledger has nineteen reasons in it with a day against every one of them, and one of the nineteen is a man who wanted to get over a bridge, and the reason is in the book, and it is the same reason he gave in the seventh month, and nobody has asked him again and nobody is going to. A bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line against it in nineteen years and a foreman who will not ask a man for a better reason than he has got, and those two facts have been standing on that bank since before the woman of twenty was born and neither of them is going to be paid and neither of them is going to be forgiven,** and most of the men in that bay know both of them and about nine hundred yards away there is a woman of twenty with a wage held about four hundred and thirty miles from her own door. Then the girl of seventeen at the back of the bay on her fourth day, on a written engagement, at the same rate as everybody else on the list, not thanked, did not ask, not going to start, nobody spoke to her about the season, **and the foreman of fifty-one looked at the back of her bay twice in about four minutes and did not go over.** And the close: **And the whole of the second season and everything that came after it is on a bank about four hundred and thirty miles down this river, and about four people in a bay cannot read a paragraph,** and a reader of seventeen is the one person on that bank who is the wrong person to ask and the only one who is free to answer, and nobody sent anybody to her and nobody is going to, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and the count is four, and a foreman of fifty-one has just said out loud in a room that she is going to make herself harder to find, and the lamp is not lit yet, and that is the end of the movement and it is not the end of anything.

## The Volume 05 Batch 0002 review repair, and the nine summaries above it

**The word counts in the headings for Chapters 212 to 220 are the post-repair measurements, taken with `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w` after every chapter-level edit was finished. Nine of the ten changed, by at most forty-five words and by as little as one.** Chapter 211 is unchanged at 3,116 because the repair did not touch it. **The batch total is 29,517 and Volume 05 stands at 58,307 across twenty chapters.** No summary above this line is rewritten and no chapter's situation, decision or ending changed.

**Four of the corrections change what a summary ought to say, and are recorded here because the summaries themselves were written before the repair and are not restated:**

- **The elapsed figure for the fourth asking is now consistent across Chapters 213, 216, 218, 219 and 220** — about nine weeks, nine days, three weeks, the same morning, and about seven weeks respectively — and **Chapters 216 and 218 no longer say the first three askings are a year old or more, because no anchor supports it.** The first three are all in the year before the fourth. The summaries above still carry whatever figure they carried; **the figures on the page are the ones that govern.**
- **Chapter 219's summary reflects a count that was wrong on the page and is now four** in a clerk's mouth, matching its own closing paragraph.
- **Chapter 218's summary reflects two hand-ruled boxes that were merged with the paper and the watch and are now separate from both**, which is what Volume 04's record and the Batch 0003 lock both require.
- **Chapter 213's summary reflects a rule that had been given to the reader of seventeen and is now given to the book-keeper who wrote it down in his own hand in Chapter 0211**, which is where the rule is made on the page.

## Chapters 221–230, Volume 05, Batch 0003, "The Clause, And The Office That Is Not A Person" (3,011 / 2,758 / 2,714 / 2,956 / 3,018 / 3,164 / 2,763 / 2,754 / 2,919 / 2,975 = 29,032 as repaired, 28,577 as first written)

**The first week of the third month of the year after next to the fourth week of the fifth month of the year after next. Three findings and one new instrument that leaves no mark, and the count of askings does not move, and the two halves of the volume's own question come apart on the page and neither half is answered. Ten new named figures: none.**

## Chapter 221 — remainder of the entry

A window about two feet wide with no pane in it. A tray goes up a corridor and comes down again. **She writes the Cinder Clause into the sixth book for the second time in the manuscript, with the day against it and her own name at the foot of it, and the whole of it takes about nine seconds, and she counts them and has never told anybody she counts them.** A question was put to a court in this city about nine years ago in a room with a number on the door, the court is a court of this empire, it still exists, it sits on the same ground, the question was about a clause, the clause has a name, **and the name is written in about nine hundred documents in this empire and is not on any of the boards in this city.** Then she says it out loud to the room, because a room is the only kind of place in this empire where that can be said: **a name written down is not an answer, and a name on a question is the only protection a question has ever had in this empire**, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to answer one. Then the list, made as a list because that is what she is, of **what a clause is not**: not a question, not a person, not an answer. It does not ask anybody anything; nobody can be asked to keep one; it cannot be asked to explain itself; it cannot be refused anything; there is no form in this empire for serving a thing that was agreed before anybody in the room was born. A carrier of about thirty comes in with a tray, puts it on the table, which he is not supposed to do, and asks whether that is the office. **She says no: there is no office in this building and there never has been one, and the eleven things on that shelf are about one and none of the eleven is it.** He asks what is in the building, and she says the price of answering first, because she has said it in this room twice and both times it was too late: **a document that names an office is a document and not an office** — one side of a sheet of good paper, not printed, a seat's own device cut into the head of it, set out in six numbered lines and a foot, **the second asking that no inference of any kind be drawn from the absence of any name and the sixth declining to name whoever holds the office at all, every one of the six about a room** — and there is a second paper of the same office with a name at the foot of it, **and I am not going to say that name in this room, and you did not ask me for it, and I would like it noticed in this room that you did not ask me for it.** He asks whether she has written a question down twice, and she says she has written the day against it twice and the thing itself once, and the reason she wrote the day twice is that **a question said in a room with nine people in it is a question with nine people standing in the way of it.** Then the second thing she made him ask: what a line in one of these is. She opens the second book, reads one entry out loud because he cannot read, and says it is a day, a room, a thing a room decided and a clerk's name, and that a minute book is a record of what a room decided and not a register of people. **Both of his questions are plain questions and not questions about a word on a form, and the count of plain questions is a different count and is not printed anywhere; the count of the other kind is four, and it has been four since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, and nobody has asked anybody anything since, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and I am not going to be one and neither are you.** Then the wall, standing up, because he cannot leave without the tray: **there is a question on the record that nobody is ever going to be able to answer, because the only paper that would bear on it does not exist in a form a person can hold, and an office is not a person and cannot be served and cannot be made to stand in a room, so there is nobody who could be asked whether that office gave an order — and it is not unanswered because it is hard, it is unanswered because the thing that would answer it cannot be produced at all, and that is a stronger wall than a document nobody has read.** A document nobody has read can be read next week by anybody who is patient; a document that cannot be produced can be produced next month or next year or never, and there is nobody in between to hurry it, **and the same machinery that made the two of you is the machinery that made the absence, and it makes both in about nine seconds and it has never once asked anybody.** And about nine hundred buildings have a form for asking; not one has a form for waiting; nobody has ever been appointed to the waiting; and that is the whole of what happened on that day, and it was nine seconds and a tray. **The review repair added the last beat of the chapter and it is the batch's only appearance of Marek Kest in it: the carrier puts the tray down a second time and says she has names in that book, and she answers that she has about nine hundred and forty on a shelf and has never once asked about one of them, and that there is a man of thirty-eight in this city called Marek Kest whose name is not on any of them and never has been, and nobody has ever come to that room and asked her to look for him, and that if she were told he had stood in it she should be surprised and should want to know what day.** Nothing was cut to make room for it and no question was asked in it, so the count of askings is four before and after.

## Chapter 222 — remainder of the entry

**Two instruments require her in any room he is in and they are not one instrument and a form gets merged by being inconvenient: the first is a paper made in a form in four days with a day on it, the second is a watch that does not name him and does not name her and requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be present; about six hundred people in this city could satisfy the watch, she has satisfied it every time, and nobody has ever paid her for it.** He comes at about the ninth hour with nothing in his hands. He has been five times since the first month and has not asked her anything, and she says she has a reason for every one of the five and has not asked him for any of them. Then she gives the shape of hers: **the only question worth asking in this empire is whether an office can be made to account for a thing it did in the fifth month of the year after, and if she asked it in this bay he would have to answer it, and answering it would make him a person somebody has answered, and in about four years a man with a copy of the right page could put a line under his name beside a sheet with a clerk's name at the foot of it** — and that would be the second time somebody in this matter was found by a thing they said in a room, the first being a woman of twenty with four lines at the back of a book of eleven houses that nobody pays her to keep, who does not know that Tamsin Rook knows about them. **And I am not going to be the reason there are two.** And the finding of the batch's shape, in her mouth: **he is the only person in this matter who is answerable and cannot be found, and the reason he cannot be found is that nobody was ever going to have to look him up, and that is the same sentence as the reason the office of a seat cannot count its own entries, and the reason a man is safe and the reason a room is helpless are the same sentence and neither of them was designed.** Then the non-use, which is entered: **he declines to put the choice in writing and asks her to enter the not-putting, with the day on it, in her own hand, in a book with no office on it**, and she writes it, and it is on the page:

> *The fourth day of the second week of the third month of the year after next. A choice was not put in writing and no question was asked, at about the ninth hour, in a bay, by the person it would have been put to, and the reason given out loud, and no form anywhere in this empire requires a not-doing to be entered. — M.K.*

He reads it twice, and she says the second reading is always slower and that it has found a thing she has got wrong about four times in two years and she has never told him about those. Then a man comes for a copy at fourpence and is served in two minutes and does not ask her anything, and the shape of it: **a room gets a day**, and there is nothing on the board under the rail and no fee and no form that says what may be asked at it. Then the count, and it does not move: four, the first three dated, the third the first one anybody did on purpose, the fourth by the same woman on purpose again, **and in the ten weeks since the fourth nobody has asked anybody anything, and it is not a finding about people, it is a finding about what happens when a thing happens once, and there is no way to want to know a thing you have not heard about and there is no way to hear about it.** And about nine people a year at that counter want to ask her something and not one of them opens their mouth, and she has said the reason out loud four times now, once in the ninth month of the year after and once in the eleventh and once in the first month of the year after next and once in the second, and has not improved it once. Then **Marn Ottery comes in for a form her press does not have**, four bays away, and Tamsin Rook cannot let it go because the line under it is not to be completed by this office, and Marn Ottery says this is the first time in eleven years she has come for nothing and she is not going to be paid for it and not going to be thanked, and has read the same four lines and got the same answer, and **there is no fund in this city for either of us and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a fund.** The two of them stand at a counter for about four minutes and say nothing. Then Marn Ottery says a carrier told her in a street that a clerk of about fifty-five said out loud that a question written down is not an answer. **Then it is in a street, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that puts a street in a book, and that is the first time in this matter that a thing has got out of a room for nothing and landed in a person who is not paid to carry it**, and she has not got a rule about that and is not going to make one at about the tenth hour. Then Marn goes, having said nothing at all through the whole of it, and the bay is quiet for about nine minutes. He did not ask her either. **The two of them have built a second instrument in about nine weeks and neither appointed the other and neither wrote it down, and I am not going to say what it is called, because whatever it is called it will get a form inside four years and then it will be a machine and it will need a person to be told they may come.** And the two boxes in her own book with the same name in each are not the paper and not the watch, and she has not ruled a third and is not going to, and a person who rules a box for everything that happens to her has turned her own book into a form, and she would like to be the last. And the debt: **he has never once asked her whether she has decided and she has never told him that she has not, both are the same silence, and neither of them is going to be disappointed about that, including her, because two pieces of evidence is not a decision and three is not either and she is not going to be the one who says it is.**

## Chapter 223 — remainder of the entry

He reads the four definitions out loud for the first time since the second month of the year after, to nobody. **The sheet is one side of a sheet of good paper in a fair hand, in ink, on foolscap, and it came into his hands in the second month of the year after and it is the only document in this matter he has never wanted to give back.** The third of the four turns on a roll of households. Then he does the thing he has been putting off since the seventh month of the year after: he reads all four looking for one word. **There is not one. Not *holder*. Not *office*. Not *person*. Not *seat*.** A person of that family who is of age and resident; a person who may be entered; a person who is not to be entered; and not one of the four definitions has a word in it for a person who holds an office and does a thing in the name of it. He wrote that down in the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after, in one line, and read it back, and it is the eleventh line of a book with no office on it, **and it has not been a question since the fourth week of the first month of the year after next.** Out loud to the table: **a document is the only thing anybody can be held to, the absence is in a document, a thing that is not in a document cannot be held to anybody at all, so the person who holds the office cannot be held to anything and the office cannot be held to anything either, because an office is not a person and the four definitions are the document and the office is not in them, and that is not an oversight, that is a hole somebody cut, and I have known that since the seventh month and I have not been able to find the hand.** Then the twelve lines: eleven with something on them and a twelfth with nothing at all, and no form anywhere in this empire requires a man to fill a twelfth line and none requires him to leave it empty, **and that is the whole of the difference between the two things and nobody in this matter can pay for it**, and a fact on a line in a book with no office on it is the worst of the two, because a question can be answered and a fact can only be repeated. Then he takes out a pencil, which he has not done in this room since the second month of the year after, and puts the point on a clean sheet of paper after the twelve lines — **there is no form anywhere in this empire for a man to be issued with a clean sheet of paper, and he has had this one in a drawer since the fourth week of the first month of the year after next, and it is the only paper in that room that is his** — and he does not write the word a clerk of about fifty-five said once, in a room with a rail in it, in the first week of the fifth month of the year after, and then stopped, and would not describe the hand and would not say which of two papers of the same office it came from. He says **no**, and then says why, to the table, about four times: **nine hundred documents carry that word and not one of them is mine, and a man with a copy of the right page can have that word out of any of them in about a minute, and if it is also in my hand then there is a thing in this empire that has my name near it, and in about four years there is a person who can put the two side by side and ask me what I meant by it** — and then, cut back to one sentence and left plain: **a man who cannot be found must not try to be found by writing down the name of a thing**, and that is the reason nobody in this matter is going to be. He burns the clean sheet in the stove in about four seconds and the twelve lines are the only lines in this matter with anything on them. Then a knock, and it is Tamsin Rook, and she does not open the door. She will not come in and gives the reason: **a room you are in and a person who is in it is a party, and the two of us in a room is the instrument, and there is no form that says what a room is for.** She has been at the counter thinking about the carrier and the street, and says she is not going to be the one who starts counting, **because the first person who counts is the first person who has made it a thing that can be asked about, and every one of them ended up with a heading over it**, and she counts how many times she has not let him argue: three, and the counting is hers, and she is not going to be thanked for it. He asks her one plain question through the door, and says if it is about a word on a form he will shut up. She says his own sentence back to him and is using it correctly, **and if you use it on everything then in about four years you will have a book that is a form and no office on it, and that is the worst thing that can happen to a man with nothing, because a form is a machine and a machine needs somebody to be told they may use it.** Then the decision that is still owed, from the stair, four seconds of it: **you have never once asked me whether I have decided and you are not going to now and I am not going to tell you, and that is correct, and it is the third time we have both not done it, and two pieces of evidence is not a decision and three is not either, and there is a clerk in this city who has been told something true once and has been carrying the price of it since the first week of the tenth month, and being told a third true thing would not make her any safer, and I have watched that arithmetic get done twice in this matter by a person who was pleased with it and I am not going to do it a third time.** The door stays as it was. He stands with the four definitions on the table and counts, because he is a person who counts: **four in about a hundred and forty years with the day against each**, a court room in the fifth month of the year after that wanted a record, a window in the first week of the tenth by a man afraid about his own household, a desk in the second week of the eleventh by a woman of twenty who walked about four miles, a rented room in the first week of the first month of the year after next by the same woman who walked about nine miles in one day. Two were accidents; the third was the first on purpose; the fourth was the same person on purpose again; and **in the ten weeks since, nobody has asked anybody anything**, and a clerk of about fifty-five has put a name on the question itself in about nine seconds and a man with a tray has carried it into a street for nothing, and neither of those is an asking, and the count is still four.

## Chapter 224 — remainder of the entry

She can write and cannot read a paragraph. She has a copy of nine lines in her own hand in her coat that she has had read to her once, in a doorway, in a lane about four hundred and thirty miles up this river, by a man who cuts kindling, and there was no form and no fee and no day at the end of it. **Nobody sent her, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person who comes on her own account.** The woman of about thirty-five who keeps the rooming house has a book on the shelf with the day against every name in it, and she is not unkind, and Bess Tarrant has watched her be kind to about nine people for no reason and knows what it is when it is on the way to becoming a document. The woman says she has put her down for the day after as well, and that it is a day she is in rather than a day anybody has paid for, and that she has never been able to write the difference down so anybody could tell. Then Bess Tarrant asks, standing, and says the price first and says it is a plain question: **has anybody ever come to that book.** Nobody ever has. It is a book of who is in which room and on which days and what they owe, and about nine people a day go up those stairs and not one has asked what it is for, and there is no form in that building or in that city that says a person may ask. And Bess Tarrant tells her that **somebody has to have told her that a name in a book does not go looking** — a woman of about fifty-five in a room off the old river road said it on the second day of the second week of the twelfth month of the year after, and said it because Bess asked her to go and look for her in a book of about nine hundred and forty names, and she said no, and she was right. Then the finding, in nine sentences: **a name in a street does not go looking either, and a name in a street is not a name in a book, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that puts a street in a book, and a person who says their own name in a street cannot be produced, because there is no form that says a person must produce themselves on being asked** — the first thing she has found out in about three weeks, found out in a room above a shop, with no use for it. And the other half, to a woman of thirty-five with a book on a shelf: **you have been keeping a book that cannot hurt anybody until somebody who is paid to look for people decides to look in it, and there is one such person in this empire and she is about fifty-five and she has already refused once, and I am telling you because you are thirty-five and nobody has ever told you what a book is, and I was sixty-three before anybody told me and I did not have anybody to tell me.** Then the woman asks, and it is not about a form: are you all right. Bess Tarrant says she has been asked that once in about a year by a woman of twenty-four in a bay, through a door, and did not answer it then. **I am going to answer it one day in about four years and I am going to say I did not know, and it will be true, and that is the whole of the protection I have got and it has never once worked.** And she touches the copy in her coat: **there is a day in a minute book in that building with her name at the foot of the line, she has found out nothing about it, she is not going to, and if a person asks her what it is for she will say she does not know and it will be true and she will be sixty-seven and they will write it down and it will be the first thing anybody has written about her that is not a lie.** Then she goes down at about the fifth hour, buys nothing she needs, comes back up the street, stands at the end of it the way she stands at a wall most days, **and says her own name out loud. Three times. To nobody. About nine people go past and not one turns round, and one of them is a man going to a counter with four words in his head and fourpence in his hand, and he did not hear it, and he will not have heard it, and that is the whole of what happened. She did it on purpose, and nobody sent her, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person who says their own name in a street.** Then she stands there about an hour and a half and works out what she has done: **she has been unfindable for nine years and has had that the wrong way round for most of them, and being unfindable is not why she was safe and is the reason she was never asked, and the machinery does not need her, it needs a person who writes** — and **a person who says their own name in a street is findable, and being findable is the one thing that has never once worked in her favour, and she has done it anyway, on purpose, three times, to nobody, and there is not one form in this empire that can tell her she was wrong to.** Not for the asking, and not because anybody asked, and **because a woman of about fifty-five has written nineteen years of minutes about a thing she has never been in a room with, and a man of about thirty-four bought a copy at fourpence and went home, and a woman of twenty has been in a box since the fifth month of the year after and does not know she is in it, and not one of them can be found by a thing anybody in this city could go and look for, and every one of them found out what had happened because a person said something out loud to them. Not to a book. To them.** Then she counts the four out loud to the wall, with the dates, and says **that is four and none of them is me, and I said my name in a room in the second week of the twelfth month and that is not one of them and it is not one of the count, because a name said in a room is not a question about a word on a form and I have not asked anybody anything about anything. I have taken things. That is all I have done for about a year and it is the first useful thing I have ever done and it has cost me a coat.** Then upstairs, and the woman of about thirty-five has left the book on the shelf and a lamp on, and Bess Tarrant looks at the book for about four minutes and does not touch it, and then sits down and writes a letter — **she cannot read a paragraph, she can write, and her hand is in a copy of nine lines in her own coat that she has never been able to read, and the hand is the same hand, and she knows it is the same hand because a man who cuts kindling read it to her twice and said so the second time.** The letter tells the woman of thirty-five that there is a date four years off in a minute book in a building off the old river road, that it is not hers, that she is not going to find out, that the woman of about fifty-five was right not to look for anybody and would be right to look for one person, and that the one person is her. **She put the day against it in her own hand and read it back to herself out loud, once, and it is nine words and she got all of them right the first time.** Then she puts it in her coat with the copy and does not take it down to the shop and does not give it to the woman of about thirty-five, **and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a woman of sixty-three to take a piece of paper down a street**, and there are about three weeks before the lamp is a thing she cannot afford. **The review repair added four lines to the letter and they are the batch's second appearance of Marek Kest by name: the first of the four is his name, the second is that the woman of about fifty-five has never looked for him and never will, the third is that a woman of sixty-three who cannot read a paragraph is saying that name to a woman with a book on purpose, and the fourth is that she would rather the woman of about thirty-five did not write it down and is going to say so in the room as well on her way down.** She writes it out of her own head in the hand that has not changed since a copy of nine lines, and the chapter's point is unchanged, which is that saying a name out loud is not the same as being found. **The repair also cut the first sentence in two, because it mixed a past-tense action and a present-tense description in one breath, and it changed the opening paragraph's third clause from *and Bess Tarrant has watched her be kind* to *and the woman who rents the room has watched her be kind*, because a chapter should not name its own point of view in narration about a different character one paragraph before identifying her.**

## Chapter 225 — remainder of the entry

The court keeps a roll of households of the district for the water, and **the roll says a household is at a door, and that is all it has ever said, and it is on the face of the form.** A basket about nine inches deep under the sill, a corridor behind the window going away about four hundred yards with four presses along it, about nine people past the window in an hour and not one has ever asked her anything. The man of about thirty-four comes a third time: four words, a reference, fourpence, a copy on a sheet of press in about a minute and a half, read out, it is right. Then he does not go. **He has moved again, out of the district, over the boundary, a street and a river and a different water office, and the woman at the door told him inside a week that the water had changed and that he would want to know what the water wanted, and he did not go and ask her, because he has been to a window about four times since the first week of the tenth month and has never once got an answer out of a room, and has got a great deal out of a window.** She says the price first, slowly, for the third time, and then he says he is not going to ask her about a word on a form. And the answer: **a man in two districts is a man in two districts, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that says a person may only be in one.** His household is at a door, and was at a door on a street with a shop on it, and is at a door over the boundary, and both doors are real and both have people sleeping at them, and this court's roll has one and the other court's roll has the other, **and the two rolls are in two buildings in two districts with a street and a river between them, and there is nothing anywhere in this empire that joins them.** About nine hundred districts, the office sends one return to every one, a return goes to one district and not to two, **and a man can be at a door in four districts in four rolls in this empire and be on none of them in the sense he means**, and she told him that in the first week of the tenth month of the year after. And the rule printed in a room: **that is a plain question and not a question about a word on a form, and the count of plain questions is a different count and is not printed anywhere, and the count of the other kind is four and it has been four since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, and nobody has asked anybody anything since, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and I am not going to be one.** And a correction she insists on: she told a man of about sixty at a counter four bays and a corridor and a river away that he could ask, **and I do not know a thing about that man, and I am not going to pretend I gave that to you as well, because you did not ask and I have not offered, and I would like both of those noticed.** Then the basket. He asks what is in it and she does not answer for about a minute. **There is one in that basket about a street with a shop on it, and if it goes into a hand then in about four years there is a person who can put it beside a sheet of mine and ask me what the difference is between the two, and that person would not be a stranger. That person would be you, and you would be doing it about yourself and about your wife and your boy, and I have got no way of stopping you and I would not want to.** He does not ask for one. **You are the only person in this empire who has come back to this window three times and asked me nothing, and a man who comes back and asks nothing is the only kind of person it is safe to tell anything to at all, and I would like that written down somewhere and I am not going to write it anywhere.** Then the column: he has been in the corridor four hundred times in nine years, he can read, he has never asked her about it because a clerk's own column is not a thing a customer has got — **then you have been the right kind of man for nine years and you are the first one to have noticed it, and nobody is going to thank you and I am not going to.** She has not filled it in nine years and has never told anybody why and has no answer, **and the reason she has never told anybody is that she does not know, and a person who cannot say why has no business putting a heading over a thing.** He says the obvious thing — then nobody is ever going to fill it in — and she says yes, and that it is a waste of a column, **and it is the correct thing to be done with it**, and she would like him to go away now and is not being rude. Then the corridor, and the door a couple of inches open halfway along it, which has been every working day for nine years and which she has passed about two thousand times. **A person who opens a room nobody has asked her about is a person who has given herself a duty, and a person who has given herself a duty cannot stop.** She says she is not going to go in there. He says he was not going to go in there when he said it, and she says she was not going to before he said it, **and you have not asked me to and I want that noticed, because a man who stands in a corridor and does not ask a question is doing me a favour and there is no form in this empire for that either and I have got about nine of them to my account and not one of them is a debt I can pay back.** Then she goes to the second press for a second copy for a man of about fifty who asked twice and gave no reason and is not entitled to one, and comes back and reports: the corridor is about four hundred yards long, four presses, about four hundred returns a year between them, **and the majority of them are a household at a door and the day and the water, and not one of the two four hundreds is a question and nobody has ever asked either of them for anything.** And standing at the door four seconds, not going in, and she knows what it costs in about four years, and there is nothing in that room that anybody has ever told her about. Then the man of about fifty comes back, pays fourpence again, and asks whether it would tell her anything, and she says it would tell her that a man of about fifty wanted a second piece of paper for about fourpence, and that is a different thing, and that is all it would tell her. **And I have just walked past four presses with about four hundred returns in them and I have not told you what is in the presses, and that is not an oversight and I am not going to let it be one, because I told you about nine inches under this sill and I have not told you about the four hundred yards behind you, and both of those are true, and the reason I told you the first one is that you came back and asked me nothing, and the reason I am not telling you the second one is that you came back and asked me nothing, and I have not got two different reasons and I have got one and I am using it twice.** Then the window shuts at about the fourth minute past the hour, a man of about fifty is served in two minutes, and the reason nobody asks at a counter is that a person who wants to know something at a counter does not come to a counter. Her own book, no office on it, her own hand, the day on it, read back twice, the second reading slower, the reason she has written eleven times in nine years: **a hand of mine is the only hand anybody is going to be able to put side by side in about four years, and I have now done this for the thirteenth time and I have not stopped and I am not going to.** And after that, in the same book, on the same day, the only second line she has ever written in it, and it is not a rule: **Two doors, one man.** And the close: she looks at the corridor and at the door and does not go and close it either, **and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to shut a door**, and the count is four and has not moved, and a woman of about thirty-five in another city has said in a room that a name in a book does not go looking, and a woman of sixty-three in the same city has said her own name out loud in a street three times to nobody, and neither is an asking, and none of the three has a heading over it. **The review repair rebuilt that closing roll-call as five sentences instead of one 222-word sentence, which was the longest sentence in the batch, and added the fourth figure to it: four hundred and thirty miles up this river there is a man of thirty-eight in a rented room called Marek Kest, who has no office and no fund and no register and whose name is not on any of the boards in that city, and he is the one of them that anybody can go looking for and come back from with nothing, because there is nothing there to find, and he says that out loud in rooms to whoever is standing in them, and it is the reason nobody on that flood terrace is going to hear a name this season.** The roll-call still ends on the door a couple of inches open halfway along the corridor, which is still not shut and still not entered, and the number four is still the count of askings and is still not the number of figures in the paragraph.

## Chapter 226 — remainder of the entry

**Her wage stopped in the second week of the third month of the year after and it is stopped now and the word for it is *held*.** There are fifteen books in the room on a table and a board and every one of the fifteen is somebody else's and none of the fifteen has an office on it. A woman of about thirty comes up at about the tenth hour, stands in the doorway about four minutes, and says *not for them, for me*: there are four of them and a door, the water came to it in the first week of the second month of this year and went off again and nobody came, not the man from the water and not a woman from anywhere and not a boy, and she has had that since the first week of the second month and has said it to nobody. **The price, out loud, before anything else: nobody is going to be told that I have done this** — not the man from the water, not the man at the other end of the lane, and the woman is not going to be thanked for asking and she is not going to be thanked for doing it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person to be thanked for keeping a book and none for a person to keep one at all, **and I have looked, and I looked with a clerk of thirty-four in the ninth month of the year after because I cannot get through a shelf of forms quickly enough on my own.** And the worse half: no fee, no wage, no day rate, and if she is not here in a year the sixteen go to a woman of about thirty with a key who will not know what to do with them. *How much is it.* **It is nothing, and I want that said first, because you have come in here with a face that has done a sum.** *I have not got anything.* **Then it is nothing and it is going to stay nothing, and the day you can pay me for it is the day I stop, and I am telling you that now so that you cannot come back in a year and say that I did not tell you.** Then the hour and a half, and the street-name problem: the woman says the name the way she says it and the water office spells it differently, and Nell Kest does not correct her. **Because if this book is a place then it is a door with a name on it, and a door with a name on it is a thing that can be carried out of a room, and there is about nine hundred and forty names in a book on a shelf about nine miles off that road that came off a building off it, and I am not going to be the second one.** A household book is not a place; it is some numbers that somebody agreed to write down; and she is going to write the street exactly as the water office spells it, because that is what the water will send a man to, **and I am not going to be the reason a letter about four people goes to a street that does not have them in it.** *You would have found that out for me in a year.* **I would have found it out in about four years, when somebody came asking, and by then there would be a wrong name in a book that nobody has ever opened, and that is what I am frightened of.** Then the woman asks her name, which nobody has ever done in that room, and gets the finding of the chapter: **nobody asks me anything** — four people to ask since the fifth month of the year after, not one of them has asked her a question, and the two she asked are a woman of about fifty-five in a room nine miles off who has said the price out loud to about nine people since the second week of the ninth month, and a man with no office, answerable since the first week of the first month of the year after next, who cannot be found and does not know it, **and I asked him a question in a rented room about nine miles from where I live and I walked there and back in one day, and there is not one form in this empire for what I did.** *Did anybody send you.* **Nobody sent me.
