There is no form anywhere in this empire for a person who comes on her own account, and I have looked, and a woman of sixty-three in this city told me that in a room in the second week of the twelfth month of the year after, and she was not sent either, and she is the only other person I have met who did a thing for nothing and got nothing for it.** Then the knock, and the second object of the chapter: **a foreman off the dye end, about forty, whose name is on nothing, who has come up two stairs twice, the second time the second week of the third month of the year after when he told her that her wage was held.** He has been told to tell her it is still held, and that is all he has got, and he has had it since the first week of the fourth month of last year, and he did not come up, **and the reason he did not come up is that he could not think of one thing to tell her that would not be a reason to keep holding it.** He has asked twice — third week of the third month of the year after, fourth week of the ninth — and both times was told to keep asking, and both times a person said the same thing, which is that it is held and it will be looked at. **Nobody has ever told me what it is held against. There is no form. I have been in a room with about nine hundred forms in it and I have never seen one that has a person's wage on it and nowhere on it to put the thing it is held for. And I am the only person in this city who is holding your money and I cannot tell you what it is held for, and that is not a way a person is usually described.** *You have just said that to me without saying it.* **A person who is paid can be told to stop. You are the only man in this city who can pick up or put down the whole of what I have, and you cannot do it for a reason nobody will give you, and that is being told to stop with a different word on it, and I have heard that sentence out loud from a man with no office in a rented room in the first week of the first month of the year after next and from a book-keeper on a bank about four hundred and thirty miles down this river before that.** And he works it out in four seconds: **then I am one of about four people in this matter who cannot be asked, and I have never once been told that, and I have been forty for about nine years and I have not noticed until this minute.** And her answer: **nobody is going to tell you, there is no post in this empire whose job is to tell, and the only way anybody finds out that they cannot be asked is that they work out that they have asked and been let off**, and she is not going to thank him and is going to do the other thing, which is to tell him that a woman of about sixty-four has said the same sentence out loud in a room with about nine men in it, **and you are the first person in nine years who has arrived at it on your own and I have got nothing to add to it.** Then her instruction at the door: **write to me. A line, with the day on it, saying it is held. That is all. Do not ask them anything and do not tell them anything and do not put my name at the foot of a thing.** *I have not got a book.* **You have got a hand, and there is nobody in this empire more findable than a hand.** Then back to the fifteen and the sixteen: a new book with a street two streets off at the top of it in her own hand, a day against it, no name — and she looks at that about a minute and a half and then **puts a name on it, and it is her own**, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a book-keeper to put her own name at the head of another person's numbers, and she is the only person who will ever read it, and in about four years there is a person with a copy of the right page who could put that name beside a door. **She did it on purpose, in about four seconds, and read it back once and not twice**, and she has read the other four lines at the back of a book of eleven houses once each and not twice either, **and that is the only difference between them and it is the whole of what she has got.** Then the count, dated, the four, the two she has asked, the two she has not: a woman of about sixty with a board and a basket, four hundred and thirty miles up this river, the only person alive who can say how a box got filled and who does not know anybody knows she is the only one; and a window four hundred and thirty miles down it, on a flood terrace, **and she has not got a fund and there is no form in this empire for a fund, and four hundred and thirty miles of road is what it costs anybody who wants to go and be asked a question, and she is not going this month, and she has not said she is not going at all, and there is a difference between those two things and it is the only thing in this room she is going to say out loud.** And the coat on the back of the chair that she does not go out in, and no form that says a woman of twenty has to decide a thing on the day she decides it, and none that says she has to decide it at all, and sixteen books on a table and not one with an office on it and nobody told about any of the sixteen.

## Chapter 227 — remainder of the entry

Nobody sent her, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a person who comes on her own account, and she was let in because the watch requires a person who is not from the office that cannot be named to be present, which is the reason she is at the table with a sheet of paper in her hand, and nobody merged the two instruments and Tamsin Rook says so from the stair.** From the stair: *you have not sent for me.* **A clerk of thirty-four told you where this room was and would not write it down, and you walked nine miles, and I was not told and I would not have been able to stop you.** And: *then why are you standing at the table.* **Because of the other instrument, and I am not going to say its name out loud to you because it is not mine, and it is the reason I am here and it is the reason you are being let up a stair, and neither of us has ever merged the two and I am not going to start in a doorway.** He does not get up. He says the wrist line out loud and then does not mention it again that afternoon. Then she asks what he is answerable for, and the order of the sentence is the point: **for an answer I gave you in the first week of the first month, in a rented room, in this city, when you had walked nine miles, and I could not give it to you and I gave you a price instead. And there is nobody in this empire who can look me up. The answer came first and the unfindability is not the reason for it, it is the reason nobody ever found out about it.** And she: **I am answerable for asking, and I am in a box at the other end of a column that a clerk filled in in about nine seconds in the fifth month of the year after, and if anybody comes with a copy of the right page in about four years then you are the one they will come to and I am the one they will not.** Then Tamsin Rook, standing, four minutes: **the only person in this matter who can be found is the one who is answerable for asking, and the only person who is answerable and cannot be found is the one who answered. Every answerable person in this empire so far has been a clerk, and a clerk has a window or a counter or a table, and a clerk is the cheapest person in a city to send anywhere, and I have been one for two years. You have never been sent. I have never been sent, and that is the good news, and the reason nobody sent me is that nobody wanted anything sent.** Then the plain question, and it is marked as one by both other people in the room: **are you the most dangerous person in this matter, and if you are, is it the same dangerous as the one a clerk of about fifty-five has a record of, or is it a different dangerous, and is there anybody in this empire who can protect you, and if there is not, is that because you would not let them.** And the answer: **there is nobody who can protect me and that is not a misfortune. There is nothing anybody can instruct me about, nothing anybody can pay me for, and nothing anybody can thank me for.** That is why he is the most dangerous person in this matter and also why nobody in it can stand in front of him; a person nobody can pay cannot be bought, cannot be threatened, cannot be shown a bill; a person nobody can thank cannot be repaid. **And it is the same sentence as the one about a person with a discharged bar, and it is the same sentence as the one about a foreman nobody can serve, and I have heard all three of those said out loud in three rooms about four hundred and thirty miles apart and I am not going to pretend I did not hear them.** And what it is worth him, to a person who can be found and a man who cannot: **about four years, and about fourpence a copy and about a shilling a question, and I have not got any of it. And I could be handed a hundred pounds tomorrow by a person who wanted me to stop and I would have to be able to refuse it, and there is no form in this empire that makes a man refuse a hundred pounds. The only thing standing between me and being paid is that nobody in this empire has anything to pay me for, and that is not a defence and it is not a wall and it is a thing that is true this afternoon and not necessarily on a day in the next week.** Then Tamsin Rook: **then we are both of them.** And the answer is no, and this is the part she walked nine miles for: **he cannot be found and I can be found, and that is the whole asymmetry, and it means that the one person in this matter who could answer for him is the one person who could not be left to do it twice. And I have got four people I was going to ask and I have asked two of them, and one of the two is him, and I have just found out what asking him costs and I am going to pay it, and I am not going to ask the other two this month.** *You knew that in the first week of the first month.* **I knew that in the second week of the eleventh month of the year after and I have been arguing with myself about it ever since and I have lost.** Then the word he would not put down, said to a person for the first time: a clerk of about fifty-five said it once in a room with a rail in it in the first week of the fifth month of the year after and then stopped and would not describe the hand and would not say which of two papers of the same office it came from; it is in about nine hundred documents and not on any of the boards in this city and a court asked about it about nine years ago and has not been answered. *You have got it in your head.* **I have got it in my head and I have not written it down and I am not going to, and I have not said it to a person until this afternoon.** *Then write it down, you have a book with no office on it and you have a hand, write it down and then there is a record of it.* **That is exactly why I am not going to, and the reason is the one you have just used against me without knowing you were using it. Nine hundred documents in this empire have that word in them, and I would not be the first person to have it in a hand, and I would be the first person to have it in a hand and nothing else behind it.** Every other person in this empire who has that word in a hand is a clerk, or a factor, or a man in a building with about nine hundred and forty names on a shelf, and every one of them has a trade, and the trade is the wall; he has got no trade, a bar that came off on one line and about four feet of a wall he does not use. And then, plain: a word in his hand is the only thing anybody could put a name to, there is nothing about him for the name to stick to, **and that is the whole of why I am the most dangerous person in this matter, said by a person of twenty who has walked nine miles and is standing in the room.** Then the two candidates, laid out and not chosen: **a liability I cannot refuse, or a labour I can be refused. The first is the shape of four years and it costs nothing and nobody has to pay anything and there is no form for it. The second is the shape of a road, about four hundred and thirty miles each way, about six days, and a man who can be told to stop because somebody is paying for the day. Both of them are true of me. I have not got a form that says which one I am, and there is no form in this empire for that, and I am not going to invent one at about the fourth hour with two people in the room.** And Tamsin Rook: **you have not chosen because choosing is a decision about what you are, and you have not got one, and the twelfth line of your sheet is empty and it has been empty since the third week of the ninth month of the year after and it is the only honest thing in that book.** And the price, said standing, and nobody thanked her and nobody was going to: **I have said the price of being a party out loud ten times and nobody has thanked me once and I did not expect to be thanked once and I would like it written down that I did not.** Then she goes out of the door and down the stair and does not shut it, and he says which instrument it was after she has gone, out loud, to a room with a table in it, **because a form gets merged by being inconvenient**: a watch does not send anybody anywhere, it is not in force now and it is not spent, it requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be present, about six hundred people in this city could satisfy it, **and nobody has ever been paid for satisfying it and nobody has ever been thanked.** And the last of it, from Nell Kest, after another about nine minutes: **I am not going to write this down either. Then there is no document in this empire that says you were ever in this room. No. And I want it said that I worked that out in a few seconds and that I worked it out because of a woman of twenty-four who did exactly the same thing in the third week of the twelfth month of the year after, and told a man of about thirty-four at a counter about fourpence that she was not going to write his answer down and then told him what the price of it was a second time. And that is the good news and it is the bad news, and I am going to walk nine miles back and I am not going to have anybody pay for the day.**

## Chapter 228 — remainder of the entry

Thirty-four, eleven years at the counter, came to it from the second counter where estates are, not a lawyer and does not want to be one, a party to an instrument about a dead woman's name, **and there is no form in this empire for a document about a person who did not ask to be in one and she knows that better than anybody in this city.** Alone at the rail from the ninth hour until about the eleventh; eleven copies made, four blanks sold. The man of about sixty comes with his slip and four words, a copy on a sheet of press in about a minute and a half, read out, it is right, fourpence. Then he does not go, and she has thought about that for about four days. *You told a woman of twenty in the second week of the ninth month of the year after that you may ask me anything, and I have been coming here every week since with a slip and I have not asked you anything, and I have got a question and it is a plain one and it is not about a word on a form and I am telling you that before you tell me the price of it.* Then: **can an office hold a person to a thing.** She puts the copy down face up, which she does about twice a year. **No.** And she tells him the reason and then the thing underneath the reason and then he is to leave, and the reason is nine sentences: **an office is not a person.** An office is a room with a word over the door; there is no form in this empire for serving an office and none for making an office come and stand in a place and be asked. **A document that names an office is a document** — one side of a sheet of good paper, not printed, a seat's own device cut into the head of it, set out in six numbered lines and a foot, **and the second of the six asks that no inference of any kind be drawn from the absence of any name, and the sixth declines to name whoever holds the office at all.** Every one of the six is about a room, and she has read that paper twice, has never been asked to, and has never been shown the other one of the two, and is not going to say a word about the other one, and he did not ask and she wants that noticed. *So nothing can be done to the office.* **Nothing can be done to the office and nothing can be done by it either, and that is the same fact seen from the other side. I have never seen a clerk work that out out loud in any of the buildings in this city. I have worked it out out loud twice and both times to a man of about sixty who came back**, and the second time was to a man who was told the answer to his own question in nine sentences by a woman with a fee board over her head. *Then who can be held to anything.* **Anybody with a hand at the foot of a thing.** A person can be held to a thing they signed and to nothing else; in about four years a man with a copy of the right page can put your hand beside a day and ask you what you meant by it, **and that is the whole of the instrument in this empire and it is not enough and it is all there is.** A hand, and a day, and about four years; nothing else, no third thing; eleven years of watching every document go out of that counter and not one person in this city held to anything by anything but a hand — not a clerk, not a man in a building off the old river road with eleven books on a shelf and about four thousand entries in them. *And a thing said out loud.* **A thing said out loud in a room to a person with no office is not held to anything by anybody, ever, because there is no form that makes a room into a place a sentence can be got at. And I know that because a man of about thirty-eight was in a room with me in the third week of the ninth month of the year after and said a sentence out loud about four words on a form, and he has no office and no register and no fund and a bar that came off on one line he wrote himself, and nothing in this empire can put a line under his name for it, and I have checked, and a woman of twenty checked first and paid fourpence for the checking.** *And a thing said out loud to a clerk.* A minute and a half, and it is not hesitation: **that is a different thing and it is the reason I am the cheapest person in this city to send. There is no form anywhere in this empire that says a clerk of this table has to write down what she has been asked, so the honest answer is that it depends entirely on the clerk**, and about nine hundred of them are paid and every one can be shown the day she answered and the words she used. So a clerk is a person who can be held to a thing by a person who pays her, and a person who pays her can show her the day. **And I have said that reason out loud on a bank about four hundred and thirty miles down this river, because a book-keeper of thirty-eight said it before me in the second week of the tenth month of the year after and wrote it down in his own hand and read it back twice, and I am not going to say it as though I had it first.** *You have a book under the counter with no office on it. Then write this down.* **No** — fast, then again slower, which she does about four times a year. **I have a rule and I made it myself, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for the rule, and that is exactly why it is mine.** She does not put a sentence of her own into a book with no office on it, because the moment there is one with a day on it, in about four years there is a thing anybody can hold up against her and ask what she meant by it, **and the whole of what she has been afraid of since the second month of the year after is a document with my hand at the foot of it** — and a woman of twenty-four asked her in the ninth month of the year after to write down one sentence of her own and put her name under it, and she said no, and the reason she gave was this one, and she was right to be refused and has not been thanked and is not going to be. And: **I have not got a book with no office on it under this counter. I have got a drawer with a day-book in it that belongs to this table and goes into a press at the end of every day, and everything in that day-book is a thing somebody paid fourpence for, and there is not one word of me in it and there is not going to be.** Then the cost, and it is the finding of the chapter: **so there is no record that I asked you anything.** None at all. He asked one plain question on the fourth day of the fourth week of the fourth month of the year after next at about the tenth hour; she gave nine sentences and about a minute and a half and wrote nothing down and is not going to; he is the only person who knows it and she is the only other one, **and in about four years neither of us can prove a word of it, and I have wanted a thing for myself in this matter for two years and I have just not had it on purpose, and I have never once done that before and I would like it noticed that I noticed.** He pays fourpence, reads his copy out to himself, goes. Then a man of about fifty with a form in a folder, and she cannot complete it, and why is a plain question and she says so before answering: she turns it round, puts a finger on a printed line near the foot of the second page, **and reads it out loud to him in about four seconds in the plainest words there are and does not write anything down and does not have to**: the fourth line says this office does not complete it, and there are about six hundred of those in the press in a bay in this city, and she has read it to about nine people a year for eleven years and has got faster at it and not better at it. *And who completes it.* **Whichever office the heading at the top of it names, and the heading names an office, and the office is in another building, and I have never seen anybody from it and I have never been sent anything from it and there is no form in this empire that says a person may send anybody anything.** And: **that is the fourth time somebody has been in this room and not got what they came for, and three of the other three were clerks, and a clerk is the cheapest person in a city to send and nobody has ever sent me, and I have been at this counter eleven years and I have been here for all four of them.** *And if the office in the other building does not do it either.* *Then what.* **Then there is no procedure for a form that nobody has completed. It goes back. It sits in a tray. It is not a complaint and it is not a refusal and nobody is going to be told about it**, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that says what a person may do with a piece of paper that has come back with a line on it not completed. *So I have got a piece of paper.* **You have got a piece of paper with a day on it and a heading at the top of it, and one goes out of every building in this city in a year, and one goes to every district, and a return goes to one district and not to two, and when one comes back, all that has happened is that a person somewhere has found out that an office is a word over a door with nobody behind it. That is worth knowing. It is not worth fourpence and I am not going to charge you for it, because I have not got a line on the board for it and I am not going to cut one.** *You could put one.* **I have got a knife in this drawer, and it is the same knife I use for the box of blanks under this counter, and I have taken it out and looked at a blank and put it back, and that was in the first week of the second month of the year after next, and I have not taken it out since, and you are not going to make me, and if you are going to keep standing there I am going to serve the next person at this rail.** Then the slab, which takes about a minute and is the only part of the hour in which she is not a clerk, and the count said out loud to the slab, for the fifth time and to a counter and not to a person: four, in about a hundred and forty years, and it has not moved since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next; the first three dated; two of the four were accidents; the third was the first one anybody did on purpose; **and a man of about sixty has now been in this room more times than any other person in eleven years, wanting to ask and using about a third of it, and that is not one of the four, because it is not about a word on a form, and I have said so out loud twice this morning and I am not going to be thanked for saying it.** The board over her head had four things on it and none of them was a question, a blank in a box under the counter is still uncut, **there is no form anywhere in this empire that says a clerk of a notary's table may put a question on a fee board**, nobody has ever sent anybody anywhere, the count is four, the ink is dry, and the fourth day of the fourth week of the fourth month of the year after next came and went at that counter exactly as the two hundred years before it did. **The review repair rewrote that closing sentence, which had restated the knife, the fee board and the uncut blank three beats the chapter had already delivered, and it shortened the chapter by fifty-six words, which is where the room for the next paragraph came from. It also made two changes inside the chapter. The first is that the man of about thirty-eight who was in her room in the third week of the ninth month of the year after is now named, and he is Marek Kest, and the naming is what lets the second change exist: a second speech, in which she says that when a person looks at him there is less of him and not more, that she has got a day and a district and a room he rents by the week and there is nobody in this city who could put those three side by side and get a person out of them, that a woman of twenty is the only person in this matter who can be found on purpose, and that she has looked, has not told her, and is not going to, and would like it noticed that she looked.** The count of askings did not move, and the man of about sixty's one plain question on the day is still the only question asked in the chapter.

## Chapter 229 — remainder of the entry

**A factor's letter of four lines on the bench where it has been since the second week of the third month of the year after, wanting the name of a person who may be served and that person's consent, and it says without both that it cannot proceed and will not proceed. It is not going to be answered, and every day it is not answered is a day nobody has been entered.** A carter asked in the second week of the tenth month of the year after to have his own line written in the place where the no is, and went up the road for the winter. A bill at Lowcross at nineteen pounds three and fourpence, unpaid, nobody liable, **no line for a bridge in that fund in nineteen years**, and nobody in that shed is going to be asked for a penny or for a better reason than the one he has got. **And about four people in that bay cannot read a paragraph. They are on the list of works. Their work is on the list. They have never been asked anything and they are not going to be asked this season, and the finding he entered in his own hand in the second week of the tenth month of the year after stands, and he has not re-entered it, and he has not improved it, and it is not his to improve.** Then a man of about fifty-five at the end of the bench with his own printed slip, asking what the day under it means, and Rennick Adley tells him in about four seconds in a book-keeper's voice, does not read the slip out of his hand, writes nothing down, and then stops because he has done it in a bay with about nine men in it and did not mean to. *I meant whether it is the day it started or the day somebody decided.* **Then somebody decided.** And: **You read it to me just now. I answered you. A man reading a man his own paper is not a question and it is not one of the four and it is not counted, and the count of the four is a count of people who asked a person out loud what a word on a form means, and there is a clerk of fifty-five in this city who says that in a room and I am not going to improve on it.** And the four have not been asked about the hold and he is not going to ask, and that is a not-doing and there is a day against this one. Then the list read in about four minutes in a foreman's voice, and about nine men heard her and did not hear anything in particular, **because that is what she is for** — Halla Wray, fifty-one, the foreman of the Slade Cut, an office with no office in it, on that bank since she was nine, who can read and whom nobody at the district works has ever once in nine years asked to read anything, **and who has been answering questions for four men in that bay since she was forty-two without being asked, and a man with a ledger has seen her do it twice now and has not said so to her and is not going to.** Then she says the thing, and it is not about the works: **if the office was meant to be temporary, who changed it into an inheritable one.** He does not answer it and does not ask her where she got it, **and neither of those was careful. Both of them were the arrangement**, made in the seventh month of the year after in a shed with about nine men in it: the day he asks is the day she stops saying the half of a sentence she has got, and the half came out of her mouth in the last week of the eleventh month of the year after to a man from a district works who asked her a question about the works, and it cannot be got back, and he heard it and did not ask and did not write it down. **That is a plain question and it is not about a word on a form, and I am not going to count it and neither are you.** *I did not count it.* **I am not going to ask you where you got it, and I want that noticed, and the reason is that a man who asks a foreman a question that is not about the works is a man who has begun asking a foreman questions, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for that and there is no post and no wage for it and I have a wage.** *Write it down.* **No. That is the first thing you have ever refused me that was not about money. It is not about money. It is about a book in a building, and it is the only thing in this shed that is not about money.** And the reason, with his hand flat on a shut ledger: **that letter on that bench wants a name that may be served. Four lines, and a factor's hand, and it says it will not proceed without a name and that name's consent.** A man who wants that name writes to the company, and the company has no office and no fund and a book-keeper, and he does not have to come up the road at all. **He asks for the book.** And then, to himself and to the shed: **and that is the whole of the reason, and it is a reason about a bench and not about a question, and I have thought about it for about four minutes and I have got a better one and I am going to keep the better one for myself and write this one down instead.** Then she tells him she has read the notice nine times and the second line nine times and the sixth line about four times, **and that the sixth line will not name whoever holds the office, and that she has never said that out loud in a shed before and has said it to him now and is not going to say it again.** And the two sheets, said in her mouth for the first time in this form: **there is a copy of the six lines in a book of my own with the day against it and no name on it, and neither of those two sheets is in my hand except one of them and that one is a copy and I wrote it out myself, and neither of them has my name on it and I did not put it there.** *Then say it once more and I will not write it down.* **No, because you have just told me the reason you are not going to write it down, and it is a good reason, and it is the reason I have not put my own name on anything since the first week of the seventh month of the year after, and it is also the reason nobody has come up this road asking me anything, and both of those are the same reason and it is the fourth time I have said it and it is a bad one.** *It is a good reason.* **It is a good reason that keeps a foreman in a box that nobody is looking in, and a bad reason that keeps four men who cannot read a paragraph from ever being told what the hold is, and I have got two bad reasons and I am not going to hand you a third one in a shed at about the fifth hour and have you read it back to me in a book.** Then the second thing he came to do, which is an entry: **a girl of seventeen is on this list of works at the rate the list is set at, on a written engagement, on her fourth day. She has not been thanked. Nobody has thanked her.** And then he stops, and looks at it about four minutes, **and does not put a reason under it, and it is the first entry he has made in that book since the second month of next year without one, and every one of the other four hundred and some has got one, and he reads them going back, and they are good reasons, and they are his.** And out loud to the bench: **a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop.** That is the reason for the four, the reason a foreman has not told her she is the one person in that bay who could read the hold, and the reason he did not tell the foreman he read a man's paper to him in a yard in the first week of the twelfth month of the year after. **It is the same reason every time and I have got it exactly where I want it, and I could have put it under this entry in about four seconds and it would have been true, and I did not, and I do not know why.** And the harder not-doing: **he did not write the question down either, and it is the first time in about two years he has wanted to put a thing in that book and not done it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a thing a man wanted to write and did not** — *a foreman of fifty-one said a question out loud in a shed on the second day of the first week of the fifth month of the year after next, and about nine men heard it and not one of them will be able to repeat it, and neither can I in about four years, and the man who can repeat it is the one person in this empire that the question is about, and he is not in this room, and he cannot be in this room, and there is no form in this empire that says a man has to be in a room.* Then the man of about fifty from the fourth gang at the door at about the sixth hour, the bridge at Lowcross, **that is not a reason the bridge knows about**, and *I know*, and **then it is not a reason and I am not going to ask you for another one, and there is no form in this empire that requires a man to give you a better reason than I want to get over there, and I have read every form in that store and there is not one page in any of them that says a person has to give you a better reason than that**, and *that is the reason I am still here* and *nobody is going to ask you to count them out loud. If they do, there is no form for it and you do not have to help them.* And the close: the third column has nineteen reasons with a day against every one and one of the nineteen is a man who wanted to get over a bridge and the reason is the same one he gave in the seventh month of the year after and nobody has asked him again and nobody is going to; **a bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line against it in nineteen years, and a foreman who will not ask a man for a better reason than he has got, and those two facts have been standing on that bank since before the woman of twenty was born and neither of them is going to be paid and neither of them is going to be forgiven**; a book-keeper on a bench about four hundred yards up the road with a book shut on his knee, an entry with no reason under it, and a half of a sentence in his head that came out of a foreman's mouth in the last week of the eleventh month, which he has not asked for and is not going to and cannot get back; about nine miles off that road a clerk of about fifty-five with a name at the foot of a question in a book with a day on it and the answer not in the book, and a court that asked about a thing with a name about nine years ago and has not been answered, and a foreman who said the first half of that out loud in a shed and nobody answered it and nobody is going to; four askings, the count unmoved, no post, a guarantee standing on about four hundred and forty foot with about sixty children inside it, a reader of seventeen not thanked, about four men who have never been asked anything and are not going to be, **a woman of fifty-one who has said in a shed that she is going to stop being glad of a thing that nobody can check, and the lamp is not lit yet.** **The review repair broke that 190-word roll-call into four paragraphs and added a fifth, and the fifth is the batch's fourth appearance of Marek Kest: about four hundred and thirty miles down that river there is a man of thirty-eight with no office in a rented room, and he is not in any of the things on that bank, and he is the one of them that anybody can go looking for and come back from with nothing, and he says that out loud in rooms to whoever is standing in them, and it is the reason nobody in that shed is going to hear a name this season.** The chapter's own finding is untouched: the man who can repeat the foreman's question is not in that shed and cannot be in it, and the repair did not put him in it.

## Chapter 230 — remainder of the entry

She had a third reason by the fourth hour and it was the worst of the three and she gave it out loud in a shed to about nine men, **because that is where the other two were given and she was not going to give it anywhere else.** *A guarantee is a thing that can be signed.* **There is one standing on about four hundred and forty foot of this bank, it has not been answered, and about sixty children under sixteen are inside it.** She has not got the terms of it in her head and is not going to read them out in a bay at about the seventh hour. And what a guarantee is: a piece of paper that somebody signs, and the whole of what it is for is that there is a name at the foot of it that somebody can come and ask. And the other half in the same breath: **about nineteen people on this cut have said no to the season out loud, one after another on a day, and every one of them gave a reason and every one of the reasons is in a column in a book in that store with the day against it, and there is not one name in that column. The names are in a different book in a different building, in nine hands, over about sixty years. And about four of the people on this cut are not in that list either, because they cannot read a paragraph and they have never been asked anything, and there is no column for them and there is not going to be one, and I have got a reason about those four and I am not giving it in this shed.** *Why are you telling us that.* **Because I am the foreman, and a foreman who is found is a foreman somebody can be asked to be a guarantor.** There is no form in this empire for asking a woman of fifty-one on a bank to be a guarantor and there never has been one, and that is not an accident, and it is why the third reason is worse than the other two. And then the mechanism: **a guarantee does not ask a person whether it is a good idea. It is written so that the answer is on a line at the foot of it with a name on that line, and a foreman who has not been asked anything since the seventh month of the year after is a foreman whose name has never been on a line, and that is the only thing standing between this bank and a piece of paper with my name at the bottom of it, and it is not a wall, it is a habit, and habits can be given up in a shed at about the fourth hour of the afternoon by a person who is tired.** *You are saying being found is a danger.* **I am saying being found is a door, and I have been standing in front of it for about nine years and I never knew that was what I was doing, and I have said in this shed in the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after that I would like to be found, and I meant it, and I am not going to unsay it, and I am also not going to pretend I did not know what it cost.** And the cost is a piece of paper: not a room, not a court, not a wage; a piece of paper with a line at the foot of it for a name, about forty-one people on this cut, and the Company's whole money four shillings a year and all of it the rent of this building, **and there is nothing behind me that a piece of paper could lean on.** Then the ordinary things, because a foreman who stops doing them has begun to make a speech of her own job: a man of about fifty-five with a chain asks about the third link and it takes about two minutes, **and every man in that shed watched a foreman do a perfectly ordinary thing to a man who cannot read a paragraph, and she did not ask him** — not about the hold, not about the season, not about the two sheets on the bench in the store, and a book-keeper read a piece of that man's own paper to him in a yard in the first week of the twelfth month of the year after and wrote nothing down and told nobody, and she knew, and she was not going to ask him about it, **because asking is the only instrument there is and she has not spent it once in about nine years and neither has anybody else on that bank.** Then the decision, said to the shed and not to a person, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a foreman to say a thing to a shed: **I have decided to stop being glad of it.** The bay is quiet about a minute. Then the two facts kept apart and only one of them a decision: **I said in a shed in the second week of the third month of the year after that I had been glad of not being asked about anything for nine years and I had not admitted it to anybody, and I admitted it in front of about nine men in the fourth week of the second month of the year after next, and I have not stopped and I am not going to. What I am going to stop is the second thing, and I want the two halves of that said separately because they are two facts and only one of them is a decision. I am not going to make myself easier to ask. That stands.** It was decided in a room and said out loud and it is the wrong decision and she has named the right one and the woman who did it, and she is not going to say any of that a third time in a bay. **What I am stopping is the gladness. And I have worked out this week why I was glad, and it is because a foreman who is glad of not being found is a foreman who has arranged to stay in a box, and a box is the cheapest thing anybody in this empire can put a person in, and it costs a clerk about nine seconds, and it does not need a room and it does not need a form and it does not need anybody to be told.** *Who is going to know that you have stopped being glad of it.* **Nobody. That is the point of it and it is the whole of what I have got and I have had since the fourth day of the first week of this month. There is no form anywhere in this empire that says a woman of fifty-one on a bank has to declare what she is not glad about, and there is no post and no wage and no instrument, and a decision that nobody can check is not a rule and it is not going to change anything on this bank and it is mine.** And the other half, because she is not going to be a woman who has found something out and keeps it to herself: **it is the wrong thing to do and the right thing to do is the thing a woman of twenty did in the second week of the eleventh month of the year after, who walked about four miles to a desk and asked how the box got filled, and nobody sent her, and she is the only reason I know what I am, and I have not asked her and I am not going to ask her this season.** Then the ordinary bay again: a man of about thirty asks whether the hold will be on the list before the season shuts, and she tells him it will not and why in about four seconds, and he says that was not what he meant, and she says it was the only meaning it has, and he goes, and about nine men did not hear any of it, **and that is what a foreman is.** And a girl of seventeen at the back of that bay on her written engagement, on her fourth day, on the same rate as everybody else on the list, **not thanked, did not ask, not going to start, nobody spoke to her about the season, and the foreman of fifty-one did not go over to the back of that bay, and there are now three reasons for that and the third one is the guarantee and about sixty children under sixteen are inside it.** Then the man of about fifty from the fourth gang at about the seventh hour, the bridge, **that is not a reason the bridge knows about**, *I know*, **then it is not a reason and I am not going to ask you for another one, and there is no form in this empire that requires a man to hand you a better reason than I want to get over there**, and *that is the reason I am still here* and *nobody is going to ask you to count them out loud; if they do, there is no form for it and you do not have to help them.* And the close, in order: the third column with nineteen reasons and a day against every one and one of them a man who wanted to get over a bridge; **a bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line against it in nineteen years, and a foreman who will not ask a man for a better reason than he has got, and those two facts have been standing on that bank since before the woman of twenty was born and neither of them is going to be paid and neither of them is going to be forgiven**; a book-keeper of thirty-eight about four hundred yards up the road with a book shut on his knee, an entry with no reason under it, and a half of a sentence in his head that cannot be got back; about nine miles off that road a clerk of about fifty-five with a name at the foot of a question in a book with a day on it and the answer not in that book, a court that asked about a thing with a name nine years ago and has not been answered, and a foreman who said the first half of that out loud in a shed on the second day of the first week of the fifth month of the year after next and nobody answered it and nobody is going to. Four askings in about a hundred and forty years, the count unmoved since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, no post whose job is to ask, a guarantee standing on about four hundred and forty foot with about sixty children inside it, a reader of seventeen at the back of a bay not thanked, about four people who cannot read a paragraph never asked anything and not going to be, **a woman of fifty-one who has said in a shed that she is going to stop being glad of a thing that nobody can check, and the lamp is not lit yet, and that is the end of the movement and it is not the end of anything.** **The review repair broke the 158-word close into five paragraphs and put one new paragraph above it, and that paragraph is the batch's last word on Marek Kest and the only one of the six additions that connects him to an instrument: about four hundred and thirty miles down that river there is a man of thirty-eight with no office in a rented room with a twelfth line in a book he has not filled in since the third week of the ninth month of the year after; he has never been able to be served for anything; he has never once written a name down; and if he ever did want the name of a person on that bank he would have to ask a company that has no office and no fund for it, and he would do it in writing, and there is nobody in this city who could stop him, and the clerk in the room nine miles off that road would put the day against it in about nine seconds.** It is the fact the book-keeper gave in the previous chapter — a man who wants a name that may be served asks for the book and does not have to come up the road — carried four hundred and thirty miles, and the foreman's third reason now has a shape on it instead of only being a habit. **The repair also split her third-reason speech, her being-found speech, the book-keeper paragraph and the roll-call, recast five of the chapter's *no form* frames, and cut nothing but the repetition.**

## Notes on these ten summaries

**The word counts in the headings are measured with `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w` after every edit was finished, and they are the post-repair figures: the batch total is 29,032 and was 28,577 when the batch closed. The ten of them are 3,011 / 2,758 / 2,714 / 2,956 / 3,018 / 3,164 / 2,763 / 2,754 / 2,919 / 2,975, all ten inside the 2,700 to 3,200 band, lowest 2,714, highest 3,164, and nothing was trimmed to reach a number.** Where a summary and the prose disagree, the prose is the authority, and every figure, date and count in these ten summaries was read off the finished files rather than off a plan.

## The Volume 05 Batch 0003 review repair, and what it changed in the ten summaries above

**Added by the run that read `logs/batch-0003.review.log`. No summary above was deleted, and the six that changed are changed at the end of their own paragraph so that a reader of the summary and a reader of the chapter are looking at the same chapter. The full record is the last section of `state/continuity.md`; the short version is at the top of `state/current.md`; the batch's own account, items 37 to 44, is in the closing section of `outline/batches/volume-05-batch-0003.md`.**

**What the six changed summaries now carry that they did not: the batch's only six appearances of Marek Kest in chapters that had neither him nor Tamsin Rook.** As first written, Chapters 0221, 0224, 0225, 0228, 0229 and 0230 had no trace of either of them, so six of ten chapters of a male-led serial had no through-line in them at all. **Each of the six is recorded in the paragraph it belongs to, and the four chapters that did have him are unchanged in their substance, because the repair changed no point of view and cut no beat.** Two further changes are recorded where they sit: Chapter 0224's opening paragraph no longer names its own point-of-view character in narration about a different character, and Chapter 0221's opening sentence no longer mixes two tenses inside one sentence.

**The figures in the headings are the post-repair measurements and they are stated as such, because the batch grew by 455 words and a reader comparing this file with a pre-repair record will otherwise find a discrepancy with no explanation.** Chapters 0228 and 0226 were the two nearest the ceiling and were trimmed elsewhere before anything was added: Chapter 0228 lost fifty-six words of a closing paragraph that restated three beats it had already delivered, and Chapter 0226 lost the recurrence of a *no form* frame it had used twice in one speech. **Nothing was trimmed to reach a number. Ten of ten are inside 2,700 to 3,200, lowest 2,714, highest 3,164.**

**And the two craft measurements that were false in this batch's own closing section are now carried here as measured, because a summary file is where a writer looks first: nine emphasis runs in the batch hold two or three sentences and the longest holds three, and ninety-four twelve-word runs are common to three or more of the ten files, of which twenty are the calendar idiom, the count as it is recited, or one of four named refrains.**

## Chapter 231 — remainder of the entry

He put the roof there on purpose.** The store is about four hundred yards from a person who can be served and the north end of it is about nine foot by eleven with a bench along the north wall. The works money is counted once a week, at about the fourth hour, by him, alone, out loud, and he takes the rent off first because the rent is the only thing the company owes anybody, and what is left is about a shilling and a bit. Then a man of about thirty from the second gang comes in with his hat in his hand and asks whether he will be on the list after the season shut, and it is a plain question and Rennick Adley marks it as one out loud before he answers it, and the answer is that he cannot tell him, because the list of works is made in a bay in about four minutes and the foreman reads it out and he writes it down, and **it is a list of works and not a list of persons, and he has never had it in his hands before it is read and has never got it afterwards.** Then the man says the finding in a sentence Rennick Adley will not improve, and reaches for the ledger, and is stopped by two fingers on the cover, and is told that there is nothing in this empire more findable than a hand. Then the second question, which is not about a word on a form either: *whose name is at the foot of the line and why is it not written the way a man would write it.* And the answer, in about four seconds, in a book-keeper's voice: **I am a man who is paid to hold forty-one names in a hand for about four minutes, and I have never once been paid for a day of anything else, and there is nothing anywhere in this empire that a man can be paid for a day of unless it is a day of works on a list.** Then, alone: he is not on the list and has never been on it, and he built the column with no names in it on purpose in the second month of next year, **because a name in a column is a thing a person can be served at**, and a list of works with forty-one names on it goes into a company book about four hundred and thirty miles down the river with about nine hundred and forty names in it in nine hands over about sixty years, and about four hundred of the nine hundred and forty are dead, **and the newest hand in that book is his.** Then the arithmetic, and the days, and the thing nobody has ever told him: **the days he spends on the numbers of other people are not paid and cannot be, and a foreman of fifty-one on this bank has known that since a day in the sixth year of a guild's blank list.** And then the entry, and the finding that is the batch's: **a reason under an entry about a person needs a day, and a day about a person needs a name, and there is not one name in that column.** The names are in a different building in nine hands over about sixty years, and a name cannot be turned back into a reason and a reason cannot be turned back into a day, and there is nothing anywhere in this empire that joins those two books. **So there is one entry in four hundred with a name at the foot of it, and it is the one he cannot say anything about.** And then out loud, to a store with a stove in it: **a thing that leaves no mark can go four hundred yards. It cannot go into a book. And a wage is in a book.** The rest is where it has been: a factor's letter of four lines on that bench wanting the name of a person who may be served and not going to be answered, and every day it is not answered is a day nobody has been entered; a bill at Lowcross at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line against it in nineteen years; a guarantee standing unanswered on about four hundred and forty foot of bank with about sixty children under sixteen inside it; about four people in that bay who cannot read a paragraph and have never been asked anything, and the finding he entered in the second week of the tenth month of the year after stands and has not been re-entered and is not his to improve. **The count of the four askings has not moved since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, the third of the four was the first one anybody did on purpose, and the lamp is not lit.**

## Chapter 232 — remainder of the entry

**Her fourth reason is given out loud in the shed at about the fourth hour, to about nine men, and it is the first of the four that is about her rather than about other people: I am the only person on this bank who can say out loud what an entry in a box does to a life.** About nine hundred people in this empire have been entered in a box this year by a class, and I do not know what it is like for any of the nine hundred and I have not asked and I am not going to, **because a person who is entered in a box and then asked how it feels has been asked twice.** Being the only one who can say it is worth more than being nobody and worth a great deal more than being safe, and she has known that for about eleven weeks and has not told one person. *Who is going to know that you have stopped being glad of it* is answered in Chapter 0230 and is not restaged. **You are not anybody. You are nine men in a shed and a foreman talking to a bay, and in about four years none of you will be able to repeat a word of it and neither will I, and I have worked that out and I did it in the second week of the fifth month of the year after next when a book-keeper said a question out loud in this bay and wrote it down nowhere.** Then the half-sentence, said out loud in a bay for the first time: **in the last week of the eleventh month of the year after she said half a sentence to a man from the district works who asked her a question about the works, and he heard it and did not ask and did not write it down, and it cannot be got back, and the arrangement they made in the seventh month of the year after in that shed is that the day he asks is the day she stops saying the half she has got.** He has not asked, and she says that is not him being careful and she knows it is not. Then the ordinary things: a man of about forty from the third gang asks about the third link of a chain and she answers without turning round in about four seconds and nine men do not notice anything at all, **and she did not ask him about the hold, or about the season, or about a piece of paper a book-keeper read to him in a yard in the first week of the twelfth month of the year after.** Then, and this is new to the page: **she works out, standing behind the bench, that she has been reading that bay with her ears since she was forty-two, that she knows how many are in it to about nine, that she knows when somebody has gone out and come in, that she knows a man's height by the length of his step and a man's age by the length of his breath, and that she has never once in nine years had to look up from a bench to find out where a man was standing, and there is not one form anywhere in this empire that pays a woman for knowing where a man is standing.** And the reason she has not told one of the four: it is nine years of a habit that started because she could read and about four men in that bay could not, and she gave that reason in the second week of the ninth month of the year after and is not giving it a second time. Then a man of about twenty-eight with a folded paper asking whether a person can be on two lists at once, and the answer is that it depends which two, and that if his brother cannot read a paragraph either then the two of them have about four lists between them and not one of the four knows about the other three; and she will not find out the rest for him and will not say why, and if he goes and finds out he is to put the paper back the way he found it. He comes back on the second day and has found out that the district list says a household and not a person, and she says that was about four years late for him and about four years early for him, and she is not going to do it for free and she is not going to do it at all. Then the standing facts: the two sheets on a bench in the hired stone store, one with a seal and nothing under the seal and one in her own hand with a day against it and no name on it, **two sheets and only one of them is in her hand and neither has her name in it and she did not put it there**; a factor's four lines; nineteen pounds three and fourpence at Lowcross with no line against it in nineteen years; about nineteen people who said no again; about four who cannot read a paragraph and have never been asked anything; a third season shut with nobody agreeing it. **The close names him four hundred and thirty miles down the river and says that neither of them knows the other's name and there is no form anywhere in this empire that will put a bay and a rented room in the same place, and the count is four, and the third of the four was the first one anybody did on purpose, and the lamp is not lit.**

## Chapter 233 — remainder of the entry

His name is on the list of works in a fair hand and it is not his hand, it has been in somebody else's hand for about four seasons, and he has known that for the whole of the four.** A book-keeper of thirty-eight reads his paper to him in a yard for the third time, says first that he has got very good at it, and then says the reason he is doing it: **you asked me what a day under it meant in the second week of the first month of the year after next and you have not asked anybody since, and a man who has asked one question and not been answered is a man who stops asking, and I am not having that on my own bank.** He puts a mark in chalk on the wall at the height of the third link, and the man asks what the mark is for, and it is marked as a plain question, and the answer is that the mark is for the book-keeper and that there is nothing in this empire that keeps it, and that in about four years a man with a copy of the right page could put that cross and that wall and that yard together and ask a question about them, **and he would be the only person who could answer it, and the answer would be that a man of fifty-five asked him a question in a yard on the second day of the third week of the sixth month of the year after next, so I put a mark that means nothing on a wall that nobody looks at.** And then he tells him where he got it: a clerk of thirty-four in this city says the same thing about hands, and a woman of sixty-three put his name into a letter in the fourth week of the third month of the year after next and asked in her own hand that it not be written down. **And: you are the only person in this empire who has asked me a question this year that I have wanted to be asked, and I am telling you that at about the fourth hour of the afternoon in a yard because I will not say it anywhere else.** Then four minutes about the chain: it came off a gate that came down and a man of about thirty-eight cut the link out with a cold chisel and would not take anything; he mends things; a woman at the second end brings him a thing about twice a week and has never said please; **and here is how he reads a hand without reading a paragraph — a fair hand that comes down that road on a counting day is not the fair hand of the man whose name is in the company book, a foreman's voice goes up on the last word and a book-keeper's does not, and a paper folded by a man has the crease at the bottom and a paper folded by a woman has it at the top.** Then the second question, and it is the first question he has ever asked anybody in nine years: **does it cost you anything. Nine years. Somebody has been writing my name at the foot of a line four or five times a season and I have watched every one of them go on and I have never asked once.** And the answer, given properly because it was worth asking: about four seconds, somebody has to hold forty-one names while a foreman reads them out, four seconds of a week is a thousandth of a day's pay, and he has not been paid for one of them and is not going to be. Then the first reason, said out loud for the first time: **I have not been asked anything in nine years and I have got very good at not being asked anything, and it is not kindness — a man who cannot read a paragraph cannot be served with a paper, and a man who cannot be served with a paper cannot be sent for anything, and a man who cannot be sent for anything is worth nothing to anybody in this empire, and that is the best thing that has ever happened to me and I have never once been sorry about it.** And the book-keeper's finding of the second week of the tenth month of the year after is referred to once as standing, and he says he is not going to explain it in a yard and the man is not going to ask. Then the third reason in this matter, and it is new, and it is the reader of seventeen's: **I am going to say a thing about her and I am saying it to you and not to her, and I want you to notice that I am saying it to a man who writes forty-one names down for a living and not to a foreman.** She is on a written engagement at the same rate as me, not thanked, not going to start, nobody has ever spoken to her about the season, and I have watched about four people in this bay be answered by a foreman out loud for nine years and she is the only one of us who is paid to do the answering and she is not allowed to. **If a person of seventeen reads a hold out loud in that bay then the thing that has been running here for nine years stops, because a thing a person does for money is a thing somebody can write down, and the first thing anybody is going to write down is a name, and the name is not going to be mine.** And the book-keeper's answer, after a minute and a half: **that is a better reason than mine, and it is better than the one a foreman of fifty-one has got, and it is better than the one I have got for not asking her; I am not going to improve it and I am not going to put it in that ledger; and I am not going to tell you why not, because a column of mine has no names in it and a reason of yours has a man in it, and a document with a man in it is a document about the man.** And: **you have got one and yours is about nine years of your own life and mine are about a piece of paper and a girl, and a man with three bad reasons and a man with one good one are not two halves of a thing.** And then, at the end, the half-sentence told back to him by a man who cannot read a paragraph and who will not use it: **about four hundred yards up that road there is a woman who owes you half a sentence and you have wanted it since the third month of the year after, and I am telling you I said it and I am not asking you anything about it, and if you tell me about it then I am a person who has been told something and I have been left alone for nine years, and that is the first thing that has ended in nine years and I am not going to have it ended over a woman's half a sentence.** The close: a mark in chalk on a wall, and two people who have said a thing out loud to each other in a yard and neither of them is going to say it a second time, and about four people in that bay have still never been asked what the hold is, and the count is four with the full recital, and the third of the four was the first one anybody did on purpose.

## Chapter 234 — remainder of the entry

**A letter came on the fourth day of the first week of the sixth month of the year after next and it is the first document in this matter anybody has ever been able to put his name near.** It is four lines and a signature, and the signature is a name he does not know and has never heard and is not the name of any office. **The four lines ask him to come and look at a thing on a bank four hundred and thirty miles down this river; they say the person writing does not know what it is; they say nothing will be paid and the carriage will be paid; and the fourth line says that if he comes he will not be asked to sign anything and not asked to speak to anybody, and that if he does not come there is nothing in the fourth line at all.** He has read it about nine times. He has not answered it and has not burned it, and he has thought about burning it for about four days, and a document with his name at the head of it is worse than the word he will not write down, because about nine hundred documents already have that word in a hand and every one of them belongs to a person with a trade. **And the finding, worked out standing up in about a minute and a half: a letter is a document, a document is the only thing anybody in this empire can be held to, it came to a rented room because somebody knew he was in it, and the person who wrote it can be found, because a person who writes a letter is a person with a hand — and he has been the one who cannot be found since the first week of the first month of the year after next, and it is the first time that finding has been useful to somebody else.** Then the cost: about four hundred and thirty miles each way, about six days, a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week at the other end, and **he does not do the sum out loud, because the answer has not changed since the third month and because a man who works an answer out loud every time he hears the question is a man who has begun to want one particular answer.** About forty-four pounds a year, thirty days of it, no fund, no form for a fund, and a man who goes is a man who has decided that his own wages are the fund. Then the part he had not got to on the first day, at about the seventh hour on the fourth day: **whoever wrote it is as lost as he is**, and there is a bank four hundred and thirty miles down this river with about nineteen reasons in a column and about four men in a bay who cannot read a paragraph, and a foreman who has been in a box since the seventh month of the year after and has not been asked about it, and a book-keeper whose wage is held against a roof he put there himself, and a man of about forty in a furnace town four hundred and thirty miles the other way who is holding the whole of a woman's money and cannot say why and has never once been asked. **And then the connection, in about four minutes, which is the batch's best new one: a labour he can be refused is a man somebody can pay for a day, and a man somebody can pay for a day is a man somebody can tell to stop, and about four hundred and thirty miles up this river there is a girl of seventeen on a written engagement at forty-five pence a day and four days a week who is not thanked, and the reason nobody on that bank is going to ask her to read the hold out loud is that a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop. That is the same instrument, and it has taken him four days and about nine readings to get from one to the other, and he has not met one person who has both ends.** Then a knock at about the ninth hour and he does not open it, and it is a woman of about thirty-five who keeps a day-book in a lodging house who has come about nine miles with two things, one of which is a question, marked as a plain question and not about a word on a form: **what does a person who cannot be found have that a person who can be found does not have.** And the answer: a person who cannot be found can be given a thing to do and cannot be charged for it, cannot be sent a paper about it, and cannot be stopped, and **everything else about this is worse for me and none of the rest of it is in that list** — and he adds that he cannot be thanked, cannot be paid, cannot be instructed, and cannot be cleared either, because the only paper that would clear him does not exist in a form a person can hold, and can be got out of his own door by nobody. She says that nobody heard the prices he says in rooms, that she put a woman's name at the head of a thing once this year and has thought about it every week since, and that there is nothing to put at the head of anything about him, **because if she got on a cart tomorrow she could say the whole of it to a carter, and that is the whole of the findability of him.** Then he says out loud that he is not going to decide at about the ninth hour on the fourth day of a week in which a stranger's letter arrived, **because a man who decides what he is at about the ninth hour on the day a piece of paper comes has not decided what he is, he has decided about the paper**, and he quotes the twelfth line and Tamsin Rook's sentence back from memory, and says he has not been able to think of one thing to put on it. The close: he does not answer the letter and does not burn it, **and the reason is the only thing he has this week that is not a repetition — if he put a second document in the stove then in about four years the only two documents anybody can put side by side about this matter would be one he did not write and one he did not keep, and that proves nothing and costs a stove**, and the letter goes in the inside pocket of the coat, and the count of the four has not moved.

## Chapter 235 — remainder of the entry

Her wage stopped in the second week of the third month of the year after and the word for it is *held*, and **the money for the nineteen days she has worked this year is in a weigh-house ledger in a furnace town with her wage in it and not her name under it.** The sixteenth book was asked for on the second day of the second week of the fourth month of the year after next by a woman of about thirty whose door the water came to and who nobody came to see; she has decided to keep it, it is unpaid, there is no fee and no day rate, nobody is to be told, and her own name is at the head of it on purpose in about four seconds. The woman of about thirty comes back with two numbers wrong and cries about the second one. The street-spelling argument is restated and shortened: the street goes in the way the water office spells it, because a book with a wrong door in it is a letter about four people going to a street that has not got them in it, **and nobody in this empire you could thank for keeping a book for nothing, because nobody has ever drawn up a form for a person to be thanked for keeping a book.** Then a man of about twenty-eight, the fourth person to have been in that room in about a year, with a sum wrong in his mother's house-book by two shillings and sixpence out and four shillings and threepence in: **a book with a mistake in it and no day against it is a book that is lying, and a book with a mistake in it and a day against it is a book that was wrong on one day and has been right about being wrong ever since, and there is nothing anywhere in this empire that says the second one is not to be used.** And when he asks about the name at the head of it: **I know whose name it is and I am not going to tell you, because about nine hundred and forty names in a book in a building four hundred and thirty miles from this room came off buildings like that one, and I said out loud in a rented room in the second week of the fourth month of the year after next that a door with a name on it is a thing that can be carried out of a room, and I am not going to be the second one.** She writes the day and the two figures and nothing under them, reads it back once, and does not put her own name at the foot of it. Then her brother's letter of the sixteenth of the eighth of last year, on the shelf behind the sixteen books, unanswered, and hers to leave unanswered: **there is a thing people do where they keep a letter for about four years and then say that they could not answer it, and that is a lie and everybody who does it knows it is a lie while they are doing it, and if she ever answers it she will answer it on a day when she has nothing else in her hands, and she has had nothing else in her hands on about nine days out of about four hundred.** Then the foreman off the dye end comes up two stairs for the third time, does not shut the door, does not sit down, and has a coat on. **Before you say it: the wage is held, and he has been told to tell her and has been told it since the first week of the fourth month of last year and has said it twice and has never once been told what it is held against and there is no form and there is not going to be one and I have stopped waiting to be told.** *You have not sent the line.* **No, and I have worked out why in about a minute and a half on a stair:** a line with the day on it is a document, a document is the only thing anybody can be held to, he has never in forty years been held to one thing that was true, and in about four years a person can put the day next to a wage and ask who held it and on what and there is nobody to answer and it will be you and me. And her answer: **that is right and it took you a minute and a half and it has taken me two years and I have never said it out loud to anybody.** And then the instruction, which is new: **write it, keep it, do not send it — put the day on it and the word held and nothing else and no name, and keep it in the inside of that coat, and when a day comes that you want it to exist, send it, and on that day it stops being a thing you have thought and starts being a thing that has happened.** And: **that is not a reason anybody has ever had for writing one down. It is a reason I have just made up in a room in about four seconds, and everything I have for money is a thing somebody else can stop and everything I have for nothing is a thing nobody can produce, and I have sixteen books and one of them has my name at the head of it, and that is not a good position to be in.** Then the count, in full, with the four itemised and dated, and **the asymmetry, and the two she has not asked: a woman of about sixty with a board and a basket four hundred and thirty miles up this river who is the only person alive who can say how a box got filled and who does not know, and a clerk of thirty-eight at a window four hundred and thirty miles down it who has no fund and no form in this empire has ever been drawn up for one.** And the work on other people's numbers is not paid and nobody ever told her and she did not know she was waiting to be told. And the last thing, said to the sixteen books: **he cannot be found and that is the only thing he has got, and a person who cannot be found can be given a thing to do and cannot be charged for it, and I have had a wage held since the second week of the third month of the year after by a man who is the only person in this city who can pick it up or put it down and who cannot tell me why.** And: she is not going up that river this year and has not said she is never going to, and the count is four.

## Chapter 236 — remainder of the entry

He is in the room with a sheet folded in three, which is what a man does when he has written something and has not decided to have written it. **Put it on the counter and then go and stand by the window and do not read it while I am reading it.** The sheet is blockquoted on the page, four lines, undated in the sense that it carries the day of the thing and not the day it was written, and it says: on the fourth day of the fourth week of the eighth month of the year after next he is going to fill the twelfth line in his own book and read it back twice; a day after that he is going to answer a letter of four lines that came to his room on the fourth day of the first week of the sixth month of the year after next; he is going to do both without being paid for either; there is nobody in this matter who has to agree to it and he has not asked anybody; he has not got a fund, he has got about forty-four pounds a year and a road of about four hundred and thirty miles, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a fund; **and the rule of the twelfth month of last year, used once, and this is the use.** She reads it twice, the second reading slower. Her answer is eleven words and then about nine minutes: **the second half, no; the first half, yes, on the day you have written.** And the reason: a fact and a decision are not the same thing, a fact can only ever be repeated and a decision can be taken back, and if he does both in about nine days then in about four years a person with a copy of the right page can put the two lines together and say that a man who wrote a fact about himself on one day is a man who decided on another, **and that is not a true thing and I am not going to let it be in a room I have been standing in.** And the instruction: leave the second half in his own hand, there is no copy of it anywhere, she has not taken a copy and is not going to, he answers the letter on a day that is not a day he has written about
