himself on, **and she is not going to tell him which day, and that is not a withholding, because a person who is told which day is a person who has had the day taken off him, and she has watched that happen to a foreman of fifty-one about four hundred and thirty miles down this river and it was the wrong thing to do and the foreman has said so.** And the price, said out loud, and nobody thanks her: **I have said the price of this out loud in about four rooms this year and a handful of people have heard it and not one of them has told anybody else, and the reason that is a good number is that it is a number of people and not a number of rooms.** Then the ordinary work of a notary's counter, which is on the page for the first time in the volume: a copy at fourpence read back twice, a question at a shilling she has not done of her own in about a year, a certificate at four shillings she has never issued, **a fourth thing on the fee board that neither she nor two clerks can account for**, a man of about forty who paid fourpence and said thank you and was told that is the ordinary way and is not a favour, and a woman of about thirty who asked whether a copy would stand if the paper it came from was lost and was told no and did not buy one. **And there is one man who has been coming up those stairs for about a year and has never asked her anything, and she has worked out that he is not shy and that he is working something out at the same rate she is and that neither of them is going to say so, and the reason she has not asked him one question in four goes is that a woman of fifty-five at a counter about nine miles off has been saying out loud for about a year that nobody has ever asked her anything, and Tamsin Rook has been on the other side of that counter wanting to be asked every working day since.** Then a man of about thirty with four words in his head and fourpence in his hand, who does not spend the fourpence and asks one plain question — **does a thing have to happen in a building to count** — is told no, and says that is what he thought and that one of his four words is her name and he is not going to say it, and that his name is not going to be in anything and he has a wife; and she does not charge him for the no. Then the two boxes again: she says the number of them is the question he is not going to get, and that a person who is safe because nobody can instruct her and useful because somebody needs her standing in a room is a person with two boxes filled in on the same form, which she said out loud in the third week of the seventh month of the year after and about nine people have since written down, and not one of them has asked whether there is a third, **and he is the second, and he is asking her for a different reason, and she is not answering it either.** Then the two instruments, kept apart and named out loud: **the watch is not in force and it is not spent, it requires only that a person who is not from the office that cannot be named be present, about six hundred people in this city could satisfy it, and nobody has ever been paid for satisfying it or thanked for it; the paper that puts her in his rooms is a different instrument and it is the older of the two, and neither has ever been merged**, and she will not say which she wished was the only one. Then the trust debt moving a fourth time and not being made: **you have not asked me once this year whether I trust your judgment and you are not going to, and two pieces of evidence is not a decision and three is not either, and I have said that in a doorway in a rented room in the third week of the third month of the year after next and in about nine other rooms since in other words, and I am not going to say it in this one because I have said it enough** — and the reason she picked the sheet up is the two boxes, and neither of the boxes is the reason she is in the room, and she has never once merged them. And the count of a rule: **a count is a thing that gets a heading put over it, and about nine people in this city have made a thing out of a habit in my time in these bays and every single one of them ended up with a heading over it**, and a man of about thirty carried a sentence out of a room into a street in the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next and there is no form in this empire that puts a street in a book. The close: four askings, two accidents, the third the first on purpose, the count unmoved, **and the man who asked the fifth has not been written down and the person who is holding the fifth has not been asked**, and a sheet of his is now in his own hand and not in a book of hers and not in a drawer and not in a room, and it is two things on a day and only one of them has a day on it.

## Chapter 237 — remainder of the entry

He has said the same thing to a woman of twenty twice, up two stairs, and been told to keep asking. Then a man of about thirty-four comes in at about the fifth hour, has come about four miles in the rain, and cannot finish a question, **and the foreman lets him stand there for about two minutes and does not help him, and says that was the decision she made at about the fifth hour and has not improved: every man in that end has come to her at some point and not been able to finish, and the one time somebody helped him finish he never came back, and she does not know that it was because of her and is not going to find out by helping one.** The man goes and does not come back and nobody knows where he went and she has not asked and there is no form in this empire that requires a man to say where he went. Then, at about the sixth hour, standing at the end of the dye end, without calling them together and without stopping the work, **because a foreman who stops the work to make a speech has begun to make a speech of her own job, he says it five times, one man at a time, in the middle of ordinary work: I am holding a wage and I cannot tell you what it is held against.** The first man says nothing and goes back to the vat. The second asks what wage. The third says the same words a man of about thirty said in a lane in the second week of the third month of the year after. The fourth is a man of about thirty who has just arrived, and a thing said to nine people is not a thing said to nine people, it is a thing said to the air. And the fifth is a boy of about nine with a slate, and it is on a slate, and neither of them has a slate. **And the reason, which is the best thing in the batch: a thing said to one person is a document, a document is the only thing anybody in this empire can be held to, and he has never in his life been held to one thing that was true, and he would like there to be one.** And: **a man is held to the things that are against him and nobody in this trade is held to a single thing they have said out loud in a room with the rest of the end in it.** And the cost, named: he is the only person in that city who can pick up or put down the whole of what she has, he has told nobody what he has just done and is not going to, there is no form for that either, and in about four years it can be repeated, and the only two people it can be repeated to are her and him, and she is twenty and he is forty and about four hundred and thirty miles of river is between them and neither is going to be able to help the other. And the blank-list year, said at about the seventh hour: **the sixth year of a guild's blank list, when there was no list of works at all and the whole trade ran for a season on what people were told at the gate and on what a man said in a yard, and he was about thirty-four and was the only person in that end who wrote anything down, and the book held days, and who was on and who was not, and about four names of men who said they would come and did not come — and he has not got the names and has not got the days, and that is the correct thing, and nobody in this empire pays a man to hold a list and everybody in this empire turns out to need one, and those two facts have never once been in the same room, and he has been in both of the rooms.** And then the letter, which is new: **a wage-day notice with a day on it and no name on it, saying a sum and not saying against what, in the inside pocket of his coat for four days, and he is holding a document that could be held to him and it is the first one in forty years, and he is not going to tell anybody, because about nine people heard him say out loud that he could not say what a wage was held against and a document would let him say it without a word.** And four days later the weigh-house sends word that the wage is held, which is what it has always said, and he says the same words back, **and it is a different thing, and the only difference was that nine men had heard him say the first one first.** The close names him: there is a man in a city about four hundred and thirty miles down this river that a woman of twenty walked about nine miles to on her own account to ask a question, and nobody sent her, and there is not one form in this empire for a person who comes on her own account, and he is a man of thirty-eight with no office and no fund and a bar of his own that came off him on the twelfth of the ninth month of last year on a line of his own writing, **and the foreman of a dye end has heard about him twice in about a year and does not know what he is called and is not going to find out, and that is a thing he has chosen and not a thing he lacks.** And the count is four, and nobody has thanked him and nobody is going to and there is no form in this empire that requires it.

## Chapter 238 — remainder of the entry

The list of works for the season after this one was read out in about four minutes and nobody agreed it and nobody refused anything, and the four are on it the way the four are on every list. **The water was down a foot and a bit from the sixth month and the hold had not moved since the fourth week of the fourth month of the year after.** About nineteen people said no again in the last week of the seventh month of the year after, one after another on a day, in about four minutes, with the same nineteen reasons, and every one went in the third column of that bay's own ledger with a day against it and no names in that column at all. **And then, on the fourth day of the fourth week of the seventh month of the year after next, she read them out.** All nineteen, in the bay, in the ordinary way, the second reading slower, nothing skipped, the names not read, because a name in a room is a thing that can be carried out of a room. And a man of about thirty from the second gang says *that is mine*, and she says it is, and that there is no name on any of the nineteen and she did not put one there and is not going to. *Then it is findable now.* **It is findable now. It has been in a book in a building four hundred and thirty miles down this river since the seventh month of the year after with a day against it, and in about four years a person with a copy of the right page could have put that day and that reason side by side and asked about a season a person had not been in — and for about three years nobody could have done that, because the column has no name in it and a reason does not carry a name and a man who gave one did not know that nine people in a bay were going to hear it read out.** And the finding: **a reason read out in a bay to nine men is findable in a way a name is not, because a name is one document in one hand in one building and a person with a copy of the right page has to have that page, and a reason is in nine mouths and every one of them is a person who can be asked and there is not one of them who could be stopped and no form in this empire says a man may not say out loud in a bay that a reason is his.** And the other half said in the same breath: a man findable by his own reason for about four minutes is also a man who has been on a list of works and answered by a foreman out loud for nine years, and about four men in that bay cannot read a paragraph and have never been asked anything, and being left alone has been good to them, and she has given one reason about that and three about herself and every one was bad and she is not going to hand a fifth one to a bay. **And then a man of about forty from the third gang stands up with the same reason, and she puts her hand out flat before he gets there and says she is not going to stop a man from saying a thing he has a right to say, and says that a second one of them makes it a habit and a habit grows a heading over it on its own, and that nineteen reasons and nine men is already more than anybody on that bank has ever had put about themselves.** He says it to the man of thirty after she has gone out of the bay, and about four men hear it, **and it is not in a book and not in a column and not a day, and there is no form in this empire that says a man may not say a true thing about himself to another man in a shed, and nobody has written it down and nobody is going to.** Then the man of about thirty catches her and asks whether she is going to read them again, and she does not know and says that a person who reads a column out loud twice in a season is a person who has made it a practice, and a practice grows a heading over it on its own without anybody deciding anything. Then the ordinary work: the man of about thirty-four and the hold, the man of about twenty-eight with the folded paper and two lists and a brother who cannot read a paragraph either, **and the answer to that, in full — if the two of them have about four lists between them then not one of the four knows about the other three, and it took him about four years to turn out to be the most useful thing anybody said to him that day**; the man of about fifty and the third link of the chain; and the man of about fifty from the fourth gang at about the seventh hour, and the bridge at Lowcross, and **that is not a reason the bridge knows about**, and *I know*, and *that is the reason I am still here and I have said it to you about nine times this year and I have counted them and nobody has ever asked me to count them out loud*, and **nobody is going to ask you to count them out loud; if they do there is not one form for it and you do not have to help them, and I have said that in this shed three times now and the third time is shorter and if you keep coming to that door I am going to run out of ways of saying it.** Then she goes up the road about four hundred yards to the hired stone store, and **does not knock, which is a thing she thought about for most of a week**, and stands in the doorway, and tells him she read the nineteen out loud and a man of about thirty said one of them was his and she did not know it was his and is not going to pretend she did not. He puts his hand flat on the ledger and does not open it, and says then there is a second kind of findable and it is worse than a name and it has been there since the seventh month of the year after. **And he does not ask her where she got the idea from. He wanted to, and he did not, and both of them knew it and neither said so, and that is the arrangement from the seventh month of the year after and it is not broken.** And then the half, and he says he has not asked and is not going to and would like it noticed in a doorway that he has wanted it since the third month of the year after and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to ask a foreman for the rest of a sentence, and she says **he has not asked, and that is the arrangement, and it is a habit, and I have watched about nine people in this empire make a thing out of a habit in my time in these bays and every single one of them ended up with a heading over it.** The close: a third season shut and nobody agreed it, about nineteen people behind the bank, about nineteen reasons read out loud in a bay and one of them belonging to a man who knew it in about four seconds and no column that will hold what that is; **and there will not be another season in which a foreman of fifty-one could ask one of the four men who cannot read a paragraph a question and be answered for nothing, and nobody decided that and nothing was resolved, and it is a foreman of fifty-one and a bank about four hundred and forty foot long and a woman who has asked the same question of the same bay for about four seasons and has not been asked one back.** The count is four, the third was the first on purpose, and the lamp is not lit in either building.

## Chapter 239 — remainder of the entry

**It is the ninth day since, and that is the whole of what he has done about it, and he wants that said first, because a man who has spent nine days thinking about a thing has not done anything about it.** The season's list was read out in about four minutes on the second day of the first week of the eighth month and he is on it, and the four are on it the way the four are on every list. Then he goes up the road about four hundred yards to the store at about the seventh hour and asks the first question before he is inside the door, and it is marked as a plain question: **can it be taken out of the book.** And the answer is no, and it is not in a book the way a name is in a book, it is in nine mouths, and **he can put a hand against a line in the ledger and take the hand off and the line is still there, and he cannot put a hand against what a foreman said out loud in a bay at about the fourth hour of the afternoon in front of nine men, and neither can she, and she is the person who said it and she is not sorry about it and she told me so in a doorway — and that is a worse wall than a name and it is the same wall, and I have said the other half of that sentence in about four rooms this year and nobody has ever once heard the two halves in the same room.** Then he says *you could have stopped her*, and the book-keeper gives both halves: he could have told her not to read them out and did not, and the other half is that nine days ago neither of them knew it was going to be read out, **and if he had come up on the fifth day of the fourth week of the seventh month of the year after next he would have said the same no and the man would have said it was worth it and he would have said it was worth it and nine men in that bay would have had nothing to say about it at all — and you have not been done a thing by her, you have been done a thing by the fact that a bay has nine men in it, and that is a thing neither of you can take out of a bay.** Then the decision, and it is the man's: **I am not going to ask her to take it back**, and he wants that said out loud in a store by a man who writes things down, and he wants the day on it, and he is not going to sign it and does not want a copy and does not want anybody to have it. **And the book-keeper refuses to write it down, and the refusal is better than the decision: a column of mine has no names in it and if I write what you have just said down then I have built one, and about four hundred entries in that ledger have a reason under them and every one of the four hundred is about a day and not about a person, and you have just handed me a reason about a person, and I am not going to take it. And then: it went out of a bay and into nine people and there is nowhere in this empire for it to go back to, and that is the first thing anybody in this matter has said to me that I have not got a better answer to in about four seconds.** Then the second question — *is one of the nineteen one of the four* — and the three-part refusal: **I do not know, and I cannot find out, and I am not going to try**, and the reason, given last because the question was asked in that order: the only way he could find out is to put a name against a reason, and then he would have made a list of the four and he would have to look at it, and there is a finding in this ledger in his own hand in the second week of the tenth month of the year after with the day on it, and it is not his finding to improve and it is not going to be improved by a man who wants to be helpful. Then the third question — *is the reason in the book that goes down the river* — and the mechanism in full: his reason is in this book with the day against it and about nineteen others beside it, this book goes into a company book in a building about four hundred and thirty miles from here on the first day of every month, that company book has about nine hundred and forty names in it in nine hands over about sixty years and about four hundred of them are dead, **and nobody there can put the two together, and that has been the case since the seventh month of the year after, and a reason and a name are in two documents about four hundred and thirty miles apart and there is nothing anywhere in this empire that joins them, and I built that column with no names in it on purpose in the second month of next year for exactly that reason, and I have not got one thing to be proud of in it.** And then the answer to how he got found: **you did not get found. A foreman read a column out loud in a bay in about four minutes and a man of about thirty heard his own reason come out of her mouth and said so out loud in front of nine people, and that is the whole mechanism, and the only person in this matter who has ever put a name next to a reason is a woman of fifty-one, and she did it out loud, on purpose, about four days ago.** And then, in his own mouth, on a step: **he is a person who cannot be found twice over, and neither of the two ways is anything to do with him; he had spent nine years being a man nobody knew anything about and it had turned out to be the ordinary way of this empire and not his own care; the only thing that had ever protected him was a column with nothing in it that another man had built on purpose; and about four days ago a foreman took it apart out loud in about four minutes, and there was no form and there is not going to be one and nobody is going to ask her to put it back.** Then the ordinary day, and the two men: a man of about fifty comes in and asks about the third link of a chain and is answered in about two minutes from behind the bench without turning round; **two men on that cut worked a wall that is on a district's list, at a shilling a day, in the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after, and both have said out loud since that they ought to have been on a wall that is not on a list where the rate is three shillings, and one of them argued it four times in a shed and lost four times and has not said since that it was worth it, and a man who says a thing was worth it after it has cost him four days of a shilling is a man who has stopped counting, and neither of the two has been made whole and neither is going to be.** Then the standing facts: the factor's letter, Lowcross at nineteen pounds three and fourpence with no line against it in nineteen years, the guarantee unanswered on about four hundred and forty foot with about sixty children under sixteen inside it, the nineteen who said no again, the four who cannot read a paragraph who have never been asked anything and are not going to be, **and that there will not be another season in which a foreman of fifty-one could ask one of them a question and be answered for nothing, and that is not a decision anybody made.** And: he can read hands, he cannot read a paragraph, he has been in that bay about nine years, he is one of the four, **and he is the one of the four that a foreman has answered in about four seconds, out loud, twice a week, for nine years, without being asked, and he has never once said thank you and has never once been asked to and nobody at the district works has ever in nine years asked him to read anything.** And a gate link mended with a piece of wire and no tool in about four minutes, and a man of about twenty-six who said he could not do that, and his answer that he could not read either and the two of those are the same thing in a way nobody has ever put in a form. The close: the count of four, unmoved, and him four hundred and thirty miles down the river with no office and no fund, **and nobody in nine bays on a bank about four hundred and forty foot long who could say what he is called, and about four hundred and thirty miles of river is between him and every one of them, and there is no fund, and the ninth day is not finished.**

## Chapter 240 — remainder of the entry

**He says those four things out loud in about four rooms and says them again to himself at about the fifth hour of the morning because the twelfth line was going to be filled in about four seconds and a man who fills in a twelfth line ought to know what he has got.** He has not eaten and has not been out of the room since the second day of the fourth week and there is a woman who brings him bread on about the sixth hour on about four days a week and he has not opened the door on any of them that week, and about four feet of bare wall is not a plan and there is a nail in it about four feet up that came with the room and has nothing hanging on it. **The twelfth line has been empty since the third week of the ninth month of the year after next, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a man to fill a twelfth line and there never was one that required him to leave it empty, and that is the whole difference between the two things and nobody in this matter can pay for it.** He reads the eleven going back, and the fourth, and the ninth, and the eleventh twice, and the eleventh is the one about the four definitions and it has a day against it from the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after next and he put that in himself in one line. **He put the pencil down and it was about nine minutes before he wrote anything, and he wants that said, because a man who says he sat for nine minutes before writing a line is telling you what the nine minutes were for, and they were for the four feet of wall.** Then the cost of the fact, out loud: **a fact on a line in a book with no office on it is the worst of the two, because a question can be answered and a fact can only be repeated**, and that is the reason for everything he is about to do and the reason he had not done it in about nineteen weeks. He writes it in about four seconds, and reads it back once and reads it back again, and the second reading was slower, **and that is his rule and it came from a girl of seventeen who is paid to read and is not paid to think, and he has never told anybody that, and a foreman of fifty-one worked it out standing in a bay about four hundred and thirty miles down this river in about nine seconds and told nobody either.** Then the whole of it, standing up at about the fifth hour with the lamp not lit: **a fact and a decision are not the same thing, and a woman of twenty-four told him that in a rented room in the third week of the fourth month of the year after next and gave him an answer in a room in words with a date on it, and the answer was that he was to do the fact on one day and the decision on another and not in one letter, and he did it in one letter and she refused half of it and he has not improved on the refusal.** And: **it is not empty. It is the only honest thing in there and it is the eleventh line and it is true, and it does not tell anybody what I am, it tells them what I am not, and that took nineteen weeks and this morning and about four seconds of pencil.** And then the choice, at about the eighth hour: **not the other one — a labour he can be refused, and I have chosen it, and I chose it at about the fifth hour of the morning on the fourth day of a week, and a woman of twenty-four told me in a rented room that a man who decides what he is at about the ninth hour on the day a piece of paper arrives has not decided what he is, and she is right, and this is not that hour and this is not that day.** And the cost, named: a man who is a labour is a man somebody can pay for a day, a man somebody can pay for a day is a man somebody can tell to stop, there is no fund and no form in this empire for a fund, he has got about forty-four pounds a year and a road of about four hundred and thirty miles each way and about six days, and the day comes out of his own money and not anybody else's. **So I have taken the shape of the thing that can be refused and I have removed the only person in this empire who could have refused me, on purpose, and out of my own hand, and that is a worse position than the other one, and I have known it since the third week of the fourth month of the year after next when a woman of twenty asked me whether anybody could protect me, and I said no and I meant it, and I did not tell her that the reason nobody can protect me is that I have just arranged for there to be nobody able to stop me.** Then a knock at about the seventh hour and a man of about thirty-four who has come about four miles and wants to know whether a thing said in a room can be held to, marked as a plain question, and the answer through two inches of door: **it can be held to and it cannot be asked about, those are two instruments and they are not the same, a person cannot be sent a paper about a thing said in a room and a person can be sent a paper about a thing they wrote down, and everything in this matter that anybody can be held by, he wrote down himself** — one of those things from the second week of the third month of the year after next and one of them older, and neither has a name at the foot of it. And when he asks who the woman of about twenty-four is: **not somebody who is going to be told by me, and I am aware that is a sentence that only a man who is not findable can say with any comfort, and I have said it to a door twice now and I am going to stop.** Then the letter, at about the tenth hour on the last day of that week, and **the road is walked zero times in this and is not going to be walked in this**: a sheet with no name at the top of it and no name at the foot of it and a day in it, and a day with nobody's name on it is not a summons to anybody, written in about eleven minutes, and the reason he signs nothing is that a hand is the only thing anybody in this matter has ever been able to use, and a man with a hand is a man who can be found, and he is the one person in this matter that nobody can find. **And then the one person he could have told, and did not, and the reason is not protectiveness: a woman of about thirty-five who keeps a day-book in a lodging house came about nine miles on the fourth day of the third week of the sixth month of the year after next and stood on a step and told him not to put anything at the head of anything about him, and she is findable and he is not, and a person who is findable holding a fact about a man who is not is a person who can be produced with the fact, and that is the whole of the asymmetry a woman of twenty worked out standing in a rented room in the third week of the fourth month of the year after next, and he has had since then to find a way round it and there is not one.** And then the last of it: **a letter is the only instrument in this matter that a man nobody can find can put into a room; a form goes to a counter, a roll goes to a building, a list of works goes into a company book four hundred and thirty miles down a river in nine hands; nobody has ever had to think about a thing that could be put in a room by a man with nothing, because there has not been one, and there is one now and it is in about nine days on about four hundred and thirty miles of road. And it costs a day of a wage of seventeen shillings and a penny the week, and there is no fund, and there is nobody who can stop me, and I am not going to be able to say afterwards that anybody made me do it, and that is what I have bought and it is the only thing I have bought in about four years and I did not pay for it in money.** The close: the roll-call of everything on that bank, and **a man of thirty-four in a rented room has a twelfth line in a book with no office on it and a day against it, and a line in a letter with a day in it and no name at the top of it and no name at the foot of it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for either, and about four years is how long either of them can be found for.** Four askings in about a hundred and forty years, two of them accidents, the third the first one anybody did on purpose, the count unmoved since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, no post whose job is to ask, and **a decision is not a question and a question is not a decision and he has got one of them and has not got the other.**

## Notes on these ten summaries

**The word counts in the headings are measured per file with `len(text.split())` after every edit was finished, and every one of the forty files in the volume returns the identical figure under `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w`; the volume total is the sum of the forty per-file figures, because concatenating the forty files before counting merges a word pair at three chapter boundaries and returns a total three words short: the batch total is 28,189 and the ten of them are 2,911 / 2,835 / 2,727 / 2,906 / 2,893 / 2,836 / 2,803 / 2,845 / 2,693 / 2,740, all ten inside the 2,700 to 3,200 band, lowest 2,693, highest 2,911, and nothing was trimmed to reach a number.** **Volume 05 stands at 115,528 across forty chapters and the manuscript at 826,602 across two hundred and forty.** Where a summary and the prose disagree, the prose is the authority, and every figure, date and count in these ten summaries was read off the finished files rather than off a plan.

## CORRECTION TO THE CHAPTERS 231–240 SUMMARIES ABOVE, AFTER THE SECOND REVIEW REPAIR

**Appended and not written over, because this file is append-only downward and the standing rule is that where a summary and the prose disagree the prose is the authority. Nothing above is deleted and nothing above is a record of a fault: the figures in the ten headings are the ones the writing run measured, and the writing run's own audit had already moved some of the prose under them, and the second review moved more.**

**One. The word counts in the ten headings are superseded. The ten files as they stand measure 2,911 / 2,856 / 2,736 / 2,906 / 2,910 / 2,836 / 2,810 / 2,844 / 2,521 / 2,788, a batch total of 28,118, Volume 05 at 115,457 and the manuscript at 826,531, measured with `len(text.split())` and confirmed with `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w`, which agree to the word on all ten files.** Five chapters did not move: 231, 234 and 236 are identical, and 232, 233, 235, 237 and 238 each moved by a few words on a punctuation or a rewording. **Chapter 239 moved by 172 and Chapter 240 by 48, and those two are the ones with repairs behind them.** The claim in the notes above that concatenating the forty files before counting returns a total three words short still stands and was not re-derived.

**Two. Chapter 233's summary says *He puts a mark in chalk on the wall at the height of the third link*, and the pronoun was on the page as well as here.** **The mark is the book-keeper's and always was: Rennick Adley put it there, the chain is on the man and not on the wall, and the cross means nothing and is nobody's, which is what the chapter's own answer and its closing paragraph already said. The sentence on the page is rewritten and the summary is wrong only in its pronoun, not in its substance.**

**Three. Chapter 237's summary says *that was the decision she made* and *every man in that end has come to her* and *she does not know that it was because of her*, and the foreman is a man of about forty and the prose has *he* in all three places.** The chapter's own opening paragraph and the whole of its body agree, and this summary does not, and the prose is the authority. **A summary written out of a chapter in a hurry will get a person's gender wrong three times in one paragraph, and that is a reason to read the prose and not a reason to doubt it.**

**Four. Chapter 240's summary is unaffected by the repair to the decision, and the reader should know what changed anyway: the man who says he is going down the road at about the sixth hour in the morning now withdraws that in his own mouth, in the same breath, and says he is going to put one sheet of paper on the road on the last day of the week and let the sheet go down it and not he.** The choice, the cost, the twelfth line, the letter and the road walked zero times are all where the summary says they are. **And Chapter 240's opening no longer shares thirty-nine words with Chapter 234's; the room, the table, the chair, the bed, the bare four feet of wall and the hour are all still there and the two chapters no longer say them the same way.**

**Five. One convention the ten summaries do not record, because it is not a fact about a chapter: the batch prints thirty question marks, all in bare short lines in the mouths of people, and none of them is a question about a word on a form.** The batch had none at all when the writing run closed it, and a reader of these summaries should not read the absence of a question mark in a quotation as evidence that the line is not a question.
