The batch is 28,573 and the ten of them are 2,744 / 2,796 / 2,861 / 2,782 / 2,720 / 2,790 / 2,756 / 2,800 / 3,105 / 3,219, ten of ten inside the 2,700 to 3,200 band, and Chapter 0250 at 3,219 is nineteen words over the top of it and was not trimmed to reach the number. Volume 05 stands at 144,030 across fifty chapters and the manuscript at 855,104 across two hundred and fifty. Where a summary and the prose disagree the prose is the authority, and every figure, date and count below was read off the finished files.**

## Chapter 241 — remainder of the entry

**A man of about thirty-four comes about four miles and asks him whether anybody has ever asked a person out loud what a word on a form means, and marks it out loud as a plain question, which is the first time he has ever said that sentence.** The chapter's work is that the four of the first four are put together once, in a yard, in mouths. He refuses to let them be written: **a list of four things that have happened in a hundred and forty years is a list, and a list has a heading, and the heading is either *things that have happened* or *things somebody should have done*, and about nine people in this city could write either one and both would be true and neither would be any use.** He recites them in the wrong order on purpose and says so, giving each a date and giving the first no age: a court room in the fifth month of the year after that wanted a record; a clerk's window in the first week of the tenth, by a man frightened about his own household; a desk in the second week of the eleventh, by a woman of twenty who walked about four miles and nobody sent her, and **the third was the first one anybody did on purpose**; and a rented room in the first week of the first month of the year after next, by the same woman, who walked about nine miles in a day and asked a man with no office what *engaged* means and was answered with a price and a refusal. He gives the number: **four in about a hundred and forty years, and there is no post anywhere in this empire whose job is to ask.** The man of about thirty-four writes the number on the back of his own left hand in a pencil that is nearly blunt. The man with the chain says he is the only person on that bank that findability does not work on, that he has known it for about four years, that he has never once used it, and that this is the first time and he wants it said out loud that he knows what he is doing. He gives the same four-seconds answer to *does it costs anything* he has given before. He writes the four on the inside of his own hat, which is the only place any of them has ever been, and does not read them back a second time because a man who has only had it in his head for twenty minutes will not get it right the second time either. He tells the man of about thirty-four that his reason for not being asked is the best thing that has ever happened to him, and that it is not about a form and not about a girl of seventeen and he is not going to say it twice in a year. The bay goes on being a bay and about nine men in it heard none of it.

## Chapter 242 — remainder of the entry

A man of about thirty-four comes about four miles and asks her what an entry in a box does to a life. **She turns round, which she does perhaps four times in a week, and says the reason for answering in a bay and not in a doorway: she has four reasons why nobody finds out that she is in a box, three are about other people and one is about her, and the one about her is that she is the only person on that bank who can say what an entry in a box does to a life and that being the only one is a door.** She has been standing in front of that door since the seventh month of the year after and has told one person and is telling a bay now because a man of about thirty-four walked about four miles and because she is tired of being the only one who knows. **She will not say what the box is for: a person who is entered in a box and then asked how it feels has been asked twice, and about nine hundred people in this empire have been entered in one this year by a class, and she has not asked any of them and is not going to.** Then she says the second thing: **there is one person in that bay who could read a hold out loud in about four minutes, and she is at the back, and she is seventeen, and she is on a written engagement at the rate the list is set at, and nobody has thanked her and nobody is going to. I have given three reasons in a shed for not asking her and every one of them was a bad one, and I am not giving a fourth today, and I am not going to be told one either by anybody in this room who is not one of the four of them.** The man of about thirty-four asks how anybody ever finds out, and she prints the count and says no post in this empire has that as its job, and warns him that a thing said in a bay is in nine mouths and cannot be taken out of nine mouths, and tells him how she did it on the fourth day of the fourth week of the seventh month of the year after next and is not sorry. Then the ordinary things, and a new one: a man of about twenty with a list of works from another cut, and her answer that two cuts do not share a list and a man can be on four of them and not one of the four knows about the other three, and that the truth is not fair and it is not her job to make it so. Then the guarantee, in a foreman's voice, in about four seconds, in a bay: **there are about four hundred and forty foot of that bank and about sixty children under sixteen inside it and about nineteen of us are not inside it, and the paper is about all of us, and it does not care which of us is which, and there is nobody in this empire I can ring up about it**, and she has read it and could not repeat one thing in it out loud and has been asked to describe it four times this year and has said no four times. Then the Lowcross bill at nineteen pounds three and fourpence and the two men who worked a wall on a district's list at a shilling a day where the rate on a wall that is not on a list is three, and neither of them is made whole. Then the nineteen who said no again. Then, in bold, the volume's promise stated on the page by a person: **a woman of twenty is four hundred and thirty miles up this river in a room two streets off a street that goes down to the river road with sixteen books on a table and a wage that has been held since the second week of the third month of the year after, and she is in a box too, and she has been in one since the fourth day of the third week of the fifth month of the year after, and she said no out loud in a room with the day on it and the no did not work, and a piece of paper with a seal on it told her that no further consent of that person is required. And the foreman of this bay is in a box as well, and has been since the seventh month of the year after, and has just said so out loud in front of about nine men, and neither of the two has been asked anything, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that will un-fill a box, and the person who would administer one if there were one is a clerk of about fifty-five in an office about nine miles off a building who has never been asked anything either and would not be a villain about it.**

## Chapter 243 — remainder of the entry

**A seat is a thing in a room without being a person**, and she said that to a foreman's face in a shed in the fifth month of the year after next, and it is in one of the eleven books in her own hand with the day on it. She goes and looks for two days, on her own time, told nobody, unpaid, for a procedure that will un-fill a box. **There is not one.** She tells the two other clerks in the building, who are not in this matter, and one of them is about thirty and has been in that room four years and says he assumed it because assuming it is cheaper than looking, and she says it is about four years cheaper and about four years is how long a thing takes to be found in a book in that building. **Nobody in this empire has ever been asked to un-fill a box, and there is no form for it, and no form for asking whether one ought to be un-filled, and no form for asking a person who is in one whether they would like to be out of it.** She writes those three sentences on a loose sheet, reads it back once, and burns it, because a loose sheet in a drawer is a document and a document is the only thing anybody in this empire can be held to. **The reason there is no procedure is the reason there is almost nothing in this empire: a procedure is a machine for making sure the reason is not needed, and it does that by not asking.** About nine hundred entries came in on about nine hundred sheets and about nine hundred people are standing in a box under a class this year, and she has administered every one correctly. **And a class is a heading, and a heading is how a person becomes a company, and a company is a thing this empire can hold wages against and give letters to and take to law, and a man who is a person is a thing it can only send papers to.** That is the finding, and a clerk of fifty-five took two days in eleven books to reach it. The worse of the two is about her own job: **the first hand writes the body and the second hand signs underneath and answers for it, and the second hand is the only one of the three who can be asked, and nobody in this empire has ever asked the second hand**, because a clerk who advises nobody is a person nobody needs, and she would not want it different. Then she enters, in the second of the eleven books, on her own motion and at nobody's request, in her own hand, with the day on it, blockquoted on the page: *whether an entry made in a box by a class may be un-filled; and if it may not, by whom it is to remain; and if it may, on whose application, to whom that application is to be addressed, and by what hand it is to be received.* **Her own name at the foot of it, in her own hand, and the hour.** Naming a question is not answering it, the recording of a question is a document, and a minute book with a day on it and a clerk's name at the foot of it is the only protection a question has ever had in this empire; a clause was named once in a minute book in another building about four hundred and thirty miles off and has been unanswered in that building's own court for nine years, and if it had been said out loud in a room it would have been in nine mouths and answered by a wrong man in a shed. The other clerk of about thirty reads it standing up over her shoulder and says *you have written a question down* and she says *not an answer*, and **a minute with an answer in it is an order, and a clerk of this office has never entered an order in nineteen years and there is no form that lets her, and that is not modesty, that is the shape of the room.** He asks whether she is going to send it anywhere and she says there is nowhere to send it, there is a shelf, and that she has had four days to think about who she is going to tell and is not going to tell anybody, because a person who is told has been given a duty and a person who has been given a duty cannot stop. **The price of putting your own name at the foot of a question is that a question is the one thing in this empire that makes a person answerable, and she has been a clerk in a room where nobody can make her answer for anything for nineteen years, and now she has asked on paper and there is no instrument that will let her be asked whether she meant it.** And the last thing in the chapter, which she has never said to anybody: a document that proves the absence of a name is a thing this office can use, and there is a notice on a bench about four hundred and thirty miles down a river with a seal at the foot of it and nothing under the seal whose second line asks that no inference of any kind be drawn from the absence of any name, and **that is not discretion, it is that a clerk who puts a name into a room where a document proves there isn't one has used the only weapon the office has against itself, and about nine hundred entries a year would be very easy to stop.** The name is not said.

## Chapter 244 — remainder of the entry

Her wage stopped in the second week of the third month of the year after, the word for it is held, and the money for nineteen days is in a weigh-house ledger in this town with her wage in it and not her name under it. The sixteenth book was asked for in the fourth month of the year after next by a woman of about thirty whose door the water came to in the second month and went off again, and nobody came, and she decided to keep it, and there is no fee and no day rate, and her own name is at the head of it in her own hand with a day against it and no name on it. On the fourth day of that week she works out what she has been carrying: about four days a week of work nobody knew about, in a room two streets off a street that goes down to a river road, for nobody, on a wage that is not hers. **A person who is paid to keep a number is a person who can be told what the number is**, which she has known since she was seventeen and has never said out loud to a living soul, and there is no form anywhere in this empire for a book-keeper and the four of them get it done in kitchens and on steps. Then she names the unpaid work from two other ends she has been told about, and says she has not met either of them and is not going to. The foreman off the dye end comes up the two stairs for the fourth time, is about forty, has a coat on, and says the wage is held and that he has never once been told what it is held against. **He has not sent the line: he has had it in the inside of that coat since the fourth day of the third week of the seventh month of the year after next with a day on it and no name on it, and he has worked out in about four seconds on a stair that a document would let him say a thing out loud without a word, and he is not going to be able to do that.** He will not tell her what is on it, she will not ask, and both of them will be right about that for about four years. And then she says the thing: **I have never been paid for any of the sixteen, and the fourth week of the ninth month of the year after next is the week I have decided to stop carrying that quietly and to go and tell them, and I have four people to tell and I have told one of them and she has offered to pay me and I have said no.** The foreman takes his cap off. She says she said no because if she takes her money then she is a trade and a trade can be told what to do, and the woman cannot be told what to do, and **everything I have for money is a thing somebody else can stop and everything I have for nothing is a thing nobody can produce, and I have sixteen books and one of them has my name at the head of it, and that is the whole of what I have got and it is not a good position to be in.** He says he has got three bad reasons and hers is the best anybody has given him in about four years, and that if hers is good then his is worse, because he is a man holding a woman's whole wage who cannot say what it is held against. She tells him the reason she is telling them at all: **in about four years one of those women is going to have a question about a sum and she is going to be the only person who can answer it, and a person who has never once been told that anything in her house is held by her cannot be told that either, and the first thing she is going to ask is who holds it and on what, and she is not going to be able to answer that, and she would rather be standing in the room when it happens than not.** Then he asks about the notice in the coat and is refused a reason, and he says the day he wants it to exist he will send it, and on that day it stops being a thing he has thought and starts being a thing that has happened, and he will not be able to take it back, and that is the only reason anybody in this matter has ever had for writing one down. She tells the first woman, and the woman says **then you will not have it when I am gone**, and about four years is what she is talking about, and she says a person who keeps another person's numbers for nothing is keeping them for the four years and not for the person, and that the two are not the same thing and that she chose the four years on purpose out of sixteen books and not out of one. **And if somebody came up those stairs tomorrow with a sheet and a seal and asked her to account for the sixteen, she could not show a soul the fourth of them, and the woman she could not account for is the one whose street the water office spells two ways.** Then the volume's standing facts in one paragraph and a close: nobody was in the room for any of it, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that requires a person to be in a room when somebody else works out what they are, and no form that requires a person to be thanked for sixteen books and a refusal.

## Chapter 245 — remainder of the entry

A man of about thirty comes out of the bay and brings in a bundle of about nine things off a wet sill and puts it on the bench, and she tells him to put them on the bench and go home, and has thought since that she did that to nine men in nine years and to one man in particular about four years ago and has not worked out which she did it to for the sake of it. Eight of the nine are ordinary. **The ninth is a sheet of foolscap folded once, wet through at the fold, with no name at the top of it and no name at the foot of it, and there is nothing on the back of it, and there is a day on it and nothing else, and the day is the fourth day of the fourth week of the eighth month of the year after next, and a day with nobody's name on it is not a summons to anybody.** She reads it four times and the fourth is slower, which is a rule that belongs to a man about four hundred and thirty miles down this river and not to her and which she has never told anybody where she got. Then she looks at the fold. **A piece of paper folded by a man is folded with the crease at the bottom and a piece of paper folded by a woman is folded with the crease at the top, and she has folded about nine hundred pieces of paper in her life and opened about nine hundred that other people had folded, and nobody told her.** She walks about four hundred yards up the road to the hired stone store and puts it on the bench beside the other two: a notice of six numbered lines with a device cut into the head of it and a seal at the foot of it and nothing under the seal, there since the seventh month of the year after; and a factor's letter of four lines wanting the name of a person who may be served and that person's consent, which says without both that it cannot proceed and will not proceed, and is not going to be answered, and every day it is not answered is a day nobody has been entered. **Three pieces of paper on a bench about four hundred yards from nine men, and not one of the three can be answered, and two of the three are asking for a name and the third one has not got one to ask for.** Then the real work of the day: **a thing with no name on it cannot be served on anybody, and a thing that cannot be served on anybody cannot be opened, because opening it is a thing a person does to a thing that has been given to them, and nobody has been given this one**, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that says what a person does with a thing that has no name on it, and it sat on a sill in the wet for about four weeks and got softer and softer and came inside because the sill was wet. **That is not anybody's fault, because there is nobody, and that is not a scandal, it is a machine.** Then she works out what the sheet is: **a day with a person's name on it is a summons; a day with nothing on it at all is not a summons and is not an offer and is not a request; it is a fact about a day put into a building about four hundred and thirty miles from the day by a person nobody in that building can name, and there is nothing in this empire that can make anybody answer a fact.** And then she says out loud, to that bay, on a counting day, **a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop**, and stops dead, because the sentence is not hers and she has never once said it in that bay. A man of about thirty tells her she said it in a shed in the sixth month in front of nine of them, and she says she knows exactly where she said it and who was in it and will not be asked about it by a bay, because a bay is not a room and a room is not a book and there is no form that carries a thing from one to another, and **she has been carrying that sentence since a foreman of about forty said it to her in a shed in the second week of the sixth month and she did not know then that it was the sentence the whole of this stands on, and she will not say what is under it, because if she says what is under it then nine men in that bay are standing on it.** On the way back she works out what she is the only person alive who could be given evidence about: **a crease is not a name and a crease cannot be served on anybody and there is no instrument in this empire that could put a crease and a person together**, and the only thing about it that could be put in a column is the fold, and a column is the thing that turns a day into a man, and she is not going to be the one who does it. And she is not going to answer it, because the factor's letter can be refused by doing nothing and this cannot be refused at all, because there is nothing in it to refuse, and a thing that can only be ignored is a thing that has been put into a room where it cannot do anything, and that is what it was for, and a person who is not asked has not been given a duty. Then the ordinary things, one of them new: a man of about twenty-six comes back in and asks her whether she is all right, and she says there is a difference between a thing that has not been said and a thing that has not been said out loud, and that there is not one form anywhere in this empire that asks a person to put a value on the difference. And a man of about twenty-six asks who writes a name in a column, and she says nobody would know, and that is the correct arrangement, and about four people on that bank have worked it out this year and four of them are not on a list at all and she is not going to help.

## Chapter 246 — remainder of the entry

**The road is not being walked, it has not been walked once in this matter, and a sheet went down it on the last day of the fourth week of the eighth month of the year after next and he did not go with it.** A woman brings him bread on about the sixth hour on about four days in a week and has started to leave it on the step, which is a thing she had not done in about a year, and he has not opened the door since the second day of the fourth week of the eighth month. He gets up at about the fourth hour, puts his hand flat on the door, waits about four minutes, opens it, picks the bread up, says nothing to the step, and shuts it. **And that is the whole of what happened at the door.** Then the real work: there is a sheet of clean paper in the drawer of that table, there is one sheet, and **nobody in this empire has ever issued him anything of any kind at any time in his life.** He has been keeping it the way a person keeps a thing they are going to need, and the four seconds it took him to say that out loud were the entire distance between the two candidates and he had been living in them for about nine weeks. **A person with a sheet of clean paper in a drawer in a rented room in a city where a woman has come about nine miles twice and a man of about thirty-four has knocked on this door and not been let in is a person who is waiting to be asked something, and a person who is waiting to be asked something is a person with a document, and a document is the only thing anybody in this empire can be held to.** About four years is how long anything about him can be found for. He tears it up, sweeps it off the table, leaves it on the floor because picking it up would have been a thing he was doing with it, and finds his hands shaking for about four minutes, and has thought since that a man who decides a thing in about four seconds and then shakes for about four minutes has not decided it in about four seconds. **It is not tidiness: about four years is how long a piece of paper about him can be found for, and it is the only piece of paper in this city that is about him and does not say so, and he has thrown away a thing that could have been nothing, and that gets him nothing and gets them nothing.** A man of about thirty-four knocks at about the seventh hour, has come about four miles once before, and says the thing he has come to say, which is **what did the choice cost.** He answers the price of the day, in a flat voice, through about two inches of door, and then refuses the cost of the choice in the same breath: **I have said it once and I am not going to say it twice, and you did not hear it and I am not going to pretend to you that you did.** He quotes her, the woman of twenty who worked it out before he did, and says there is a woman of twenty and names no week. Asked whether he will go, he says no, with no weight at all, and then refuses to say why twice in a doorway, so that the man does not go up that road and tell somebody he has a reason for staying. **He has not got a reason for staying. He has got about four feet of wall and a nail with nothing on it and a decision he made in about four seconds, and a man who says a reason for staying out loud is a man who is about to be asked to justify it.** Then he says the last thing, to the floor: **a person who is paid to read is a person who can be told to stop**, which he has known since a man of thirty-eight in a rented room nine miles off a counter worked it out in about nine minutes at a window, and a foreman of fifty-one on a bank has worked it out standing in that bay since she was forty-two and has never been asked about it, and the two of them have never met and there is no instrument in this empire that would put them in a room, and a man who is a labour is a man somebody can pay for a day, and he has chosen to be that. And what does he do with knowing it: **nothing. A man who is not paid and cannot be instructed and cannot be stopped and cannot be thanked is the most dangerous person in this matter, and every person who has met me has worked that out in about four minutes, and I am not going to be able to help anybody.** And then, because a man who has torn up a sheet of paper nine minutes earlier is in a condition in which he says true things, four sentences about four other people in four other places, and a fifth about the bread woman, and **that is the only arrangement in this matter that anybody has ever got right.**

## Chapter 247 — remainder of the entry

There are about four hundred bought and unfilled blanks in a box under her counter and there is no form anywhere in this empire that says she may keep them. **A form bought and not filled in is fourpence of proof that the question was asked, and that is the closest thing to a post in this empire and it is not a post, and there is no appointment and no clerk's name and no register and no heading over it, and about four hundred times a year somebody does it and nobody decided anything and nobody can be told to stop.** And the chapter's finding, said to the box and then to a colleague: **every other piece of paper in that building is a document and a document is the only thing anybody in this empire can be held to, and a blank is not a document, because a blank has nothing on it and a thing with nothing on it cannot be produced against a person**, and that is worth about nine hundred blanks' worth of nothing. The fee board carries four things and none of the four is time: a copy is fourpence; a question of hers at her own table is a shilling and she has not done one of her own in about a year; a certificate is four shillings and she has never issued one and no form requires her to; and the fourth is a line of writing she has never worked out what it is for, and she has asked two clerks about it and both said they did not know either, and one of those two clerks is Tamsin Rook. **Tamsin Rook, twenty-four, two years at that counter, has two boxes on a page ruled by hand in a book of her own with the same name in both, and has not ruled a third and is not going to, and has never once been asked a question by anybody who was not a person who wanted something out of her**, and there has been one person coming up those stairs for about a year who has never asked her anything, and she has worked out that he is not shy, because a shy man does not come nine times. **And the chapter's first scene is a woman of about forty who comes for a copy, pays fourpence, and then asks about the name and not about the paper**, having been told three times by kind people and not once told anything, and Marn Ottery answers the reason at last and gives it to a person instead of to a room: **a name in a column is a thing a person can be served at, and that is the reason and it is the whole of the reason, and I am not going to soften it and I am not going to charge you for it, and I have said it to about four hundred people at this counter and not one of them has ever said thank you for it, because it is not a kindness and it does not read like one.** And she puts the coin back on the counter rather than taking it, which is not a thing a clerk does. Then the chapter's decision: **she asks Tamsin Rook one question, and she says the shape of it out loud first, and the shape is that it is not a question about a word on a form, because a board is not a form and a line of writing on a board is not a word on a form, and she has thought about that for about four days and is going to ask it anyway and wants it noticed that she thought about it, because she has watched about nine people in this city make a thing out of a habit in her time at that counter and every one of them ended up with a heading over it.** The question is what the fourth thing on that board is for, and the answer is *I do not know*, and Marn Ottery says that is what she told her before and that she has never asked anybody anything in eleven years and that is not a complaint, it is a fact about her. And Tamsin Rook says: **you have just asked me a question, and nobody has asked me one since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, and you did it in eleven words and you did not charge me a shilling for it, and I have been waiting for somebody to do that every working day since about the seventh month of next year and there is not one form in this empire for a person to be asked**, and then, flatly: **a person who is not asked has not been given a duty, and I have known that since about the seventh month of next year, and it has never once applied to me, and I did not know that until about four minutes ago.** And then Marn Ottery says the half she has been keeping for eleven years, and asks whether the two of them are going to make a practice of it, and Tamsin Rook says no, because a thing that has happened once is not a practice. Then a man of about forty for a copy at fourpence read back twice; a woman of about thirty told no about a copy that would stand if the paper were lost; a woman of about sixty-three who walks past the counter without stopping four days running, and neither clerk goes after her; and a boy of about fifteen who is told he can buy as many blanks as he likes and that his master is wrong, and buys four. The close: **there are four people in this empire who have asked a person out loud what a word on a form means, the third was the first anybody did on purpose, about nine hundred and forty people have been made answerable by a court that sat in nineteen years, and there is no post anywhere in this empire whose job is to ask, and a box under a counter with about four hundred blanks in it is the nearest thing to one that anybody has ever built, and nobody appointed it and nobody is going to.**

## Chapter 248 — remainder of the entry

He has walked about four miles to that bay three times and about four miles to a rented room in another city, and neither walk has been about work, and he has told nobody at the place he works, and not the two men he asked about the hold, and not the foreman, and not the man of about fifty-five with a chain, and there are about four people in this matter he has told nothing at all, and one of them is a woman of twenty-four at a counter about nine miles from where he lives who said the price out loud twice in one afternoon. **And the price she said was the price of being a party, and he is not in this, and she is four hundred and thirty miles from where he lives and is not going to be sent and nobody is going to ask her, and he has thought about that for about six weeks and has not worked out whether it is a comfort or a theft.** He had worked out for about six weeks that he was not going to tell her, on a good reason: a person who knows a thing can be served with a paper about it, being served is what makes a person a party, a party is the only person who can be told what a thing is going to be used for, and a person who can be told what a thing is going to be used for cannot take it back, and he had never been able to get past the fourth part of that. Then he tells her, and she has noticed something for six weeks. He gives her the four of them and where he got them. She says: **then you have come about four miles twice and a third time to a bay to find out how to ask a question about a word on a form, and you are going to do it, and nobody sent you.** He says nobody sent him and that he is the only one anybody could send, which is exactly why nobody is going to, including him. She asks whether he wants to be sent and he says he wants to be asked. **And then she asks the first question anybody has asked her in nine years, which is a plain question and not about a word on a form: *do you want to be sent.* "You are going to go and ask a foreman a question about a word on a form, out loud, in a room, and there is no post in this empire whose job is to ask, and nobody is going to send you, and if it goes wrong the person it goes wrong for is her and not you, and you have worked that out, and I have not."** Then the cost of telling her, which he gives away rather than keeping: **telling you has just made a second person in this house findable by a thing I know, and I did it on purpose, and I could not have done it without asking you first, and I did not ask you first, and I am not going to pretend to you that I did it kindly**, because if he had asked she would have said no or said yes and been frightened, and both would have been him arranging the room, and there is no form in this empire for asking a person whether they will mind being told something. **So I have done the same thing to you that a foreman of fifty-one did in a bay in the ninth month of the year after next, on purpose, without asking, and it cost her nothing to tell nine men and it is going to cost you whatever it costs.** He refuses to tell her what it costs, and she says that is the cruellest thing he has said, and he says he has thought about it for six weeks and is going with it. **Then she says she is not going to ask him about it, not now and not after, and that if somebody comes to the door she will say she does not know what he is talking about, and that she is not going to thank him for telling her and he is not going to thank her for hearing it, and there is no form in this empire for either of those and there is never going to be one**, and the eldest one, who is nine, understood none of it and has since told a person at school that his father was going to ask somebody something, and was not corrected, **and that is the only account of this evening that exists anywhere in the world.** Then, because a kitchen is a room and a room gets a day, the two halves he will not say. He will not tell her which word it is, and the reason is that **in about four years somebody is going to read that a man of about thirty-four asked a question about a word on a form and want to know which word, and if it is in my wife's mouth then it is in two mouths, and two mouths is findable in a way one mouth is not, and the only person in this house who is not findable is the one who does not know.** And the last of it: **nobody is going to know that I did it. Not a clerk and not a foreman and not a book-keeper, and nobody is going to write it down, and in about four years it will not be in any document anywhere, and the only record of it will be that a foreman of fifty-one in a bay said something out loud that she had said in a shed in the fifth month of the year after next, and that is all I am buying.** She asks *are you frightened*, and he says yes, and *of what*, and he says he does not know, and gives both names and says neither is right: one is that he will open his mouth in a bay and find that there is nothing in it, because there is nothing in it, because a question about a word on a form is a question about a piece of paper and the paper is not in the room; **and the other one is that it will work, that somebody will answer me and I will feel the answer in the room and I will want it again, and that is the exact shape of the thing a man of about fifty-five with a chain has been left alone to avoid for nine years.** Close: a woman of twenty with sixteen books and a held wage, a foreman who has said in a bay that she is in a box and nobody has said a word, a clerk of about fifty-five who has entered a question in a minute book with her own name at the foot of it, and a mark in chalk on a wall that means nothing and is nobody's and is not going to be wiped.

## Chapter 249 — remainder of the entry

He is one of about four people in this matter who cannot be asked, and nobody told him so except a woman of twenty in a room two streets off a street that goes down to the river road, on a stair, in about four seconds, in the second day of the second week of the fourth month of the year after next, and he did not notice for most of a working life after that. **He is holding a wage-day notice with a day on it and no name on it, in the inside pocket of the coat on the hook by the door, where it has been since the fourth day of the third week of the seventh month of the year after next, and it says a sum and it does not say against what, and he is not going to tell anybody, because about nine men have heard him say out loud that he cannot say what a wage is held against, and a document would let him say it without a word.** He says the volume's question, four times over about half an hour, in the ordinary way, one man at a time, in the middle of ordinary work, to about nine men: **if that office was meant to be temporary, then somebody changed it into an inheritable one, and I would like to know who, and I have asked it in a shed in a different city in the fifth month of the year after next and nobody answered, and I am asking it again in my own mouth and nobody is going to answer it here either.** Nobody answers. A man of about thirty says the office is a thing in a room and not a person and there was nobody to change. A man of about forty says he does not know what an office was meant to be in the first place, **which was the best of the answers and it is a true one.** A man of about twenty-six says the question is about four hundred and thirty miles away and not about a vat. And then he refuses to say it a fifth time to a man of about forty-four who has been in that end for eleven years, because a fifth time would be him enjoying it, and the man of about forty-four says he thought he had gone mad, has not slept properly since the first one, has told his wife nothing, and has worked out something else instead: **that is the whole of what a question costs in a place like this, and about four people in this town have found that out this year and two of them have stopped coming to work.** Then the foreman says why he has said it out loud at all: **a foreman of fifty-one said this exact question out loud in a shed in the fifth month of the year after next and about nine men heard it and she was a person nobody in this matter can ask anything and she has never been asked since and never going to be, and for about four years that question has been in exactly one mouth, and a thing in one mouth is a thing one person can be asked about, and a person who can be asked about a question is a person who can be told what to do with the answer, and there is no form anywhere in this empire that stops that.** And so: **I have said it in five, and you have all of them, and a man of about thirty-four in a kitchen in a city about four hundred and thirty miles down this river has a number on the back of his hand that has gone, and a foreman of fifty-one in a bay has a box she is in and nine men who know about it and have said nothing, and now there are five mouths and none of them can be stopped and none of them can be written down. A reason that has been read out loud in a bay is a worse kind of findable than a name, and a question that has been said out loud in five places is worse still, and I have just made this one worse on purpose, and I want it written down somewhere that I did it on purpose and I knew what it cost.** A man of about twenty-six, who is the only one of the nine who has worked out who is in the room, says that a man who cannot read a paragraph is the only person in this empire you can say anything to and have it not come back, and the foreman says yes and that you have just worked that out in about four seconds and he took about four years and that is the same four seconds for everybody and the four years is the part nobody can shorten. Then the unpaid work: **in the sixth year of a guild's blank list there was no list of works at all in this district, the whole trade ran for a season on what people were told at the gate, and he was the only person at that end who wrote anything down, in a book of his own, and he threw it out, and he has never regretted throwing it out and has thought about it about four times since the second day of the second week of the fourth month of the year after next**, and then the finding: **nobody in this empire pays a man to hold a list and every single person in this empire turns out to need one, and those two facts have never once been in the same room, and I have been in both of the rooms and I have been the one holding the list in each of them, and I am about forty and I have done it for nothing twice.** A man of about thirty asks him about the notice in the coat and he refuses a reason, because a reason is a thing a man can be asked to give again, and a woman of twenty told him in a rented room in the second week of the third month of the year after next to write it, keep it and not send it, and he has not sent it, and she made that reason up in about four seconds and told him it was not a good one, and **the day I want it to exist I will send it, and on that day it stops being a thing I have thought and starts being a thing that has happened, and I will not be able to take it back, and that is the only reason anybody in this matter has ever had for writing one down.** Then the chapter's own addition, which is the reason he has not been thanked for any of it: **a man of about thirty-four in a city about four hundred and thirty miles down this river is going to ask a foreman a question about a word on a form in a bay in about two weeks, out loud, in a room with about nine men in it, and he decided on his own and nobody sent him, and I am not going to be the man who goes up that road and stands in that bay, and nobody is going to send me, and if I stand in that bay on that day then whatever he has done stops being a thing a person did on purpose and becomes a thing two people did, and that is the one thing I am not going to do.** And he leaves the other half of the question alone, on purpose, because a man who has been asked nothing for forty years does not get to choose the shape of the one question he is going to spend, and a question asked in halves by a man nobody can ask is a question with his fingerprints on it, and the only person who will ever know he knew there was one is a man of about twenty-six. And then a boy of about nine with a slate stands in the doorway at about the seventh hour and comes four steps into a dye end, which is further than a child of nine comes into one, and says **I have written down that somebody changed it, and I have not written down who, and I have written down the day I was told it, and that is the day you said it and not the day it happened, and I have not put a name on it because you did not say a name.** Nobody in that end says anything at all. And the foreman says: **that is the first time in four years that anybody has written any of it down anywhere, and it is on a slate, and neither of us has a slate, and I am not going to take it off him, and there is not one form in this empire that will take it off him, and if there were one I would not fill it in, and that is the first thing I have said today that is about me.** Close: the river is still running, a second season shut and a third open and nobody agreed to either, the guarantee, the Lowcross bill, the nineteen behind the bank, a woman of twenty with sixteen books who has decided to go and tell four women that she is not paid and is not going up the river this year and has not said she is never going to, and a foreman of about forty with a notice in a coat, and the largest thing anybody has said out loud this year now in five mouths and in no book at all.

## Chapter 250 — remainder of the entry

Nine men, a foreman of fifty-one, a girl of seventeen at the back on a written engagement, a man of about thirty from the second gang, a man of about forty from the third gang, a man of about fifty-five with a chain, a man of about twenty-eight, a man of about thirty-four who has come about four miles, and a book-keeper of thirty-eight in the doorway, which is a thing he has done once in two years and which no foreman on that bank has ever done. **She prints the count out loud before anything else happens, because she is a foreman and a foreman reads: four in about a hundred and forty years, with a date against each and nobody sent her for the third, and the two first ones called accidents and the third named as the first one anybody did on purpose, and the count has not moved since the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next.** He asks whether anybody in that bay has ever done one, and she says nobody in that bay has ever done one and nobody sent anybody, and she wants him to understand that in front of nine men before he does anything, **because a person who does a thing in there and can be told afterwards that it was arranged is going to wish he had not done it.** He says he understands it and that he is not going to be able to say afterwards that anybody made him do it, and that he has thought about that for about six weeks in a kitchen and about four in a yard. She says say what you came to say and say it quickly, because there are nine men in here and the lamp is not lit. And he does not say the question first. **He turns round and looks at the back of the bay and gives the reader of seventeen a fourth reason, and it is a new one and it is his: she would say yes.** It took him ten days. She is seventeen and she is on a written engagement at the rate the list is set at and she has not been thanked, and if a foreman asks her a question like that in front of nine men then she cannot say no, not because anybody has stopped her, but because there is nothing in this empire she can do about being asked in a room. And in about four years there will be a person who can say that a girl of seventeen stood in a bay on this cut and read a hold out loud, and there is no form anywhere that requires a person to say it and no form anywhere that says she may refuse to have said it. He says it is not from the foreman, not from the book-keeper and not from the man with the chain, and that all three of them have given a reason and none of them is this one. **The foreman answers: a man of about fifty-five with a chain gave a better reason in a yard in the third week of the sixth month of the year after next and gave it two months before he worked his out, and it was better than mine and I have said so in a bay and I am saying so again. And it is not better, and I have had two months to be sure of that, and I do not know what better is and I am not going to have it. Three reasons have been given to me and I have not acted on one of them and I am not going to act on a fourth, and you have not given me a reason. You have given me a fact about a person of seventeen.** He says he is not asking her, has not asked her, is not going to, nobody is going to send anybody to her, and there is not a form in this empire for sending anybody to her. She says that is what makes it a fact and not a reason, and then: **say your question.** And he says it, out loud, in a bay, in about four seconds: **Foreman. What does the word *representative* mean?** Nobody in that bay makes a sound. And the foreman answers it, in a foreman's voice, in about four sentences and then about four minutes, and nobody in that bay had ever heard the second four minutes of anything she said: **I have been in a box since the first week of the seventh month of the year after and I have never been asked a single thing about it, and I worked out about a minute and a half ago what it is for in the second week of the third month of the year after next, and I have not told one person, and you are the first, and I am going to say it out loud in a bay because I said in the second week of the ninth month of the year after next that I would.** And the answer: **the form was not asking me whether I would represent anybody. It was asking who I represent, and there is a column for the answer, and there is no column for a refusal. That is the whole of it. A person writes a thing in a column and the column says *and if not*, and there is no column that says *no*, and there is not one in this empire in any form I have ever seen, and that is why my no did not work when I said it out loud in a room with the day on it. Not because a clerk did anything wrong. Because there was nowhere to put it.** A man of about thirty says *about nine hundred of us* and she agrees, roughly, because that is what a class does, and gives the finding she has just been given: **a class is a heading, and a heading is how a person becomes a company, and a company is a thing this empire can hold wages against and write letters to and take to law, and a man who is a person is a thing it can only send papers to**, and says a clerk of about fifty-five took two days in eleven books in an office about nine miles off to work that out and has entered a question about it in a minute book with the day on it and her own name at the foot of it, on her own motion, because nobody asked her to, and **it is a question and not an answer and there is not one other thing in this empire anybody has done about it.** Then the part that is the reason the day happened, and it is the volume's promise closed in a mouth and not closed at all: **it has not got me out and it is not going to. There is not one form anywhere in this empire that will un-fill a box. Not in this room and not in the office nine miles off and not in any building either of us has ever been inside. The person who would administer one if there were one is a clerk of about fifty-five who has nineteen years of minutes about a thing she has never been in a room with, and she is not a villain, and she has done about nine hundred of them correctly, and she has never once been asked whether a box ought to have been filled.** And a man of about forty from the third gang says *the other one is still in one too*, and she says: **the other one is still in one too. She is about four hundred and thirty miles up this river in a room two streets off a street that goes down to the river road, with sixteen books on a table and a wage that has been held since the second week of the third month of the year after, and she said no out loud on the fourth day of the third week of the fifth month of the year after and the no did not work, and one of her books has her own name at the head of it, and she has decided to go and tell four women that she is not paid. Nobody in this bay has got her out. Nobody in this bay is going to get her out. I am in a box on this bank and I am going to be in a box when this season shuts, and I have known that since the seventh month of the year after.** Then the cost, given away before anybody asks: **a person who has asked a question in a room is a party from that day on, and a party is the only person who can be told what a thing is going to be used for. I have a trade and a house with a door on it and a wife and three of them at the table, and I told her on purpose in a kitchen without asking her first, and there is no form in this empire for asking a person whether they will mind being told something, and from today I am a person who can be served with a paper about what a foreman said in this bay. And there is no fund behind any of it, and I am not going to be able to say afterwards that anybody made me do it, and that is what I bought, and I bought it with about six weeks of a man's life and a number on the back of my own hand that is gone.** And nobody thanked him, and he had not expected it, and a reader of seventeen at the back of that bay has not been thanked for anything in her life and was not thanked now and is not going to start, and **nobody sent anybody to her, and nobody is going to, and she was not asked, and she is not going to be asked.** Then the foreman says the last of it, in the ordinary voice: **about four people in this bay cannot read a paragraph and have never been asked what the hold is, and I am not asking them, and nobody is going to ask them, and there is a finding in a ledger about four hundred yards up that road in a book-keeper's own hand with the day on it that says the four are not to be told this season, and it is not mine to improve and I am not going to improve it, and what you have just done in this bay has not changed one word of it and was never going to**, and he had said before he asked that it had not, and she noticed and is not going to thank him for noticing either. **And that is the price of the fifth, and you paid it on purpose, and I made it, because I said in a bay in the second week of the ninth month of the year after next that I am in a box, and a man walked about four miles because of it.** Then the ordinary things, one of them new: a man of about twenty-eight and a list on two cuts; a man of about twenty asking whether the season had been agreed to, and her answer that it had not been agreed to and had not been refused either, and that a third thing had happened, and that a third thing is what a season is; and the chain at the third link. Then his decision, in the doorway, to a book-keeper who stood in it for the whole of it and said nothing: **I am not going to do that again. Not because it is wrong and not because of the four in here. Because a person who does a thing twice has made a practice, and a practice grows a heading over it on its own without anybody deciding anything, and a heading is how a person becomes a company, and a foreman told me ten minutes ago that a company is a thing this empire can take to law. I have had one question in my life that I asked on purpose and I am going to go on being a man on a list of works four times a season with a fair hand on somebody else's page, and that is the whole of what I have bought and I am not going to buy any more of it.** Close: a man of about thirty-eight stood in a doorway and did not say anything and did not write it down. **Four askings in about a hundred and forty years, and a fifth one in a bay on the fourth day of the fourth week of the eleventh month of the year after next, and the count is five, and the third of the first four was the first one anybody did on purpose and the fifth was the second, and there is still no post anywhere in this empire whose job is to ask, and nobody is appointed, and nothing has been written down, and the four who cannot read a paragraph have still never been asked one question, and the two who are in their boxes are still in their boxes, and the guarantee is still standing offered and unanswered on about four hundred and forty foot of that bank with about sixty children under sixteen inside it, and the bill at Lowcross is still nineteen pounds three and fourpence and unpaid, and about nineteen people are still behind the bank, and a reader of seventeen was not thanked and is not going to start. And the largest thing anybody has said out loud this year is in five mouths and in no book at all, and it is not going to be answered by anybody who is standing on that bank, and the river is still running, and the lamp is not lit.**

## Notes on these ten summaries

**The word counts in the headings are measured per file with `len(text.split())` after every edit was finished, and every one of the ten files returns the identical figure under `sed 's/[[:space:]]*$//' file | wc -w`; the volume total is the sum of the fifty per-file figures. The batch total is 28,573 and the ten of them are 2,744 / 2,796 / 2,861 / 2,782 / 2,720 / 2,790 / 2,756 / 2,800 / 3,105 / 3,219, ten of ten at or above the 2,700 floor, and Chapter 0250 at 3,219 is nineteen words over the 3,200 top of the band and was not trimmed to reach a number. Volume 05 stands at 144,030 across fifty chapters and the manuscript at 855,104 across two hundred and fifty.** Where a summary and the prose disagree, the prose is the authority.

**Five conventions this batch used, recorded so that a reader of these summaries does not misread an absence.** **One:** the batch uses the strict quotation form, a speech turn being one paragraph that opens and closes, and the strict-form test returns zero. **Two:** there are thirty-four question marks across the ten files, per file 5 / 5 / 1 / 2 / 1 / 6 / 3 / 6 / 3 / 2, all in a bare short line in the mouth of a person, and a reader should not read the absence of one in a quotation as evidence that the line is not a question. **Three:** the count of the askings moved once, from four to five, in Chapter 0250, and the four of the first four are recited in Chapter 0241 and Chapter 0250 and printed short in four others. **Four:** a man of about thirty-four appears in six chapters of this batch and is one person throughout, and the man of about thirty-four who came four miles in the rain to a dye end in Chapter 0237 is a different figure and is not in this batch. **Five:** the reader of seventeen is on the page once, in Chapter 0250, and the two sheets in Chapter 0245 are Halla Wray's and only one of them is in her hand.

# CHAPTER SUMMARIES CORRECTED BY THE REVIEW REPAIR ON BATCH 0005 (appended; nothing above this line is rewritten)

**Word counts in every heading above are the pre-repair figures. The post-repair figures are below, and the files are the authority.** Post-repair, per chapter: **241 2,787 / 242 2,804 / 243 2,863 / 244 2,723 / 245 2,773 / 246 2,861 / 247 2,821 / 248 2,800 / 249 3,106 / 250 3,253. The batch is 28,791 words** against 28,573 before the repair, so the repair added 218.

**Five summaries need a correction, and a reader of the summaries above must take these over them.**

- **Chapter 241.** The summary's account of the yard holds in full: the four are given out loud, in mouths, in the wrong order on purpose, and the number goes on the back of a hand. **What changed:** the man with the chain no longer says he is the fourth person talked to and about the fifth to have said the number, and he no longer counts himself among the four. He says he is not one of the four, that he has not been asked one thing in nine years, and that writing him down as one of them would be a way for a list of four to go wrong. **The fifth person is now only the man of about thirty-four.** The bay's two ordinary men are now a man of about thirty who asks whether a man can be struck off a list for being drunk and what happens to his rate, and a man of about forty who asks whether a name written down at a gate is the same name as a name put on a list. The clause about a foreman doing a perfectly ordinary thing stands in this chapter as its own shorter sentence.

- **Chapter 244.** The summary's account holds: sixteen books, one of them with her own name at the head, a wage held since the second week of the third month of the year after, a decision to go and tell four women, one told, a refusal of money. **What changed:** the week in which she decides is the fourth week of the ninth month of the **year after next**, and the chapter had said the year after in one line. **The close is also cut back.** It was a nine-item list — nineteen behind a bank, four hundred and forty foot, sixty children, a reader of seventeen, Lowcross, a book-keeper, a foreman in a box, a woman in a box, a foreman with a paper in a coat — and it is now three sentences about a book-keeper who is not on the list of works, a foreman with a paper in a coat, and a bill at Lowcross that nobody in the room is going to be the reason for. The words *sixteen books* fall from eight in the chapter to five.

- **Chapter 245.** The summary's account holds: the ninth bundle, a sheet with a day on it and no name, the fold read at the bottom, the three pieces of paper on a bench, and a foreman who is the only person alive who could give evidence about the crease. **What changed:** the ordinary-things paragraph no longer reuses the third link of a chain or the clause about a perfectly ordinary thing. It is now a man of about fifty asking whether being late twice in a week loses your place on the list and whether that is the same as losing your rate, and then a man of about thirty off the wet asking whether a bundle may stand on a sill overnight, which takes about nine minutes and which not one of the nine men could have described afterwards. **The close is also cut back** from a five-item restatement of the guarantee, the nineteen, the four and the reader of seventeen to the three pieces of paper, the fold, and the fact that a bank of four hundred and forty foot is still standing offered and unanswered.

- **Chapter 246.** The summary's account holds: the rented room, the nail, the sheet of clean paper, the tearing up, the shaking hands, the cost of the choice refused, the man of about thirty-four on the landing, and the sheet posted to a building that cannot answer it. **What changed, and none of it is a decision:** the paragraph that mixed him with a first person is now wholly third person. Three questions he puts to a wall and a floor are now attributed and given the room's own answer back, and the reply that had been agreeing with his own sentence now names the arrangement instead. The three added question paragraphs carry no emphasis, because this batch does not emphasise a short question in a mouth. **He has not changed his mind about anything and the second half of the reason for staying is still not said.**

- **Chapter 247.** **This summary needs the most correction and a reader must take the version below.** Tamsin Rook's visitor came **in the last week of the ninth month of the year after**, and he is **not** the third of the four. The chapter had placed him at a counter on the second day of the first week of the first month of the year after next, which is the day the count of the askings reached four, and had called him the third, which is the woman of twenty at the desk in the second week of the eleventh month. **That was a contradiction of the batch canon on date, ordinal and agent at once, and it is repaired here and in no other chapter.** She said the price at him twice because he had not caught the first one. She has not asked him anything since the first week he came; he has come about nine times in about a year, has not come back, and she is not going to make him. **She says out loud that she does not know whether it counted, that she has got no book that says, and that there is nobody she could ask without asking a person to look themselves up.** A second contradiction in the same chapter, which the review did not catch and which the first repair exposed, had the narration saying he had never asked her anything while a clerk said he had; the narration now says he asked her one thing in the first week he came and has not asked her anything since. Marn Ottery's question about the fourth line on the fee board, the box of about four hundred blanks, the six clerks at the counter, the woman of about forty upstairs and her father's name, the man of about thirty-four and his copy, the boy of fifteen and his four blanks, and the closing roll-call of four are all unchanged.

**Three chapters need no correction to their substance: 242, 248, 249 and 250 stand as summarised above, with three exceptions worth naming.** In Chapter 248 a sentence that opened with a lower-case word inside an emphasis run now opens with a capital, and nothing else in the chapter moved. In Chapter 249 the phrase *this year* is gone from four places and the *about nine men* who heard the question in a shed is now *every man in that shed heard it*, and nothing else moved. In Chapter 250 the volume's last ordinary-things beat is rewritten around two ordinary things that are not elsewhere in the batch, keeps the third link of a chain, and now ends on the man going out into the yard and doing it with his own hands and telling nobody; the man of about twenty-eight and the two-lists question are no longer in it. **Chapter 250's inventory close is untouched, because it is the last page of the volume and the one place the full roll-call belongs.**

## Volume 06, Batch 0001, Chapters 251 to 260 — ten summaries and nothing else
